{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma_{0}};\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma_{0}};i^{*}\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0\ .}

By taking
G
-invariants, we obtain an exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma};\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma};\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma_{0}};i^{*}\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}})^{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0\ .}

Recall that
S=\Gamma w_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\Gamma w_{r}
and note that

H^{0}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma_{0}};i^{*}\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}})^{G}\cong\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathrm{an}}(w_{i})^{\Gamma_{w_{i}}}\cong\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}(\Sym^{k}H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{E}_{w_{i}}))^{\Gamma_{w_{i}}}\ ,

where
\Gamma_{w_{i}}
is the stabiliser of
\Gamma
at
w_{i}
, so we can rewrite the residue sequence as

(5.7)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma};\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma};\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}(\Sym^{k}H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{E}_{w_{i}}))^{\Gamma_{w_{i}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0\ .}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res}

Remark 5.9
.

One can use this exact sequence to deduce dimension formulae for the space of meromorphic (quasi)modular forms with prescribed pole orders, such as in
[
Mat22
, Theorem 1.4]
.

Recall from Proposition
5.5
that a cohomology class
\alpha
in
H^{1}(\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma};\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}})
can be represented by a meromorphic modular form
f
. The residue of
\alpha
is computed explicitly in terms of the principal part of
f
at the points of
S
as follows.

Lemma 5.10
.

Let
f\in M_{k+2,\Gamma}^{!}(*S)
and, for every
i=1,\ldots,r
, denote by
f(z)=\sum_{n\gg 0}c_{n}(w_{i})(z-w_{i})^{n}
its Laurent expansion at
w_{i}
. Then

\Res([f(z)(X-zY)^{k}dz])=\Bigg(\sum_{j=0}^{k}\binom{k}{j}c_{-j-1}(w_{i})(X-w_{i}Y)^{k-j}(-Y)^{j}\Bigg)_{i=1,\ldots,r}\ .

Proof.

The residue in cohomology is induced by the residue of vector-valued functions (see §
A.2
), which we compute in the Betti frame. By writing

X-zY=(X-w_{i}Y)+(z-w_{i})(-Y)

and applying the binomial formula, we obtain

f(z)(X-zY)^{k}=\Bigg(\sum_{n\gg-\infty}c_{n}(w_{i})(z-w_{i})^{n}\Bigg)\Bigg(\sum_{j=0}^{k}\binom{k}{j}(X-w_{i}Y)^{k-j}(-Y)^{j}(z-w_{i})^{j}\Bigg),

which gives

\displaystyle\Res_{z=w_{i}}f(z)(X-zY)^{k}dz

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j+n=-1\\
n\gg-\infty,\ 0\leq j\leq k\end{subarray}}\binom{k}{j}c_{n}(w_{i})(X-w_{i}Y)^{k-j}(-Y)^{j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{k}\binom{k}{j}c_{-j-1}(w_{i})(X-w_{i}Y)^{k-j}(-Y)^{j}.\qed

Given
n\geq 0
, we denote by
M_{k+2,\Gamma}(nS)
the
\mathbb{C}
-subspace of
M_{k+2,\Gamma}^{!}(*S)
defined by meromorphic modular forms which are holomorphic at the cusps and have poles of order at most
n
at every point of
S
. When
n=0
, we denote it simply by
M_{k+2,\Gamma}
; it is the space of holomorphic modular forms of weight
k+2
for
\Gamma
.

Lemma 5.11
.

If
n\leq k+1
, then the natural map

(5.8)

M_{k+2,\Gamma}(nS)\longrightarrow M_{k+2,\Gamma}^{!}(*S)/\textstyle{\frac{\partial^{k+1}}{\partial z^{k+1}}M_{-k,\Gamma}^{!}(*S)}\cong H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma};\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}})

is injective.

Proof.

A modular form
f\in M_{k+2,\Gamma}^{!}(nS)
in the kernel of (
5.8
) is of the form
f=\partial^{k+1}h/\partial z^{k+1}
for some
h\in M_{-k,\Gamma}^{!}(*S)
. Since
f
is holomorphic at infinity, so is
h
. Moreover, if
h
had a pole along
S
, then
f
would have a pole of order at least
k+2
along
S
, which would contradict the hypothesis
n\leq k+1
. This shows that
h
is holomorphic everywhere. Since there are no non-constant holomorphic modular forms for
\Gamma
of weight
-k\leq 0
, we conclude that
f=0
.
∎

It follows from the above lemma that, for
n\leq k+1
, we may always identify
M_{k+2,\Gamma}(nS)
as a subspace of
H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma};\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}})
. By Lemma
5.10
, for
p=1,\ldots,k+1
and
f\in M_{k+2,\Gamma}((k+2-p)S)
, we have

\Res([f(z)(X-zY)^{k}dz])\ \in\ \bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}F^{p-1}(\Sym^{k}H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{E}_{w_{i}}))^{\Gamma_{w_{i}}}\ ,

since
-j-1\geq-(k+2-p)
implies that
k-j\geq p-1
. Here,
F^{p-1}(\Sym^{k}H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{E}_{w_{i}}))^{\Gamma_{w_{i}}}
is the
(p-1)
th step of the Hodge filtration, given explicitly by
\Gamma_{w_{i}}
-invariant degree
k
homogeneous polynomials in
X-w_{i}Y
and
-Y
which are divisible by
(X-w_{i}Y)^{p-1}
.

Proposition 5.12
.

For every
p=1,\ldots,k+1
, the residue induces an exact sequence of
\mathbb{C}
-vector spaces

(5.9)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{k+2,\Gamma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{k+2,\Gamma}((k+2-p)S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}F^{p-1}(\Sym^{k}H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{E}_{w_{i}}))^{\Gamma_{w_{i}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0\ .}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res}

Proof.

The above sequence is well-defined and exact in the middle by Lemma
5.10
. It is trivially exact on the left, and we are left to show that the residue map is surjective. Using the
\mathbb{C}
-basis
X-w_{i}Y
,
X-\overline{w}_{i}Y
of
H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{E}_{w_{i}})
, and the notation (
3.14
), we see that
F^{p-1}(\Sym^{k}H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{E}_{w_{i}}))^{\Gamma_{w_{i}}}
is generated by

\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{w_{i}}}(X-w_{i}Y)^{m}(X-\overline{w}_{i}Y)^{n}\Big|_{\gamma}=\kappa_{\Gamma,w_{i}}^{m,n}(X-w_{i}Y)^{m}(X-\overline{w}_{i}Y)^{n}\text{, }\qquad m+n=k\text{, }m\geq p-1\text{, }n\geq 0.

Now, for every
i=1,\ldots,r
and
j=0,\ldots,k+1-p
, it follows from Proposition
3.3
that
\Psi_{\Gamma}^{j,k-j}(z,w_{i})\in M_{k,\Gamma}((k+2-p)S)
. We conclude by an application of the residue computation of Corollary
3.11
.
∎

We prove in the next paragraph that (
5.9
) is actually given by the Hodge filtration on
H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma};\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}})
.

5.5.
Hodge filtration and pole order

The exact sequence of
\mathbb{C}
-vector spaces (
5.7
) underlies an exact sequence of
\mathbb{Q}
-mixed Hodge structures (
cf.
Remark
A.7
):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma};\mathcal{V}_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma};\mathcal{V}_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}(\Sym^{k}H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{w_{i}}))^{\Gamma_{w_{i}}}(-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res}

Using the strictness of the Hodge filtration, we obtain for every
p\geq 1
an exact sequence of
\mathbb{C}
-vector spaces

(5.10)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{p}H_{\mathrm{dR}}^{1}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma};\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}}_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{p}H_{\mathrm{dR}}^{1}(\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma};\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}}_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}F^{p-1}(\Sym^{k}H_{\mathrm{dR}}^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{w_{i}}))^{\Gamma_{w_{i}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0\ .}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res}

We shall prove that the above sequence agrees with (
5.9
) for
p=1,\ldots,k+1
.

We recall the definition of the Hodge filtration on
H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma};\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}})
, as in §
A.4
. Let
\mathcal{X}_{\Gamma}
be the proper modular stack over
\mathbb{C}
associated to
\Gamma
. With the above notation, it is the quotient stack
\mathcal{X}_{\Gamma}=[G\backslash\mathcal{X}_{\Gamma_{0}}]
, where
\mathcal{X}_{\Gamma_{0}}
is the smooth projective curve over
\mathbb{C}
whose analytification is the usual (Satake) compactification of a modular curve
\mathcal{X}_{\Gamma_{0}}^{\mathrm{an}}\cong\Gamma_{0}\backslash(\mathfrak{H}\cup\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{Q}))
. Note that we have a chain of open immersions

\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{X}_{\Gamma},

where
\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}\setminus\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma}
is given by a closed substack
\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma}
corresponding to the orbits
\Gamma w_{1},\ldots,\Gamma w_{r}
, and
\mathcal{X}_{\Gamma}\setminus\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}
is given by a closed substack
\mathcal{Z}_{\Gamma}
corresponding to the cusps
\Gamma\backslash\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{Q})
. Formally, the closed substacks
\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{\Gamma}
are given by finite unions of residue gerbes of the corresponding points of
\mathcal{X}_{\Gamma}
(
cf.
the discussion in §
10.2
). In what follows, to simplify the exposition, we work directly with
\mathcal{X}_{\Gamma}
, but the reader can always take this to mean that we work
G
-equivariantly on
\mathcal{X}_{\Gamma_{0}}
.

The vector bundle with connection
(\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}},\nabla_{k}^{\mathbb{C}})
on
\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}
extends canonically
[
Del70
]
to a vector bundle
\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}}
over
\mathcal{X}_{\Gamma}
with logarithmic connection

\overline{\nabla}_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}:\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}}\longrightarrow\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}}\otimes\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{X}_{\Gamma}/\mathbb{C}}(\log\mathcal{Z}_{\Gamma}).

The Hodge filtration on
\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}}
also extends canonically to a Hodge filtration
F^{p}\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}}
satisfying Griffiths’ transversality condition:

\overline{\nabla}_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(F^{p}\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}})\subset F^{p-1}\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}}\otimes\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{X}_{\Gamma}/\mathbb{C}}(\log\mathcal{Z}_{\Gamma})\ .

Explicitly, using the isomorphism
\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}}\cong\Sym^{k}\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{1}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}}
, the Hodge filtration on
\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}}
is induced by the Hodge filtration on
\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{1}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}}
, which is given by

F^{1}\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{1}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}}=\overline{p}_{*}\Omega^{1}_{\overline{\mathcal{E}}/\mathcal{X}_{\Gamma}}(\log\overline{p}^{-1}\mathcal{Z}_{\Gamma})\ ,

where
\overline{p}:\overline{\mathcal{E}}\to\mathcal{X}_{\Gamma}
is the ‘universal generalised elliptic curve’ over
\mathcal{X}_{\Gamma}
.

Remark 5.13
.

Since
dz=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\frac{dq}{q}
, if
q=e^{2\pi iz}
, and since
X-zY
canonically extends to a section of
F^{1}\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{1}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}}
on a neighbourhood of the cuspidal locus
\mathcal{Z}_{\Gamma}
, a meromorphic modular form
f
which is holomorphic at the cusps defines a vector-valued 1-form
f(z)(X-zY)^{k}dz
on
\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma}
with logarithmic singularities along
\mathcal{Z}_{\Gamma}
. This yields, for every
m\geq 0
, an isomorphism of
\mathbb{C}
-vector spaces

(5.11)

M_{k+2,\Gamma}(mS)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{0}(\mathcal{X}_{\Gamma},F^{k}\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}_{\Gamma}}(m\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma})\otimes\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{X}_{\Gamma}/\mathbb{C}}(\log\mathcal{Z}_{\Gamma})))\ \text{, }\quad f\longmapsto f(z)(X-zY)^{k}dz\ .

The Hodge filtration on
\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}}
induces a filtration on the complex
\overline{C}\coloneqq\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{X}_{\Gamma}/\mathbb{C}}(\log(\mathcal{Z}_{\Gamma}\cup\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma});\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}})
:

F^{p}\overline{C}:\qquad F^{p}\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{\nabla}_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}F^{p-1}\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}}\otimes\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{X}_{\Gamma}/\mathbb{C}}(\log(\mathcal{Z}_{\Gamma}\cup\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma}))

and the Hodge filtration on de Rham cohomology is defined by

F^{p}H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma};\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}})\coloneqq\im(\mathbb{H}^{1}(\mathcal{X}_{\Gamma};F^{p}\overline{C})\longrightarrow\mathbb{H}^{1}(\mathcal{X}_{\Gamma};\overline{C}))

under the identification
H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma};\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}})\cong\mathbb{H}^{1}(\mathcal{X}_{\Gamma};\overline{C})
(see Lemma
A.6
).

Lemma 5.14
.

Let
S=\emptyset
. Then for every
p=1,\ldots,k+1
, we have isomorphisms:

F^{p}H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma};\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}})\cong H^{0}(\mathcal{X}_{\Gamma},F^{k}\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}}\otimes\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{X}_{\Gamma}/\mathbb{C}}(\log\mathcal{Z}_{\Gamma}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:holomorphic-at-cusps}}}{{\cong}}M_{k+2,\Gamma}.

The above result is classical, see
[
Zuc79
, §12]
or
[
Fon20
, §6]
and references therein.

Theorem 5.15
.

Under the isomorphism
H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma};\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}})\cong M_{k+2,\Gamma}^{!}(*S)/\frac{\partial^{k+1}}{\partial z^{k+1}}M_{-k,\Gamma}^{!}(*S)
of Proposition
5.5
, the Hodge filtration is given by

\displaystyle F^{0}H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma};\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}})\cong M_{k+2,\Gamma}^{!}(*S)/\textstyle{\frac{\partial^{k+1}}{\partial z^{k+1}}}M_{-k,\Gamma}^{!}(*S)

\displaystyle F^{p}H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma};\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}})\cong M_{k+2,\Gamma}((k+2-p)S)\text{, }\qquad p=1,\ldots,k+1

\displaystyle F^{k+2}H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma};\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}})=0.

Proof.

Let
p=1,\ldots,k+1
. By the exact sequences (
5.10
) and (
5.9
), it suffices to prove one inclusion: if
f\in M_{k+2,\Gamma}((k+2-p)S)
, then its class in cohomology
\alpha\coloneqq[f(z)(X-zY)^{k}dz]
lies in
F^{p}H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma};\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}})
. Again by the same exact sequences, there is some
\beta\in F^{p}H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma};\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}})
such that
\Res(\alpha)=\Res(\beta)
, which means that
\alpha-\beta
lies in
H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma};\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}})
. Since both
\alpha
and
\beta
are locally represented by vector-valued 1-forms with logarithmic singularities at the cuspidal locus
\mathcal{Z}_{\Gamma}
, the same holds for
\alpha-\beta
, which implies that
\alpha-\beta\in F^{p}H_{\mathrm{dR}}^{1}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma};\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}})
by Lemma
5.14
. Thus:

\alpha\ \in\ \beta+F^{p}H_{\mathrm{dR}}^{1}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma};\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}})\subset F^{p}H_{\mathrm{dR}}^{1}(\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma};\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}})\ .\qed

For simplicity, we state the next corollary only in the case
S=\Gamma w
.

Corollary 5.16
.

Let
F^{\bullet}
be the Hodge filtration on
H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma};\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}})
. The meromorphic modular forms
\Psi^{m,n}_{\Gamma}(z,w)
, with
m+n=k
and
m,n\geq 0
, satisfy

(5.12)

[\Psi_{\Gamma}^{m,n}(z,w)(X-zY)^{k}dz]\ \in\ F^{n+1}\cap\overline{F}^{m+1}

and induce a splitting of both the residue sequence (
5.7
):

\displaystyle(\Sym^{k}H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{E}_{w}))^{\Gamma_{w}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma};\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}})

\displaystyle\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{w}}(X-wY)^{n}(X-\overline{w}Y)^{m}\Big|_{\gamma}

\displaystyle\longmapsto(-1)^{m}(w-\overline{w})^{k}\binom{k}{m}^{-1}[\Psi^{m,n}_{\Gamma}(z,w)(X-zY)^{k}dz]

and of the Hodge filtration on
F^{1}\cong\bigoplus_{n=0}^{k}\mathrm{gr}^{n+1}_{F}H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma};\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}}).

Proof.

That
[\Psi_{\Gamma}^{m,n}(z,w)(X-zY)^{k}dz]\in F^{n+1}
follows from Theorem
5.15
, together with the properties of
\Psi^{m,n}_{\Gamma}(z,w)
(Proposition
3.3
and Corollary
3.11
). To see that we also have
[\Psi_{\Gamma}^{m,n}(z,w)(X-zY)^{k}dz]\in\overline{F}^{m+1}
, we note that
\overline{F}^{m+1}
is image of
F^{m+1}
under Betti complex-conjugation. But, by Corollary
4.8
,
\Psi^{m,n}_{\Gamma}(z,w)
is a Betti-conjugate of
-\Psi^{n,m}_{\Gamma}(z,w)
, which, by the first part of this proof, defines a class in
F^{m+1}
; this proves (
5.12
). The splitting statements follow from Proposition
5.12
.
∎

Remark 5.17
.

The splitting of the residue sequence (
5.7
) is reminiscent of the theorem of Manin-Drinfeld with the points in the orbits of
w_{i}
playing the role of the cusps, and the meromorphic modular forms
\Psi_{\Gamma}^{m,n}(z,w_{i})
playing the role of Eisenstein series. Note however that, in contrast to the Manin-Drinfeld case, which gives a splitting of the whole
\mathbb{Q}
-MHS, the above corollary concerns only the Hodge filtration.

6.
Realisations, periods, and single-valued periods

We discuss extensions in a category of mixed Hodge structures, as a prototype for an abelian category of mixed motives and their periods. Since our main results concern the computation of the associated periods, some details about the Hodge theory are omitted.

6.1.
A category of realisations

In the absence of a suitable abelian category of mixed motives, it is convenient, following Deligne, to work in a Tannakian category of realisations of motives. The variant most convenient for our purposes will be a category
\mathcal{H}_{K}
, where
K\subset\mathbb{C}
is a number field, whose objects are tuples:

(6.1)

\left(V_{\mathrm{B}}\ ,V_{\overline{\mathrm{B}}}\ ,V_{\mathrm{dR}}\ ;\mathrm{comp}_{\mathrm{B},\mathrm{dR}}\ ,\mathrm{comp}_{\overline{\mathrm{B}},\mathrm{dR}}\ ,F_{\infty}\right)

where
V_{\mathrm{dR}}
is a finite dimensional
K
-vector space, equipped with an increasing (weight) filtration
W
and a decreasing (Hodge) filtration
F
;
V_{\mathrm{B}}
and
V_{\overline{\mathrm{B}}}
are two finite-dimensional
\mathbb{Q}
-vector spaces equipped with an increasing weight filtration
W
; and

\mathrm{comp}_{\mathrm{B},\mathrm{dR}}:V_{\mathrm{dR}}\otimes_{K,\iota}\mathbb{C}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}V_{\mathrm{B}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C}\qquad\hbox{ and }\qquad\mathrm{comp}_{\overline{\mathrm{B}},\mathrm{dR}}:V_{\mathrm{dR}}\otimes_{K,\overline{\iota}}\mathbb{C}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}V_{\overline{\mathrm{B}}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C}

are isomorphisms which respect the weight filtration
W
, where
\iota:K\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
is the inclusion, and
\overline{\iota}:K\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
is its complex-conjugate. Finally, the real Frobenius
F_{\infty}:V_{\mathrm{B}}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}V_{\overline{\mathrm{B}}}
is an isomorphism preserving
W
which is compatible with
c_{\mathrm{B},\mathrm{dR}},c_{\overline{\mathrm{B}},\mathrm{dR}}
via the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{\mathrm{dR}}\otimes_{K,\iota}\mathbb{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{\mathrm{dR}}\otimes_{K,\overline{\iota}}\mathbb{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{\mathrm{B}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{\overline{\mathrm{B}}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\otimes c_{\mathrm{dR}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{comp}_{\mathrm{B},\mathrm{dR}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{comp}_{\overline{\mathrm{B}},\mathrm{dR}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{\infty}\otimes c_{\mathrm{B}}}

and where
c_{\mathrm{dR}}
,
c_{\mathrm{B}}
both denote complex conjugation.
Following convention, the inverse of
F_{\infty}
will also be denoted by
F_{\infty}
without ambiguity. The vector space
V_{\mathrm{B}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C}
(respectively
V_{\overline{\mathrm{B}}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C}
) together with the filtrations
W
,
c_{\mathrm{B},\mathrm{dR}}F
(respectively
c_{\overline{\mathrm{B}},\mathrm{dR}}F
) are required to be mixed Hodge structures.

The category
\mathcal{H}_{K}
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-linear Tannakian category which is neutralised by either of the two Betti realisation functors
which send an object (
6.1
) to
V_{\mathrm{B}}
(or to
V_{\overline{\mathrm{B}}}
), respectively.

The basic example of an object in
\mathcal{H}_{K}
is given by the system of cohomology groups in degree
n
of a smooth algebraic variety
X
defined over
K
. We define

(6.2)

H^{n}(X)=\left(H^{n}_{\mathrm{B}}(X(\mathbb{C});\mathbb{Q})\ ,\ H^{n}_{\mathrm{B}}(\overline{X}(\mathbb{C});\mathbb{Q})\ ,\ H^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X/K)\ ;\ \mathrm{comp}_{\mathrm{B},\mathrm{dR}}\ ,\mathrm{comp}_{\overline{\mathrm{B}},\mathrm{dR}}\ ,F_{\infty}\right)\ ,

where
H^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X/K)=\mathbb{H}^{n}(X;\Omega^{\bullet}_{X/k})
denotes algebraic de Rham cohomology,
\overline{X}
is the base change
X\times_{K,\overline{\iota}}\overline{\iota}(K)
, and
\mathrm{comp}_{\overline{\mathrm{B}},\mathrm{dR}}
is the comparison isomorphism for
\overline{X}
. The fact that it defines an object of
\mathcal{H}_{K}
follows from theorems of Grothendieck (on the comparison between Betti and algebraic de Rham cohomology) and Deligne (on mixed Hodge theory). If
X
is defined over a real subfield of
K
then the two Betti realisations are canonically isomorphic

(6.3)

H^{n}_{\mathrm{B}}(X(\mathbb{C});\mathbb{Q})=H^{n}_{\mathrm{B}}(\overline{X}(\mathbb{C});\mathbb{Q})\ ,

and likewise the comparison isomorphism

\mathrm{comp}_{\mathrm{B},\mathrm{dR}}=\mathrm{comp}_{\overline{\mathrm{B}},\mathrm{dR}}
. In this case, the real Frobenius
F_{\infty}
is an involution of
H^{n}_{\mathrm{B}}(X(\mathbb{C});\mathbb{Q})
; in general, it is only an isomorphism between two different vector spaces.

6.1.1.
Periods

There are two notions of periods for the category
\mathcal{H}_{K}
. Given an object (
6.1
) and the data of
\omega\in V_{\mathrm{dR}}
,
\sigma\in V_{\mathrm{B}}^{\vee}
, the associated period is

\sigma\,\mathrm{comp}_{\mathrm{B},\mathrm{dR}}(\omega)\ \in\ \mathbb{C}\ .

Similarly, given
\sigma^{\prime}\in V_{\overline{\mathrm{B}}}^{\vee}
, the associated period is
\sigma^{\prime}\,\mathrm{comp}_{\overline{\mathrm{B}},\mathrm{dR}}(\omega)\in\mathbb{C}
. We shall call such periods ‘conjugate’ periods, since the two notions are related as follows:

\overline{\sigma\,\mathrm{comp}_{\mathrm{B},\mathrm{dR}}(\omega)}=(F^{*}_{\infty}\sigma)\,\mathrm{comp}_{\overline{\mathrm{B}},\mathrm{dR}}(\omega)\ ,

where
*
denotes inverse transpose.

6.1.2.
Single-valued periods and ‘Betti-conjugation’ periods

The formalism of the category
\mathcal{H}_{K}
is sufficient to express the notion of single-valued periods. The single-valued map is

\displaystyle\mathsf{s}:V_{\mathrm{dR}}\otimes_{K,\iota}\mathbb{C}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}V_{\mathrm{dR}}\otimes_{K,\overline{\iota}}\mathbb{C}\ ,\quad\qquad\mathsf{s}\coloneqq\mathrm{comp}_{\overline{\mathrm{B}},\mathrm{dR}}^{-1}\circ(F_{\infty}\otimes\mathrm{id})\circ\mathrm{comp}_{\mathrm{B},\mathrm{dR}}\ .

Given
\omega\in V_{\mathrm{dR}}
and
\eta\in V_{\mathrm{dR}}^{\vee}
, the associated single-valued period is defined to be
\eta\,\mathsf{s}(\omega)\in\mathbb{C}
. Given a
K
-basis
(\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{r})
of
V_{\mathrm{dR}}
, the corresponding matrix of
\mathsf{s}
is given by

S=\overline{P}^{-1}P\in M_{r\times r}(\mathbb{C})\ ,

where
P
is the matrix of
\mathrm{comp}_{\mathrm{B},\mathrm{dR}}
with respect to
(\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{r})
and any other
\mathbb{Q}
-basis of
V_{\mathrm{B}}
. It does not depend on the latter choice of basis. It satisfies
\overline{S}=S^{-1}
.

Definition 6.1
.

Define the
Betti-conjugation map
relative to the inclusion
\iota:K\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
to be

(6.4)

c_{\iota}:V_{\mathrm{dR}}\otimes_{K,\iota}\mathbb{C}\longrightarrow V_{\mathrm{dR}}\otimes_{K,\iota}\mathbb{C}\ \text{, }\qquad c_{\iota}\coloneqq\mathrm{comp}_{\mathrm{B},\mathrm{dR}}^{-1}\circ(\mathrm{id}\otimes c_{\mathrm{B}})\circ\mathrm{comp}_{\mathrm{B},\mathrm{dR}}\ .

Note that it is complex anti-linear:
c_{\iota}(v\otimes\lambda)=c_{\iota}(v)\otimes\overline{\lambda}
for
v\in V_{\mathrm{dR}}
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}.

Lemma 6.2
.

The Betti-conjugation map is related to the single-valued map via the formula

c_{\iota}=(\mathrm{id}\otimes c_{\mathrm{dR}})\circ\mathsf{s}\ .

In particular, the restriction of
\mathsf{s}
and
c_{\iota}
to
V_{\mathrm{dR}}
coincide.

Proof.

It follows from the axiom
\mathrm{id}\otimes c_{\mathrm{dR}}=\mathrm{comp}_{\overline{\mathrm{B}},\mathrm{dR}}^{-1}\circ(F_{\infty}\otimes c_{\mathrm{B}})\circ\mathrm{comp}_{\mathrm{B},\mathrm{dR}}

that
\mathsf{s}\circ c_{\iota}=\mathrm{id}\otimes c_{\mathrm{dR}}
. The result follows from
c_{\iota}^{2}=\mathrm{id},
which follows from
c_{\mathrm{B}}^{2}=\mathrm{id}
, and from
c_{\mathrm{dR}}^{2}=\mathrm{id}.

∎

Betti-conjugation can be represented by the matrix
\overline{S}=P^{-1}\overline{P}=S^{-1}
, but note that it is a
\mathbb{C}
-antilinear map, so does not satisfy the usual formula for the linear action of a matrix on vectors. By abuse, we shall call such a matrix
a ‘Betti-conjugation period matrix’.

6.1.3.
Variant with coefficients

Let
F
denote a finite extension of
\mathbb{Q}
. We denote by
\mathcal{H}_{K}\otimes F
the category defined by the Karoubian envelope of the
F
-linear category obtained from
\mathcal{H}_{K}
by extending coefficients from
\mathbb{Q}
to
F
. It is an
F
-linear Tannakian category such that

(6.5)

\Hom_{\mathcal{H}_{K}\otimes F}(V,W)=\Hom_{\mathcal{H}_{K}}(V,W)\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}F

for all objects
V,W
of
\mathcal{H}_{K}
. One may define the periods (and single-valued periods) of objects in
\mathcal{H}_{K}\otimes F
: they are
F
-linear combinations of periods of
\mathcal{H}_{K}
taking values in
F\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C}
.

6.2.
Examples of objects in
\mathcal{H}_{K}

Let
K
be as above.

6.2.1.
Tate objects

The Lefschetz object
H^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{K}^{1})
in (
6.2
) is denoted by
\mathbb{Q}(-1)
. Since
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{K}=\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathbb{Q}}\times_{\mathbb{Q}}K
is defined over
\mathbb{Q}
, its two Betti realisations are identical (
6.3
). For any object
V
of
\mathcal{H}_{K}
,
V(n)
denotes the object
V\otimes\mathbb{Q}(-1)^{\otimes(-n)}
. The object
\mathbb{Q}(-n)
has periods (resp. single-valued periods) proportional to
(2\pi i)^{n}
(resp.
(-1)^{n}
).

6.2.2.
Kummer extensions

Let
x\in K^{\times}\setminus\{1\}
and consider the object
\mathcal{K}_{x}=H^{1}(\mathbb{P}_{K}^{1}\setminus\{0,\infty\},\{1,x\})
of
\mathcal{H}_{K}
. The relative long exact cohomology sequence gives

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(\mathbb{P}_{K}^{1}\setminus\{0,\infty\})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(\{1,x\})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{K}_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\mathbb{P}_{K}^{1}\setminus\{0,\infty\})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

from which we deduce a simple extension in
\mathcal{H}_{K}
of the form:

(6.6)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}(0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{K}_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}(-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0\ .}

A de Rham basis of
\mathcal{K}_{x}
is given by
[\frac{dz}{1-x}],[\frac{dz}{z}]
; a Betti basis by the classes of any path
\sigma_{x}
in
\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})\setminus\{0,\infty\}=\mathbb{C}^{\times}
from
1
to
x
, and a small simple loop
\sigma_{0}
around
0
. The period matrix and its single-valued version are

P=\begin{pmatrix}1&\log x\\
0&2\pi i\end{pmatrix}\qquad\hbox{ and }\qquad S=\overline{P}^{-1}P=\begin{pmatrix}1&\log|x|^{2}\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}\ .

6.2.3.
Elliptic curves

Let
E
be an elliptic curve defined over
K
, and denote its cohomology object (
6.2
) by
H=H^{1}(E)
in the category
\mathcal{H}_{K}
. It admits a surjective anti-symmetric polarisation morphism

\langle\ ,\ \rangle:H\otimes H\longrightarrow\mathbb{Q}(-1)

which is equivalent to a canonical isomorphism

H^{\vee}\cong H(1)\ .

Denote
S^{n}H\coloneqq\mathrm{Sym}^{n}H
, where
S^{0}H=\mathbb{Q}(0)
. The polarisation gives a canonical isomorphism
\bigwedge^{2}H\cong\mathbb{Q}(-1)
, and hence the semi-simple Tannakian subcategory of
\mathcal{H}_{K}
generated by
H
and its tensor powers has simple objects
(S^{n}H)(d)
for integers
n\geq 0,d\in\mathbb{Z}
. We have canonical isomorphisms

(S^{n}H)^{\vee}\cong S^{n}H^{\vee}\cong(S^{n}H)(n).

For all
m,n\geq 0
there is a natural surjective map (multiplication of symmetric tensors)

(6.7)

S^{m}H\otimes S^{n}H\longrightarrow S^{m+n}H\ .

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
[\omega],[\eta]
denote a de Rham basis for
H_{\mathrm{dR}}=H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(E/K)
such that
[\omega]\in F^{1}H_{\mathrm{dR}}
and which is symplectic,
i.e.
, such that
\langle[\omega],[\eta]\rangle_{\mathrm{dR}}=1
, where

\langle\ ,\ \rangle_{\mathrm{dR}}:H_{\mathrm{dR}}\otimes_{K}H_{\mathrm{dR}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Q}(-1)_{\mathrm{dR}}=K

is the pairing induced by the polarisation. Choose an isomorphism
E(\mathbb{C})\cong\mathbb{C}/(\mathbb{Z}+z\mathbb{Z})
, where
\Im(z)>0
, and consider the basis
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}
of
H_{\mathrm{B}}^{\vee}=H_{1}(E(\mathbb{C});\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}+\mathbb{Z}z
corresponding to the basis
1,z
of the lattice. If we write the period matrix with respect to these bases in the form

P=\begin{pmatrix}\int_{\sigma_{1}}\omega&\int_{\sigma_{1}}\eta\\
\int_{\sigma_{2}}\omega&\int_{\sigma_{2}}\eta\\
\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\omega_{1}&\eta_{1}\\
\omega_{2}&\eta_{2}\end{pmatrix}

then
\det(P)=2\pi i
,
z=\omega_{2}/\omega_{1}
, and the associated single-valued period matrix is

S=\overline{P}^{-1}P=-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\begin{pmatrix}\omega_{1}\overline{\eta}_{2}-\overline{\eta}_{1}\omega_{2}&\eta_{1}\overline{\eta}_{2}-\overline{\eta}_{1}\eta_{2}\\
\overline{\omega}_{1}\omega_{2}-\omega_{1}\overline{\omega}_{2}&\overline{\omega}_{1}\eta_{2}-\eta_{1}\overline{\omega}_{2}\end{pmatrix}\ .

Consider the following quantity, which is the quotient of two entries of
S
, and its complex conjugate:

(6.8)

\rho=\frac{S_{11}}{S_{21}}=\frac{\omega_{1}\overline{\eta}_{2}-\overline{\eta}_{1}\omega_{2}}{\overline{\omega}_{1}\omega_{2}-\omega_{1}\overline{\omega}_{2}}\ ,\quad\overline{\rho}=\frac{\overline{\omega}_{1}\eta_{2}-\eta_{1}\overline{\omega}_{2}}{\omega_{1}\overline{\omega}_{2}-\overline{\omega}_{1}\omega_{2}}\ .

With these notations, we have

(6.9)

\mathrm{comp}_{\mathrm{B},\mathrm{dR}}^{-1}\left(X-zY\right)=\frac{1}{\omega_{1}}\,[\omega]\qquad\hbox{ and }\qquad\mathrm{comp}_{\mathrm{B},\mathrm{dR}}^{-1}\left(X-\overline{z}Y\right)=\frac{\omega_{1}}{2\pi i}\left(\overline{z}-z\right)\left([\eta]+\overline{\rho}[\omega]\right)\ .

Proof.

The computation of the determinant (classically known as ‘Legendre’s period relation’) follows from the compatibility of the polarisation map with the comparison isomorphism and the fact that both the Betti basis and the de Rham basis are symplectic. Since, under the isomorphism
E(\mathbb{C})\cong\mathbb{C}/(\mathbb{Z}+\mathbb{Z}z)
, the class
[\omega]
is proportional to
[du]
, where
u
is the coordinate on
\mathbb{C}
, it follows that
\omega_{2}/\omega_{1}=(\int_{0}^{z}du)/(\int_{0}^{1}du)=z
.

Recall from §
5.1
that
(X,Y)
denotes the Betti basis dual to
(1,-z)
. Thus we have

\mathrm{comp}_{\mathrm{B},\mathrm{dR}}^{-1}\left(X-zY\right)=[du]=\frac{1}{\omega_{1}}\,[\omega]

and

\mathrm{comp}_{\mathrm{B},\mathrm{dR}}^{-1}\left(X-\overline{z}Y\right)=\mathrm{comp}_{\mathrm{B},\mathrm{dR}}^{-1}\circ(\mathrm{id}\otimes c_{\mathrm{B}})\left(X-zY\right)=c_{\iota}\circ\mathrm{comp}^{-1}_{\mathrm{B},\mathrm{dR}}\left(X-zY\right)=c_{\iota}\left(\frac{1}{\omega_{1}}\,[\omega]\right)

where the second equality follows from the definition of
c_{\iota}
. By applying Lemma
6.2
we obtain

\displaystyle c_{\iota}\left(\frac{1}{\omega_{1}}\,[\omega]\right)=\frac{1}{\overline{\omega}_{1}}\,c_{\iota}\left([\omega]\right)=\frac{1}{\overline{\omega}_{1}}(\mathrm{id}\otimes c_{\mathrm{dR}})\circ\mathsf{s}([\omega])=\frac{1}{\overline{\omega}_{1}}\left(\overline{S}_{11}[\omega]+\overline{S}_{21}[\eta]\right)=\frac{\overline{S}_{21}}{\overline{\omega}_{1}}\left(\overline{\rho}\,[\omega]+[\eta]\right)\ .

since the action of
\mathsf{s}
is computed by the matrix
S
. To complete the calculation, substitute the definition of
S_{21}
and use the fact that
\omega_{2}=z\omega_{1}
.
∎

6.2.4.
Elliptic curves with complex multiplication

Let
E
be an elliptic curve defined over
K
with complex multiplication by an order
\mathcal{O}
in a quadratic imaginary extension
F
of
\mathbb{Q}
, normalised such that the action of
\lambda\in\mathcal{O}
is multiplication by
\lambda
(as opposed to
\overline{\lambda}
) on
F^{1}H_{\mathrm{dR}}
. We assume that
K
contains
F
.

Lemma 6.4
.

Suppose that
E
has complex multiplication as above, and let
[\omega],[\eta]
be a basis of
H_{\mathrm{dR}}
as in Lemma
6.3
. Then, the corresponding quantity
\overline{\rho}
(see (
6.8
)) lies in
K\subset\mathbb{C}
. In particular, the de Rham class

[\eta^{\prime}]\coloneqq[\eta]+\overline{\rho}\,[\omega]

is defined over
K
, so that
[\omega],[\eta^{\prime}]
is an eigenbasis of
H_{\mathrm{dR}}
for the action of
\mathcal{O}
, which satisfies the conditions of Lemma
6.3
. The period matrix
P^{\prime}
associated to the de Rham basis
[\omega],[\eta^{\prime}]
and a symplectic basis of
H_{\mathrm{B}}
satisfies

P^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}\omega_{1}&\eta^{\prime}_{1}\\
\omega_{2}&\eta^{\prime}_{2}\end{pmatrix}\quad\hbox{ with }\quad\frac{\omega_{2}}{\omega_{1}}=z\ \hbox{ and }\ \frac{\eta^{\prime}_{2}}{\eta^{\prime}_{1}}=\overline{z}\ ,

where
z
lies in the quadratic imaginary field
F
.

Proof.

Let
\varphi:H\to H
be an endomorphism in
\mathcal{H}_{K}
corresponding to some
\lambda\in\mathcal{O}\setminus\mathbb{Z}
. Since
\varphi_{\mathrm{dR}}:H_{\mathrm{dR}}\to H_{\mathrm{dR}}
is compatible with the Hodge structure, its eigenvalues are
\lambda
and
\overline{\lambda}
, and its action on the basis
[\omega],[\eta]
satisfies

\varphi_{\mathrm{dR}}\,[\omega]=\lambda\,[\omega]\qquad\varphi_{\mathrm{dR}}\,[\eta]=\mu\,[\omega]+\overline{\lambda}\,[\eta]

for some
\mu\in K
. The compatibility between
\varphi_{\mathrm{dR}}
and the single-valued map
\mathsf{s}
, namely
\mathsf{s}\circ(\varphi_{\mathrm{dR}}\otimes_{\iota}\mathrm{id})=(\varphi_{\mathrm{dR}}\otimes_{\overline{\iota}}\mathrm{id})\circ\mathsf{s}
, amounts to the matrix identity

\begin{pmatrix}S_{11}\lambda&S_{11}\mu+S_{12}\overline{\lambda}\\
S_{21}\lambda&S_{21}\mu+S_{22}\overline{\lambda}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}S_{11}&S_{12}\\
S_{21}&S_{22}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\lambda&\mu\\
0&\overline{\lambda}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\overline{\lambda}&\overline{\mu}\\
0&\lambda\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}S_{11}&S_{12}\\
S_{21}&S_{22}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\overline{\lambda}S_{11}+\overline{\mu}S_{21}&\overline{\lambda}S_{12}+\overline{\mu}S_{22}\\
\lambda S_{21}&\lambda S_{22}\end{pmatrix}\ .

By taking the quotient of the
(1,1)
entry by the
(2,1)
entry on both sides of the above equation, we obtain the relation

\rho=\frac{S_{11}}{S_{21}}=\frac{\overline{\lambda}S_{11}+\overline{\mu}S_{21}}{\lambda S_{21}}=\frac{\overline{\lambda}}{\lambda}\rho+\frac{\overline{\mu}}{\lambda}\iff\overline{\rho}=\frac{\lambda}{\overline{\lambda}}\overline{\rho}+\frac{\mu}{\overline{\lambda}}\ .

Since
\lambda\neq\overline{\lambda}
, we conclude that
\overline{\rho}\in K
. To see that
[\omega],[\eta^{\prime}]
is an eigenbasis for
\varphi_{\mathrm{dR}}
, it suffices to use the previous formula for
\overline{\rho}
to check that
\varphi_{\mathrm{dR}}[\eta^{\prime}]=\overline{\lambda}[\eta^{\prime}]
.

The assertion that
z\coloneqq\omega_{2}/\omega_{1}
lies in
F
is classical; it follows from a period matrix computation, similar to the argument in the last paragraph, given by the compatibility
\mathrm{comp}_{\mathrm{B},\mathrm{dR}}\circ(\varphi_{\mathrm{dR}}\otimes_{\iota}\mathrm{id})=(\varphi_{\mathrm{B}}\otimes\mathrm{id})\circ\mathrm{comp}_{\mathrm{B},\mathrm{dR}}
. From the formula (
6.8
) for
\overline{\rho}
, we deduce that:

\frac{\eta_{2}^{\prime}}{\eta_{1}^{\prime}}=\frac{\eta_{2}+\overline{\rho}\,\omega_{2}}{\eta_{1}+\overline{\rho}\,\omega_{1}}=\frac{\overline{\omega}_{2}}{\overline{\omega}_{1}}=\overline{z}\ .\qed

Remark 6.5
.

The algebraicity of
\overline{\rho}
fails if
[\omega],[\eta]
is not adapted to the Hodge filtration (
[\omega]\notin F^{1}H_{\mathrm{dR}}
).

Remark 6.6
.

It follows from Lemma
6.3
that, for
[\omega],[\eta^{\prime}]
as in Lemma
6.4
, we have

\mathrm{comp}^{-1}_{\mathrm{B},\mathrm{dR}}(X-zY)=\frac{1}{\omega_{1}}[\omega]\qquad\hbox{ and }\qquad\mathrm{comp}^{-1}_{\mathrm{B},\mathrm{dR}}(X-\overline{z}Y)=\frac{\omega_{1}}{2\pi i}(\overline{z}-z)[\eta^{\prime}]\ .

Proposition 6.7
.

Let
E
be as above and
H=H^{1}(E)
. There is a canonical decomposition in
\mathcal{H}_{K}

(6.10)

S^{n}H=\bigoplus_{0\leq 2i\leq n}C^{n-2i}(-i)

where
C^{0}=\mathbb{Q}(0)
, and, for
m\geq 1
, the objects
C^{m}
are of rank two with Hodge numbers
(0,m)
and
(m,0)
.

Proof.

The graph of complex multiplication by
\lambda\in\mathcal{O}
defines an algebraic cycle
\Gamma_{\lambda}\subset E\times_{K}E
defined over
K
. The cycle class map defines a morphism
H^{0}(\Gamma_{\lambda})(-1)\rightarrow H^{2}(E\times_{K}E)
in
\mathcal{H}_{K}
, inducing a map

\varphi_{\lambda}:\mathbb{Q}(-1)\longrightarrow S^{2}H\ ,

after projecting onto the summand
H\otimes H\subset H^{2}(E\times_{K}E)
by the Künneth decomposition, and from there onto
S^{2}H
. We compute this map in de Rham cohomology. Let
e,f
denote a symplectic eigenbasis for the action
\varphi_{\mathrm{dR}}
of complex multiplication by
\lambda
on
H_{\mathrm{dR}}
(
e.g.
,
[\omega],[\eta^{\prime}]
as in Lemma
6.4
), which is represented by

\varphi_{\mathrm{dR}}=e^{\vee}\otimes\lambda\,e+f^{\vee}\otimes\overline{\lambda}\,f\ \in\ \End(H_{\mathrm{dR}})=H_{\mathrm{dR}}^{\vee}\otimes H_{\mathrm{dR}}\ ,

where
\overline{\lambda}
is the complex conjugate of
\lambda
. Since
e,f
is symplectic (
i.e.
,
\langle e,f\rangle_{\mathrm{dR}}=1
), the isomorphism
H^{\vee}\cong H(1)
induced by Poincaré duality satisfies
e^{\vee}=f
and
f^{\vee}=-e
. Therefore the element
\varphi_{\mathrm{dR}}
corresponds via this isomorphism to

f\otimes\lambda e-e\otimes\overline{\lambda}f\ \in\ H_{\mathrm{dR}}\otimes H_{\mathrm{dR}}\ .

Its image in
S^{2}H_{\mathrm{dR}}
is
(\lambda-\overline{\lambda})ef
and is non-zero if and only if
\Im(\lambda)\neq 0
, in which case
\varphi_{\lambda}:\mathbb{Q}(-1)\rightarrow S^{2}H
is injective. Choose any
\lambda\in\mathcal{O}
with non-vanishing imaginary part (equivalently,
\lambda\not\in\mathbb{Z}
) and write
\varphi=\varphi_{\lambda}
. By the above, it only depends on
\mathrm{Im}\,\lambda
and is therefore well-defined up to multiplication by a non-zero rational number.
Let
C^{1}=H
and for all
n\geq 2
define

C^{n}=\coker\left(\varphi^{n}:\mathbb{Q}(-1)\otimes S^{n-2}H\longrightarrow S^{n}H\right)\ ,

where the map
\varphi^{n}
is the composition of the injection
\varphi\otimes\mathrm{id}:\mathbb{Q}(-1)\otimes S^{n-2}H\rightarrow S^{2}H\otimes S^{n-2}H
with the multiplication (
6.7
) in the case
m=2.
This map is injective. To see this, consider the eigenspace decomposition of the de Rham realisation
H_{\mathrm{dR}}=H_{\mathrm{dR}}^{\lambda}\oplus H_{\mathrm{dR}}^{\overline{\lambda}}
with respect to the complex multiplication. Let us denote the
\lambda^{p}\overline{\lambda}^{q}
-eigenspace in
S^{n}H_{\mathrm{dR}}
by
H_{\mathrm{dR}}^{p,q}\cong(H_{\mathrm{dR}}^{\lambda})^{\otimes p}\otimes(H_{\mathrm{dR}}^{\overline{\lambda}})^{\otimes q}
. By the above computation, the image of
\varphi_{\mathrm{dR}}
is the component
H^{1,1}_{\mathrm{dR}}
of
S^{2}H_{\mathrm{dR}}
generated by
ef
. Therefore
\varphi^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}
is multiplication by
ef

\varphi^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}\ :\ H^{1,1}_{\mathrm{dR}}\otimes\bigoplus_{p+q=n-2}H^{p,q}_{\mathrm{dR}}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\bigoplus_{p+q=n-2}H^{p+1,q+1}_{\mathrm{dR}}\ \subset\ S^{n}H_{\mathrm{dR}}\ ,

which is injective. Thus
\varphi^{n}
is injective, and we see from the above that
C^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}\cong H^{n,0}_{\mathrm{dR}}\oplus H^{0,n}_{\mathrm{dR}}
has rank
2
.

Since
H^{\otimes n}
and all the objects defined above lie in a semi-simple Tannakian subcategory of
\mathcal{H}_{K}
, we deduce from the definition of
C^{n}
and injectivity of
\varphi^{n}
a canonical decomposition

S^{n}H=C^{n}\oplus S^{n-2}H(-1)\ ,

from which (
6.10
) follows. Since
H
has Hodge types
(1,0)
and
(0,1)
,
S^{n}H
has Hodge numbers
(n-i,i)
for
0\leq i\leq n
with multiplicity
1
. By the previous equation,
C^{n}
has Hodge numbers
(0,n)
and
(n,0)
.
∎

Remark 6.8
.

The previous proposition holds in the category of Chow motives over
K
with trivial coefficients.

Remark 6.9
.

In the category
\mathcal{H}_{K}\otimes F
, complex multiplication by a non-real element of
\mathcal{O}
defines an endomorphism of
H\otimes F
with distinct eigenvalues in
F
. Therefore it may be decomposed into eigenspaces

H\otimes F\cong M_{+}\oplus M_{-}

in
\mathcal{H}_{K}\otimes F
. The objects
M_{+},M_{-}
are realisations of rank one Chow motives over
K
with coefficients in
F
. The objects
C^{m}\otimes F
in turn decompose into a direct sum
M^{\otimes m}_{+}\oplus M^{\otimes m}_{-}
of rank one objects. By permuting
M_{+}
and
M_{-}
if necessary, we may assume that
(M_{+})_{\mathrm{dR}}\cong H^{\lambda}_{\mathrm{dR}}
,
(M_{-})_{\mathrm{dR}}\cong H^{\overline{\lambda}}_{\mathrm{dR}}
.
Since the complex multiplication respects the Hodge filtration, we have also
(M_{+})_{\mathrm{dR}}=F^{1}H_{\mathrm{dR}}
.

7.
Mixed modular objects in a category of realisations

The first paragraphs of this section study the rational structure on algebraic de Rham cohomology of punctured modular curves and the field of definition of the meromorphic forms
\Psi^{r,s}_{\Gamma}(z,w)
.
The section concludes with the definition of mixed modular relative cohomology objects in a category of realisations.

7.1.
De Rham cohomology of punctured modular curves over a number field

From now on, we assume for simplicity that
\Gamma\leq\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
is a
congruence
subgroup. We shall also assume that the finite union of
\Gamma
-orbits
S=\Gamma w_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\Gamma w_{r}
is such that the
j
-invariants
j(w_{i})
are algebraic numbers for
i=1,\ldots,r
. This implies that there is a number field
K\subset\mathbb{C}
over which the universal elliptic curve
\mathcal{E}_{\Gamma}\to\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}
and the closed substack
\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma}\subset\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}
are defined; we denote by
\mathcal{E}_{\Gamma,K}\to\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma,K}\subset\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K}
their
K
-models to emphasize the base field. We denote by
\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma,K}
the open complement
\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma,K}
.

Example 7.1
.

If
\Gamma
contains the principal congruence subgroup
\Gamma(N)
, then it is classical that we can take
\mathbb{Q}(e^{\frac{2\pi i}{N}})
as a field of definition for the modular stack
\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}
, and
K=\mathbb{Q}(e^{\frac{2\pi i}{N}},j(w_{1}),\ldots,j(w_{r}))
. For the Hecke congruence subgroup
\Gamma_{0}(N)
, we can take
K=\mathbb{Q}(j(w_{1}),\ldots,j(w_{r}))
.

The system
(\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}},\nabla_{k}^{\mathbb{C}},F^{\bullet}\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}})
discussed in §
5.3
comes from a system
(\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},K},\nabla_{k}^{K},F^{\bullet}\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},K})
defined over
K
, for instance:

\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},K}\coloneqq\Sym^{k}H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{E}_{\Gamma,K}/\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K})\ .

By restricting it to
\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma,K}
and computing de Rham cohomology, we obtain a
K
-structure on (
5.6
):

H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},K})\otimes_{K}\mathbb{C}\cong H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma};\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}})\ .

Let
d
be the width of the cusp at infinity, so that a meromorphic modular form
f\in M_{l,\Gamma}^{!}(*S)
admits a Fourier expansion of the form

f(z)=\sum_{n\gg-\infty}a_{n}q^{\frac{n}{d}}\ \text{, }\qquad q=e^{2\pi iz}\ ,

near
q=0
. We denote by
M_{l,\Gamma}^{!}(*S)_{K}\subset M_{l,\Gamma}^{!}(*S)
(resp.
M_{l,\Gamma}(mS)_{K}\subset M_{l,\Gamma}(mS)
) the
K
-vector subspace given by the condition
a_{n}\in K
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Recall that the ‘classical Tate curve’ admits the affine equation
y^{2}=4x^{3}-\frac{E_{4}(q)}{12}x+\frac{E_{6}(q)}{216}
over
\mathbb{Q}(\!(q)\!)
and the class
\omega_{q}\coloneqq 2\pi i(X-zY)
gets identified with
[dx/y]
. Thus, by the ‘
q
-expansion principle’, there is an isomorphism

M_{k+2,\Gamma}^{!}(*S)_{K}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{0}(\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma,K},F^{k}\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},K}\otimes\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma,K}/K})\ \text{, }\qquad f\longmapsto(2\pi i)^{k+1}f(z)(X-zY)^{k}dz=f\,\omega_{q}^{k}\,\frac{dq}{q}\ .

The above map also induces, for every
m\geq 0
, a
K
-rational version of the isomorphism (
5.11
):

M_{k+2,\Gamma}(mS)_{K}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{0}(\mathcal{X}_{\Gamma,K},F^{k}\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},K}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}_{\Gamma,K}}(m\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma,K})\otimes\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{X}_{\Gamma,K}/K}(\log\mathcal{Z}_{\Gamma,K})),

where
\mathcal{X}_{\Gamma,K}
(resp.
\mathcal{Z}_{\Gamma,K}
) denotes the compactified modular stack (resp. the cuspidal locus) defined over
K
. In particular, it follows that

M_{k+2,\Gamma}^{!}(*S)_{K}\otimes_{K}\mathbb{C}\cong M_{k+2,\Gamma}^{!}(*S)\ \text{, }\qquad M_{k+2,\Gamma}(mS)_{K}\otimes_{K}\mathbb{C}\cong M_{k+2,\Gamma}(mS).

Note that we also have a residue sequence of
K
-vector spaces (
cf.
§
A.2
) whose base change to
\mathbb{C}
is (
5.7
):

(7.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},K})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},K})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}(\Sym^{k}H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{E}_{w_{i},K}/K))^{\Gamma_{w_{i}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0\ ,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res}

where
\mathcal{E}_{w_{i},K}
is a
K
-model of the complex elliptic curve
\mathcal{E}_{w_{i}}
, which exists by the assumption that
j(w_{i})\in K
for all
i=1,\ldots,r
. The
K
-rational versions of the main results of Section
5
(namely Proposition
5.5
and Theorem
5.15
, after rescaling by suitable powers of
2\pi i
) are summarised in the next proposition; the proofs are analogous.

Theorem 7.2
.

There is an exact sequence of
K
-vector spaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{!}_{-k,\Gamma}(*S)_{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{k+2,\Gamma}^{!}(*S)_{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},K})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(q\frac{d}{dq})^{k+1}h}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[f\,\omega_{q}^{k}\frac{dq}{q}]}}

inducing isomorphisms

M_{k+2}((k+2-p)S)_{K}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}F^{p}H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},K})\text{, }\qquad p=1,\ldots,k+1.

In particular, for every
p=1,\ldots,k+1
, we have a residue exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(M_{k,\Gamma})_{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{k+2,\Gamma}((k+2-p)S)_{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}F^{p-1}(\Sym^{k}H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{E}_{w_{i},K}/K))^{\Gamma_{w_{i}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{p}H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},K})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{p}H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},K})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}F^{p-1}(\Sym^{k}H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{E}_{w_{i},K}/K))^{\Gamma_{w_{i}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res}

7.2.
The field of definition of
\Psi^{r,s}_{\Gamma}(z,w)

In this paragraph, we only consider the case of a single orbit
S=\Gamma w
. Recall that
j(w)
is algebraic and
K
contains
\mathbb{Q}(j(w))
. Furthermore, if
w
is CM, then we shall also assume that
K
contains the CM field
\mathbb{Q}(w)
.

To fix ideas, we assume for the time being that
w
is not an elliptic point, so that
\Gamma_{w}\leq\{\pm I\}
. If
k
is odd, we assume moreover that
\Gamma_{w}=\{I\}
(otherwise the group
\big(\Sym^{k}H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{E}_{w,K}/K)\big)^{\Gamma_{w}}
vanishes). Then, if
[\omega],[\eta]
is a
K
-basis of
H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{E}_{w,K}/K)
as in Lemma
6.3
, we have
(\Sym^{k}H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{E}_{w,K}/K))^{\Gamma_{w}}=\Sym^{k}H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{E}_{w,K}/K)
(
cf.
Remark
3.12
), with a
K
-basis given by

[\eta]^{m}[\omega]^{n}\text{, }\qquad m+n=k\text{, }m,n\geq 0.

By the residue sequence in Theorem
7.2
, for every
m,n\geq 0
satisfying
m+n=k
, there exists a meromorphic modular form with
K
-rational Fourier coefficients

f^{m,n}\in M_{k+2,\Gamma}((k+1-n)S)_{K}\cong F^{n+1}H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},K})

such that

(7.2)

\Res\,[(2\pi i)^{k+1}f^{m,n}(z)(X-zY)^{k}dz]=[\eta]^{m}[\omega]^{n}.

Note that
f^{m,n}
is not unique in general. By adding to
f^{m,n}
a
K
-linear combination of Eisenstein series for
\Gamma
, we may assume that
f^{m,n}
vanishes at the cusps.

Proposition 7.3
.

Let
m,n\geq 0
with
m+n=k
. With the above notation, and
\omega_{1}
,
\rho
as in Lemma
6.3
, and
\kappa^{m,n}_{\Gamma,w}\in\{1,2\}
as in Corollary
3.11
, we have

(7.3)

\Psi_{\Gamma}^{m,n}(z,w)=\binom{k}{m}\frac{\kappa^{m,n}_{\Gamma,w}(2\pi i)^{n+1}\omega_{1}^{m-n}}{(w-\overline{w})^{n}}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{m}\binom{m}{j}\overline{\rho}^{j}f^{m-j,n+j}(z)\right)+h^{m,n}(z)

for some cusp form
h^{m,n}
for
\Gamma
of weight
k+2
with complex coefficients.

Proof.

Let

f(z)=\Psi_{\Gamma}^{m,n}(z,w)-\binom{k}{m}\frac{\kappa^{m,n}_{\Gamma,w}(2\pi i)^{n+1}\omega_{1}^{m-n}}{(w-\overline{w})^{n}}\sum_{j=0}^{m}\binom{m}{j}\overline{\rho}^{j}f^{m-j,n+j}(z)\in M_{k+2,\Gamma}((k+1-n)S).

By Corollary
3.11
, Lemma
6.3
, and equation (
7.2
), we have
\Res[f(z)(X-zY)^{k}dz]=0
. Then, by Proposition
5.12
, there is a holomorphic modular form
h^{m,n}
satisfying the equation in the statement. To see that
h^{m,n}
is a cusp form, it suffices to remark that
\Psi^{m,n}_{\Gamma}
(by Proposition
3.3
) and every
f^{m-j,n+j}
(by construction) vanish at the cusps.
∎

Example 7.4
.

We have in particular

\Psi_{\Gamma}^{0,k}(z,w)=\frac{\kappa_{\Gamma,w}^{0,k}(2\pi i)^{k+1}}{\omega_{1}^{k}(w-\overline{w})^{k}}f^{0,k}(z)+h^{0,k}(z),

where
f^{0,k}
is a meromorphic modular form with
K
-rational Fourier coefficients, and
h^{0,k}
is a cusp form. This is compatible with Example
3.13
: when
\Gamma=\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
and
k\in\{2,4,6,8,12\}
, we have
\kappa_{\Gamma,w}^{0,k}=2
for non-elliptic
w
,
h^{0,k}=0
, and
f^{0,k}(z)
is
K
-proportional to
E_{k+2}(z)/(j(z)-j(w))
. The value of the prefactor
j^{\prime}(w)/E_{k+2}(w)
is
K
-proportional to the quotient of periods
(2\pi i)^{k+1}/\omega_{1}^{k}
.

If
w
is CM (possibly elliptic), then
\overline{\rho}\in K
by Lemma
6.4
, so that

[\eta^{\prime}]=[\eta]+\overline{\rho}[\omega]\in H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{E}_{w,K}/K),

and we can take
[\omega],[\eta^{\prime}]
as a
K
-basis of
H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{E}_{w,K}/K)
; it is a basis of eigenvectors for the action of complex multiplication (
cf.
Lemmas
6.3
and
6.4
). Moreover,

\kappa^{m,n}_{\Gamma,w}[\eta^{\prime}]^{m}[\omega]^{n}\text{, }\qquad m,n\geq 0\text{, }m+n=k

generates the
K
-vector space
(\Sym^{k}H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{E}_{w,K}/K))^{\Gamma_{w}}
. Again, by the residue sequence in Theorem
7.2
, and an argument involving Eisenstein series, for every
m,n\geq 0
satisfying
m+n=k
, there is a meromorphic modular form with
K
-rational Fourier coefficients

g^{m,n}\in M_{k+2,\Gamma}((k+1-n)S)_{K}\cong F^{n+1}H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},K})

which vanishes at the cusps and satisfies (note the prefactors
\kappa^{m,n}_{\Gamma,w}
compared to (
7.2
))

(7.4)

\Res\,[(2\pi i)^{k+1}g^{m,n}(z)(X-zY)^{k}dz]=\kappa^{m,n}_{\Gamma,w}[\eta^{\prime}]^{m}[\omega]^{n}.

Proposition 7.5
.

Let
m,n\geq 0
with
m+n=k
and let
w\in\mathfrak{H}
be a CM point. With the above notation, we have

\Psi_{\Gamma}^{m,n}(z,w)=\binom{k}{m}\frac{(2\pi i)^{n+1}\omega_{1}^{m-n}}{(w-\overline{w})^{n}}g^{m,n}(z)+h^{m,n}(z)

for some cusp form
h^{m,n}
for
\Gamma
of weight
k+2
. In particular, if
k=2m
is even, then for the ‘middle term’
m=n
, there is no power of the period
\omega_{1}
:

(7.5)

\Psi_{\Gamma}^{m,m}(z,w)=\binom{k}{m}\frac{(2\pi i)^{m+1}}{(w-\overline{w})^{m}}g^{m,m}(z)+h^{m,m}(z).

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Proposition
7.3
: we set

g(z)=\Psi_{\Gamma}^{m,n}(z,w)-\binom{k}{m}\frac{(2\pi i)^{n+1}\omega_{1}^{m-n}}{(w-\overline{w})^{n}}g^{m,n}(z)\in M_{k+2,\Gamma}((k+1-n)S),

and we use Corollary
3.11
, Lemma
6.3
, Remark
6.6
, and equation (
7.4
), to conclude that
\Res[g(z)(X-zY)^{k}dz]=0
. The result then follows from Proposition
5.12
.
∎

Note that, in the above statement, the quantity
w-\overline{w}
is algebraic. The proposition shows that, up to some powers of
2\pi i
and
\omega_{1}
, the meromorphic modular form
\Psi_{\Gamma}^{m,n}
can be decomposed as the sum of an ‘algebraic part’ (given by the meromorphic modular form
g^{m,n}
with
K
-rational Fourier coefficients) and a ‘transcendental part’ (given by the cusp form
h^{m,n}
, which has complex coefficients). It would be interesting to study the Fourier coefficients of
\Psi_{\Gamma}^{m,n}
from the point of view of single-valued periods of the corresponding cohomology groups, in the same spirit as
[
Fon20
]
. We also remark that the cancellation of the powers of the period
\omega_{1}
in equation (
7.5
) is a crucial point for the Gross-Zagier conjecture (see the proof of Theorem
9.1
).

Remark 7.6
.

Let
\Gamma=\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
and
D<0
be the discriminant of the CM point
w
. We may take
K=\mathbb{Q}(w,j(w))
as the Hilbert class field of
\mathbb{Q}(w)=\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{D})
. Let us recall the classical correspondence between binary quadratic forms with integer coefficients and CM points. The orbit
\Gamma w
corresponds to a
\Gamma
-equivalence class
\mathcal{A}
of primitive positive-definite binary quadratic forms
Q(U,V)=AU^{2}+BUV+CV^{2}
of discriminant
D=B^{2}-4AC
via

(7.6)

Q\longmapsto w(Q)=\frac{-B+\sqrt{D}}{2A}.

Here, ‘primitive positive-definite’ amounts to
A,B,C\in\mathbb{Z}
coprime and
A>0
. Note that
w(Q)
is the unique point of the upper half-plane such that
Q(w(Q),1)=0
; more precisely,

Q(U,V)=\frac{\sqrt{D}}{w(Q)-\overline{w(Q)}}(U-w(Q)V)(U-\overline{w(Q)}V).

Note also that the map (
7.6
) is
\Gamma
-equivariant:
w(Q|_{\gamma^{-1}})=\gamma w(Q)
for every
\gamma\in\Gamma
. Let
Q
be the quadratic form corresponding to
w
. Then

(z-\gamma w)(z-\gamma\overline{w})=\frac{\gamma w-\gamma\overline{w}}{\sqrt{D}}Q|_{\gamma}(z,1)=\frac{w-\overline{w}}{\sqrt{D}}\frac{Q|_{\gamma}(z,1)}{j_{\gamma}(w)j_{\gamma}(\overline{w})}

and, by Proposition
3.3
, we have:

\displaystyle\Psi^{m,m}_{\Gamma}(z,w)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\frac{1}{j_{\gamma}^{m+1}(w)j_{\gamma}^{m+1}(\overline{w})}\frac{w-\overline{w}}{(z-\gamma w)^{m+1}(z-\gamma\overline{w})^{m+1}}=\frac{\sqrt{D}^{m+1}}{(w-\overline{w})^{m}}\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\frac{1}{Q|_{\gamma}(z,1)^{m+1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{D}^{m+1}}{(w-\overline{w})^{m}}\sum_{R\in\mathcal{A}}\frac{1}{R(z,1)^{m+1}}=\frac{(\pi\sqrt{D})^{m+1}}{(w-\overline{w})^{m}}f_{m+1,D,\mathcal{A}}(z),

where
f_{m+1,D,\mathcal{A}}
is the meromorphic modular form considered in
[
Ben15
, Equation (3)]
. In this case, our decomposition result (
7.5
) is essentially the same as
[
Ben15
, Equation (10)]
.

7.3.
Construction of general modular objects in
\mathcal{H}_{K}

Let
\Gamma\leq\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
be a congruence subgroup and
P,Q\subset\mathfrak{H}
be unions of finitely many
\Gamma
-orbits such that
P\cap Q=\emptyset
. We also assume that
j(w)
is algebraic for every
w\in P\cup Q
and we let
K\subset\mathbb{C}
be a number field over which the open modular stack
\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}
is defined and which also contains
\mathbb{Q}(j(w))
for every
w\in P\cup Q
, and
\mathbb{Q}(w)
for every CM point
w\in P\cup Q
. In particular, we have a stack
\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K}
over
K
, with finite substacks
\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma,K},\mathcal{Q}_{\Gamma,K}\subset\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K}
.

We shall describe an object of the category of realisations
\mathcal{H}_{K}
(
cf.
§
6.1
) whose de Rham realisation is the relative de Rham cohomology with coefficients

H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma,K},\mathcal{Q}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},K}).

To study the action of complex conjugation, let

\overline{\Gamma}\coloneqq\epsilon\Gamma\epsilon^{-1}\leq\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}),\qquad\text{ where }\epsilon=\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{matrix}\right).

Note that
\epsilon
is idempotent and normalises
\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

Lemma 7.7
.

The map
z\mapsto-\overline{z}
induces a diffeomorphism of (real) orbifolds:
\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{an}}\cong\mathcal{Y}_{\overline{\Gamma}}^{\mathrm{an}}
. Moreover, if a function
f(z)
is modular for
\Gamma
of weight
k
, then
\overline{f(-\overline{z})}
is modular for
\overline{\Gamma}
of weight
k
and its Fourier coefficients are complex-conjugate to those of
f
.

Proof.

One checks that
\gamma(-\overline{z})=-\overline{(\epsilon\gamma\epsilon^{-1})(z)}
and
j_{\gamma}(-\overline{z})=\overline{j_{\epsilon\gamma\epsilon^{-1}}(z)}
for all
\gamma\in\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.
∎

Let
\overline{K}=\overline{\iota}(K)\subset\mathbb{C}
be the complex conjugate of the number field
K
(see the notation in §
6.1
). Since modular curves defined over
K
are parametrised by modular forms with
K
-rational Fourier coefficients, we deduce from the above lemma that the complex modular stack
\mathcal{Y}_{\overline{\Gamma}}
admits an
\overline{K}
-model
\mathcal{Y}_{\overline{\Gamma},\overline{K}}
, which is isomorphic to the conjugate
\overline{\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K}}=\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K}\times_{K,\overline{\iota}}\overline{K}
, and that the complex conjugation

(7.7)

\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{an}}\cong\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K}(\mathbb{C})\longrightarrow\overline{\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K}}(\mathbb{C})\cong\mathcal{Y}_{\overline{\Gamma}}^{\mathrm{an}}

is induced by
z\mapsto-\overline{z}
on the uniformisation.

The above complex conjugation extends to the level of universal elliptic curves
\mathcal{E}_{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{an}}\cong\mathcal{E}_{\overline{\Gamma}}^{\mathrm{an}}
: it is induced by
u\mapsto\overline{u}
on the fibres
\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{an}}_{z}\cong\mathbb{C}/(\mathbb{Z}+\mathbb{Z}z)\to\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{an}}_{-\overline{z}}\cong\mathbb{C}/(\mathbb{Z}-\mathbb{Z}\overline{z})
. In particular, it induces a real Frobenius between the Betti local systems
\mathbb{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{B}}
on
\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}
and
\mathcal{Y}_{\overline{\Gamma}}
, given explicitly by

(X,Y)\longmapsto(X,Y)|_{\epsilon}=(X,-Y).

Together with (
7.7
), this yields by functoriality a real Frobenius on cohomology

F_{\infty}:H^{1}_{\mathrm{B}}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{an}}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma},\mathcal{Q}_{\Gamma};\mathbb{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{B}})\longrightarrow H^{1}_{\mathrm{B}}(\mathcal{Y}_{\overline{\Gamma}}^{\mathrm{an}}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{\overline{\Gamma}},\mathcal{Q}_{\overline{\Gamma}};\mathbb{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{B}}),

where
\mathcal{P}_{\overline{\Gamma}}
denotes the image of
\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}
under
(
7.7
)
and similarly for
\mathcal{Q}_{\overline{\Gamma}}
.

Definition 7.8
.

With the above notation, we define for every integer
k\geq 0
an object of the category of realisations
\mathcal{H}_{K}
by

(7.8)

H^{1}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma,K},\mathcal{Q}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})=\big(H^{1}_{\mathrm{B}}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{an}}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma},\mathcal{Q}_{\Gamma};\mathbb{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{B}})\ ,\ H^{1}_{\mathrm{B}}(\mathcal{Y}_{\overline{\Gamma}}^{\mathrm{an}}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{\overline{\Gamma}},\mathcal{Q}_{\overline{\Gamma}};\mathbb{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{B}})\ ,\\
\ H_{\mathrm{dR}}^{1}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma,K},\mathcal{Q}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},K})\ ;\ \mathrm{comp}_{\mathrm{B},\mathrm{dR}},\mathrm{comp}_{\overline{\mathrm{B}},\mathrm{dR}},F_{\infty}\big)

where the real Frobenius was discussed above, and the comparison maps are induced by the usual Grothendieck comparison isomorphism on the universal elliptic curve.

The Hodge filtration is defined as in §
A.4
(
cf.
§
5.5
). The weight filtration, which we do not use explicitly in this paper, as well as the fact that these form a mixed Hodge structure is worked out in
[
Zuc79
]
(see Remark
A.7
). It is often more convenient to consider a
cuspidal
, or
parabolic
, variant

H^{1}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma,K},\mathcal{Q}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})

of (
7.8
). This is the sub-object
of
H^{1}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma,K},\mathcal{Q}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})
whose (first) Betti realisation is

(7.9)

\displaystyle\im(H^{1}(\mathcal{X}_{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{an}}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma},\mathcal{Q}_{\Gamma};j_{!}\mathbb{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{B}})\to H^{1}(\mathcal{Y}^{\mathrm{an}}_{\Gamma}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma},\mathcal{Q}_{\Gamma};\mathbb{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{B}}))\cong H^{1}(\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{an}}_{\Gamma}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma},\mathcal{Q}_{\Gamma};j_{*}\mathbb{V}^{\mathrm{B}}_{k}),

where
j:\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}_{\Gamma,K}
is inclusion into the compactified modular stack (
cf.

[
Loo97
, Lemma 5.3]
and
[
Zuc79
, Theorem 14.4]
). The de Rham realisation can also be explicitly described as the kernel of the ‘residue map at the cusps’

H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma,K},\mathcal{Q}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{dR},K}_{k})\longrightarrow H_{\mathrm{dR}}^{0}(\mathcal{Z}_{\Gamma,K};\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},K})/\im(N)

where
N
is the nilpotent endomorphism given by the residue of the logarithimic connection
\overline{\nabla}_{k}^{K}
on
\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},K}
which extends
\nabla_{k}^{K}
(
cf.

[
Zuc79
, Remark 14.5]
). Concretely, using Theorem
7.2
, one can check that cohomology classes in
H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR},\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma,K},\mathcal{Q}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{dR},K}_{k})
are represented by pairs

(f\omega_{q}^{k}\textstyle{\frac{dq}{q}},v)=((2\pi i)^{k+1}f(z)(X-zY)^{k}dz,(v_{1},\ldots,v_{s}))

where
f\in M_{\Gamma,k+2}^{!}(*P)_{K}
is a meromorphic modular form which is holomorphic outside
P
and the cusps, with
K
-rational Fourier coefficients at infinity, and vanishing constant Fourier coefficient at every cusp,
i.e.
,
f
is a
cuspidal
meromorphic modular form. Furthermore, writing
Q
as a disjoint union of
\Gamma
-orbits
Q=\Gamma z_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\Gamma z_{s}
, the vector
v=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{s})
is an element of
H_{\mathrm{dR}}^{0}(\mathcal{Q}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},K})\cong\bigoplus_{i=1}^{s}\Sym^{k}(H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{E}_{z_{i},K}/K))^{\Gamma_{z_{i}}}
.

8.
Modular biextensions and their single-valued periods

We study in more detail the structure of the relative cohomology modular objects defined in the previous section, and show that they are biextensions. We first consider the case where they reduce to simple extensions before turning to the general case. We then explain how complex multiplication, and the action of Hecke correspondences, enables us to extract a subquotient which is a simple extension of Tate objects.

8.1.
Simple modular extensions

Consider the situation of Definition
7.8
when
P=\emptyset
and
Q=\Gamma z_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\Gamma z_{s}
, with
z_{i}\not\in\Gamma z_{j}
for
i\neq j
. The long exact relative cohomology sequence in the category
\mathcal{H}_{K}
is:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(\mathcal{Q}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K},\mathcal{Q}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}

If
k>0
then
H^{0}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})
vanishes and we obtain a short exact sequence:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{i=1}^{s}\left(\Sym^{k}H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{z_{i},K})\right)^{\Gamma_{z_{i}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K},\mathcal{Q}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0\ .}

By taking the image of cohomology with compact supports and applying (
7.9
), we obtain an exact sequence

(8.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{i=1}^{s}\left(\Sym^{k}H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{z_{i},K})\right)^{\Gamma_{z_{i}}}\longrightarrow H_{\mathrm{cusp}}^{1}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K},\mathcal{Q}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})\longrightarrow H_{\mathrm{cusp}}^{1}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})\longrightarrow 0\ .}

Furthermore, by the Manin–Drinfeld theorem, one can also deduce another exact sequence which will play a limited role in this paper but which is of significant arithmetic interest:

(8.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{i=1}^{s}\left(\Sym^{k}H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{z_{i},K})\right)^{\Gamma_{z_{i}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\eis}^{1}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K},\mathcal{Q}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(\mathcal{Z}_{\Gamma,K})(-k-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0\ .}

By Theorem
7.2
, in the simple case when there are no punctures,
the periods of these extensions include generalised Eichler integrals

(8.3)

(2\pi i)^{k+1}\,\int_{\delta}f(z)(X-zY)^{k}dz

where
\delta
is a path in
\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K}(\mathbb{C})
whose endpoints are contained in
\mathcal{Q}_{\Gamma,K}
, and where
f
is a cuspidal weakly holomorphic modular form with
K
-rational Fourier coefficients in the first case (
8.1
), and an Eisenstein series in the second
(
8.2
).

Remark 8.1
.

The single-valued periods of the extensions (
8.1
) and (
8.2
) are given by the single-valued versions of the integrals (
8.3
). In the former case, these include the values at
z_{i}
of suitably normalised weak harmonic lifts of cusp forms; in the latter, the values of real-analytic Eisenstein series, which are weak harmonic lifts of holomorphic Eisenstein series. This follows from Lemmas
6.2
and
B.10
.

In particular, let
Q=\Gamma z_{1}
be a single
\Gamma
-orbit corresponding to an elliptic curve
\mathcal{E}_{z_{1}}
with complex multiplication. We may assume for simplicity that
\Gamma_{z_{1}}\subset\{\pm I\}
and
k
is even. By Proposition
6.7
, the extension (
8.2
) becomes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{0\leq 2i\leq k}C^{k-2i}(-i)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\eis}^{1}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K},\mathcal{Q}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(\mathcal{Z}_{\Gamma,K})(-k-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}

The periods of such an extension include the (regularised) Eichler integrals (
8.3
) where
f(z)
is an Eisenstein series and
\delta
a path from the CM point
z_{1}
to a cusp. Their single-valued versions are given by values of real-analytic Eisenstein series at the CM point
z_{1}
. Half of them correspond to regulators which are predicted by Beilinson’s conjecture to be values of
L
-functions of Hecke Grössencharakters. The other half are periods which do not have an interpretation as values of classical
L
-functions.
For example, in level
1
, when the point
z_{1}=i
, these additional periods may be interpreted as values
\xi(2m,2n)
of the double Riemann
\xi
-function studied in
[
Bro19
]
, see
loc. cit.
, Remark 9.8. They can be expressed as rational linear combinations of ‘double quadratic sums’

Q(k_{1},k_{2})=\sum_{n_{1},n_{2}\geq 1}\frac{1}{(n^{2}_{1}+n^{2}_{2})^{k_{1}}(n_{2}^{2})^{k_{2}}}
for
k_{1},k_{2}\geq 1
(
id.
Definition 9.3).

8.2.
Biextensions

Now consider the fully general situation of Definition
7.8
, where
\mathcal{Q}_{\Gamma,K}
is as above and

\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma,K}=\Gamma w_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\Gamma w_{r}
, with
w_{i}\not\in\Gamma w_{j}
for
i\neq j
.
If
k>0
we have an exact sequence:

(8.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{i=1}^{s}\left(\Sym^{k}\!H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{z_{i},K})\right)^{\Gamma_{z_{i}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\mathrm{cusp}}^{1}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma,K},\mathcal{Q}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\mathrm{cusp}}^{1}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

where the last term sits in a short exact sequence of its own:

(8.5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\mathrm{cusp}}^{1}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\mathrm{cusp}}^{1}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{j=1}^{r}\left(\Sym^{k}H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{w_{j},K})\right)^{\Gamma_{w_{j}}}(-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

Remark 8.2
.

The periods of the extension (
8.5
) include Eichler-type integrals of meromorphic modular forms with poles along
w_{i}
, along paths between two cusps. There exists a ‘reciprocity formula’ which relates such integrals to the periods of (
8.1
) after identifying
z_{i}
with
w_{i}
. It interchanges the poles of the integrand with the end points of the path of integration and conversely, and follows from the fact that the latter extension is the Poincaré dual of the former. In particular, their period matrices with respect to suitable bases are mutually inverse (up to powers of
2\pi i
). Consequently, Eichler integrals of meromorphic modular forms with poles at
w
are expressible as integrals of Eisenstein series along paths ending at
w
, and conversely. It would interesting to write down explicit formulae for such relations.

To describe the single-valued periods of such extensions we introduce the following.

Definition 8.3
.

With the notation (
4.13
), define a
(k+1)\times(k+1)
matrix:

(8.6)

\mathcal{G}_{\Gamma,k}(z,w)=\begin{pmatrix}{}^{\Gamma}\!G^{k,0}_{0,k}(z,w)&\cdots&{}^{\Gamma}\!G^{0,k}_{0,k}(z,w)\\
\vdots&\reflectbox{$\ddots$}&\vdots\\
{}^{\Gamma}\!G^{k,0}_{k,0}(z,w)&\cdots&{}^{\Gamma}\!G^{0,k}_{k,0}(z,w)\\
\end{pmatrix}

whose entries are
(\mathcal{G}_{\Gamma,k}(z,w))_{r,s}={}^{\Gamma}\!G^{k-s+1,s-1}_{r-1,k-r+1}(z,w)
for
1\leq r,s\leq k+1
.

Since

\overline{{}^{\Gamma}\!G^{p,q}_{r,s}(z,w)}={}^{\Gamma}\!G^{q,p}_{s,r}(z,w)
, the complex conjugate matrix has entries

\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{\Gamma,k}(z,w)_{r,s}={}^{\Gamma}\!G^{s-1,k-s+1}_{k-r+1,r-1}(z,w)
for
1\leq r,s\leq k+1
.

8.2.1.
The case in which
H_{\mathrm{cusp}}^{1}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})
vanishes

Suppose that
H_{\mathrm{cusp}}^{1}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})=0
, and let
P=\Gamma w_{1}
and
Q=\Gamma z_{1}
consist of a single orbit. Then the biextension (
8.4
) reduces to a simple extension:

(8.7)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\big(\Sym^{k}H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{z_{1},K})\big)^{\Gamma_{z_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\mathrm{cusp}}^{1}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma,K},\mathcal{Q}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\big(\Sym^{k}H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{w_{1},K})\big)^{\Gamma_{w_{1}}}(-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

Theorem 8.4
.

Let
M_{z_{1},w_{1}}=H_{\mathrm{cusp}}^{1}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma,K},\mathcal{Q}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})
with
P=\Gamma w_{1}
and
Q=\Gamma z_{1}
as above, and assume that neither
z_{1}
nor
w_{1}
is elliptic. Then we have an extension

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sym^{k}H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{z_{1},K})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{z_{1},w_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sym^{k}H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{w_{1},K})(-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

in the category
\mathcal{H}_{K}
. A basis for
(M_{z_{1},w_{1}})_{\mathrm{dR}}\otimes_{K}\mathbb{C}

is given by relative cohomology classes

[(0,\mathrm{comp}_{\mathrm{B},\mathrm{dR}}^{-1}\,(X-z_{1}Y)^{i}(X-\overline{z}_{1}Y)^{k-i})]_{i=0,\ldots,k}\quad,\quad[(\Psi_{\Gamma}^{k-r,r}(z,w_{1})(X-zY)^{k}dz,0)]_{r=0,\ldots,k}

In order to compute the action of
\mathrm{comp}_{\mathrm{B},\mathrm{dR}}^{-1}
on the left-hand elements, use (
6.9
). The single-valued period map
\sf{s}
is given with respect to this basis by the matrix

\begin{pmatrix}J&-\mathcal{G}_{\Gamma,k}(z_{1},w_{1})\\
0&-J\end{pmatrix}

where
J_{r,s}=\delta_{r,k+1-s}
is the
(k+1)\times(k+1)
square matrix with
1
’s along the antidiagonal and zeros elsewhere, and
\mathcal{G}_{\Gamma,k}
is defined in (
8.6
).

Proof.

Since
H^{1}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})
vanishes, the residue map

H^{1}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})\longrightarrow\Big(\Sym^{k}H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{w_{1},K})\Big)^{\Gamma_{w_{1}}}(-1)

is an isomorphism, and it follows from Corollaries
3.11
and
5.16

that the classes of the vector-valued forms
\Psi_{\Gamma}^{k-r,r}(z,w_{1})(X-zY)^{k}dz
, for
r=0,\ldots,k
, form a basis for
H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR},\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{dR},K}_{k})\otimes_{K}\mathbb{C}
which is adapted to the Hodge filtration. The statement about the basis of relative cohomology then follows from the description of the mapping cone complex §
A.1
.

We compute the single-valued period matrix by first computing the Betti-conjugation period matrix and applying Lemma
6.2
. By Definition
6.1
, the map
c_{\iota}
is just the Betti complex-conjugation
c_{\mathrm{B}}
transported to de Rham cohomology. Note that
c_{\mathrm{B}}
acts on the classes
(X-z_{1}Y)^{i}(X-\overline{z}_{1}Y)^{j}
by interchanging
i
and
j
, which accounts for the matrix
J
in the top-left block and the zero block beneath it. Equation (
4.14
) implies that
\vec{G}_{\Gamma,k}^{p,q}(z,w_{1})=\sum_{r+s=k}{}^{\Gamma}\!G^{p,q}_{r,s}(z,w_{1})(X-zY)^{r}(X-\overline{z}Y)^{s}

is a harmonic lift of the pair
\Psi_{\Gamma}^{p,q}(z,w_{1})(X-zY)^{k}dz
and
-\Psi_{\Gamma}^{q,p}(z,w_{1})(X-zY)^{k}dz
. Applying Lemma
B.10
implies that

c_{\iota}[(-\Psi_{\Gamma}^{q,p}(z,w_{1})(X-zY)^{k}dz,0)]=[(\Psi_{\Gamma}^{p,q}(z,w_{1})(X-zY)^{k}dz,0)]+[(0,\vec{G}_{\Gamma,k}^{p,q}(z_{1},w_{1}))],

which yields the remaining entries of the Betti-conjugation period matrix. The single-valued period matrix is its complex-conjugate. Conclude using the fact that
\overline{{}^{\Gamma}\!G^{p,q}_{r,s}}={}^{\Gamma}\!G^{q,p}_{s,r}
.
∎

Let us now assume that
\mathcal{E}_{w_{1},K}
has complex multiplication with respect to an order in a quadratic imaginary field
F\subset K
and assume that
k=2m
is even. Applying Proposition
6.7
we have

(8.8)

\Sym^{k}H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{w_{1},K})=C^{2m}\oplus C^{2m-2}(-1)\oplus\cdots\oplus C^{2}(-m+1)\oplus\mathbb{Q}(-m).

Lemma 8.5
.

Let
w_{1}
be a CM point. Then, in the decomposition (
8.8
), the Tate piece is invariant:

\mathbb{Q}(-m)\ \subset\ \left(\Sym^{2m}H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{w_{1},K})\right)^{\Gamma_{w_{1}}}\ .

Proof.

We use the notation of Remark
6.9
. Since
\Gamma_{w_{1}}
acts via automorphisms of
\mathcal{E}_{w_{1}}
, it preserves the polarisation. It follows that
M_{+}\otimes M_{-}=\bigwedge^{2}(H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{w_{1},K})\otimes F)=\mathbb{Q}(-1)
is
\Gamma_{w_{1}}
-invariant in the category
\mathcal{H}_{K}\otimes F
. It follows that all powers
(M_{+}\otimes M_{-})^{m}\subset\Sym^{2m}(H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{w_{1},K})\otimes F)
are
\Gamma_{w_{1}}
-invariant. In particular, the copy of
\mathbb{Q}(-m)
inside
\Sym^{2m}H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{w_{1},K})
in the category
\mathcal{H}_{K}
is
\Gamma_{w_{1}}
-invariant.
∎

Theorem 8.6
.

Suppose that
w_{1}
and
z_{1}
are CM. Then we can extract from (
8.7
) an extension

(8.9)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}(-m)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{GZ}_{z_{1},w_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}(-m-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

in the category
\mathcal{H}_{K}
. A de Rham basis for
(\mathcal{GZ}_{z_{1},w_{1}})_{\mathrm{dR}}\otimes_{K}\mathbb{C}
is given by the relative cohomology classes

[(0,\mathrm{comp}_{\mathrm{B},\mathrm{dR}}^{-1}(X-z_{1}Y)^{m}(X-\overline{z}_{1}Y)^{m}]\ ,\quad[(\Psi_{\Gamma}^{m,m}(z,w_{1})(X-zY)^{k}dz,0)]

The single-valued map
\mathsf{s}
with respect to this basis is given by the
2\times 2
matrix

\begin{pmatrix}1&-{}^{\Gamma}\!G^{m,m}_{m,m}(z_{1},w_{1})\\
&-1\end{pmatrix}.

Proof.

By Lemma
8.5
, we may pull back (
8.7
) along
\mathbb{Q}(-m-1)\subset\left(\Sym^{2m}H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{w_{1},K})\right)^{\Gamma_{w_{1}}}(-1)
to deduce an extension

(8.10)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\Sym^{k}H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{z_{1},K})\right)^{\Gamma_{z_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}(-m-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}

Since
z_{1}
is also CM, we apply a similar decomposition for
\Sym^{2m}H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{z_{1},K})
and push out along the quotient
\mathbb{Q}(-m)
of
\left(\Sym^{2m}H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{z_{1},K})\right)^{\Gamma_{z_{1}}}
, yielding the extension (
8.9
).

It follows from the proof of Proposition
6.7
that the action of complex multiplication on the de Rham realisation of
\Sym^{2m}H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{z_{1},K})
splits the Hodge filtration. The Tate object inside it corresponds to the part of Hodge type
(m,m)
which is spanned, over
\mathbb{C}
, by the image of
(X-z_{1}Y)^{m}(X-\overline{z}_{1}Y)^{m}
by Remark
6.6
. The same argument applied to
\Sym^{2m}H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{w_{1},K})(-1)
and the calculation of Theorem
8.4
, proves that
[(\Psi_{\Gamma}^{m,m}(z,w_{1})(X-zY)^{2m}dz,0)]
spans the de Rham cohomology, over
\mathbb{C}
, of the Tate piece
\mathbb{Q}(-m-1)
of
\Sym^{2m}H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{w_{1},K})(-1)
. This proves that a de Rham basis of
(\mathcal{GZ}_{z_{1},w_{1}})_{\mathrm{dR}}\otimes_{K}\mathbb{C}
is as stated. The computation of the
single-valued period matrix follows from Theorem
8.4
.
∎

8.3.
Reminders on Hecke correspondences

By a
Hecke correspondence
on
\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K}
, we mean a correspondence
T_{\alpha}
given by a diagram of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma\cap\alpha^{-1}\Gamma\alpha,K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{2}}

where
\alpha\in M_{2\times 2}(\mathbb{Z})
has positive determinant and
q_{1}
(resp.
q_{2}
) is induced on the uniformisation by
z\mapsto z
(resp.
z\mapsto\alpha z
). Such a correspondence fits into a commutative diagram (
cf.

[
Del71
, 3.16]
)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{1}^{*}\mathcal{E}_{\Gamma,K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{2}^{*}\mathcal{E}_{\Gamma,K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}_{\Gamma,K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma\cap\alpha^{-1}\Gamma\alpha,K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}_{\Gamma,K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{2}}

where the action of
\varphi_{\alpha}
on the fibres
\mathcal{E}_{z}\to\mathcal{E}_{\alpha z}
is induced by
u\mapsto\frac{\det(\alpha)}{j_{\alpha}(z)}u
, where
u\in\mathbb{C}
(
cf.
computations in §
5.1
). In particular,
T_{\alpha}
acts via
(q_{1})_{*}\circ q_{2}^{*}
