on the modular object
H^{1}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma};\mathcal{V}_{k})
of
\mathcal{H}_{K}
. More generally,
T_{\alpha}
induces a morphism

T_{\alpha}:H^{1}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma,K},\mathcal{Q}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})\longrightarrow H^{1}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K}\setminus\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{\Gamma,K},\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})

in
\mathcal{H}_{K}
, which is compatible with the residue and relative cohomology long exact sequences; here,
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{\Gamma,K}=T_{\alpha}^{-1}\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma,K}=q_{1}q_{2}^{-1}\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma,K}
and similarly for
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}_{\Gamma,K}
. Note that this means that
T_{\alpha}
is also compatible with all the structures implicit in the definition of
\mathcal{H}_{K}
, such as realisation functors, comparison and single-valued maps, and the Hodge and weight filtrations.

By representing de Rham cohomology classes by modular forms as in Theorem
7.2
(or Proposition
5.5
), we obtain the usual formula for the ‘Hecke operator’: if
f(z)
is modular of weight
k+2
, then

T_{\alpha}f(z)=\det(\alpha)^{k+1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{j_{\beta_{i}}^{k+2}(z)}f(\beta_{i}z),

where
\beta_{i}
are chosen to satisfy
\Gamma\alpha\Gamma=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{n}\Gamma\beta_{i}=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{n}\beta_{i}\Gamma
. If
f(z,w)
is modular in two variables, we shall always consider the action on
f
as a function of the first variable
z
.

Lemma 8.7
.

For every
\delta\in\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Q})
, (
3.14
) satisfies
\kappa_{\delta^{-1}\Gamma\delta,\delta^{-1}w}^{p,q}=\kappa_{\Gamma,w}^{p,q}
.

Proof.

Since
(\delta^{-1}\Gamma\delta)_{\delta^{-1}w}=\delta^{-1}\Gamma_{w}\delta
, we can write

\kappa_{\delta^{-1}\Gamma\delta,\delta^{-1}w}^{p,q}=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{w}}\frac{1}{j_{\delta^{-1}\gamma\delta}^{p}(\delta^{-1}w)j_{\delta^{-1}\gamma\delta}^{q}(\delta^{-1}\overline{w})}.

From the cocycle relations, we have on the one hand
j_{\delta^{-1}\gamma}(w)=j_{\delta^{-1}\gamma\delta\delta^{-1}}(w)=j_{\delta^{-1}\gamma\delta}(\delta^{-1}w)j_{\delta^{-1}}(w)
, and on the other hand
j_{\delta^{-1}\gamma}(w)=j_{\delta^{-1}}(\gamma w)j_{\gamma}(w)=j_{\delta^{-1}}(w)j_{\gamma}(w)
, where the last equality comes from the fact that
\gamma
stabilizes
w
. Comparing the two expressions for
j_{\delta^{-1}\gamma}(w)
proves that
j_{\delta^{-1}\gamma\delta}(\delta^{-1}w)=j_{\gamma}(w)
, and analogously for
\overline{w}
in the place of
w
, from which the statement follows.
∎

Example 8.8
.

Given
p,q\geq 0
such that
p+q=k
, it follows from part (3) of Proposition
3.3
that

T_{\alpha}\Psi_{\Gamma}^{p,q}(z,w)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{j_{\beta^{-1}_{i}}^{p}(w)j_{\beta^{-1}_{i}}^{q}(\overline{w})}\Psi_{\beta_{i}^{-1}\Gamma\beta_{i}}^{p,q}(z,\beta_{i}^{-1}w).

By Lemma
8.7
, (
3.14
) satisfies
\kappa_{\beta_{i}^{-1}\Gamma\beta_{i},\beta_{i}^{-1}w}^{p,q}=\kappa_{\Gamma,w}^{p,q}
. In particular, it follows from Corollary
3.11
that, for every
i=1,\ldots,n
, we have

(8.11)

\displaystyle\Res\left(T_{\alpha}\Psi_{\Gamma}^{p,q}(z,w)(X-zY)^{k}dz\right)=

\displaystyle\frac{(-1)^{p}\kappa_{\Gamma,w}^{p,q}\det(\alpha)^{k}}{(w-\overline{w})^{k}}\binom{k}{p}\sum_{i=1}^{n}j_{\beta^{-1}_{i}}^{q}(w)j_{\beta^{-1}_{i}}^{p}(\overline{w})(X-\beta_{i}^{-1}wY)^{q}(X-\beta_{i}^{-1}\overline{w}Y)^{p}.

If
p=q
, the coefficients in this expression are real. If, furthermore,
w
is CM, then they are rational.

The above example expresses, in the de Rham realisation, the commutativity of the diagram with exact rows in
\mathcal{H}_{K}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})(-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K}\setminus\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})(-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res}

in the case when
\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma,K}=\Gamma w
. Indeed, in this case,
\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma,K}^{\prime}
is given by the union of the orbits of
\beta_{i}^{-1}w
, the residue (
8.11
) lies in
H^{0}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}^{\prime};\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}})
, the
i
th term in the sum being an element of
\Sym^{k}H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{E}_{\beta_{i}^{-1}w}/\mathbb{C})
, and one can check that the vertical arrow on the right is given explicitly by

(8.12)

(X-wY)^{r}(X-\overline{w}Y)^{s}\longmapsto{\det}(\alpha)^{k}\sum_{i=1}^{n}j_{\beta_{i}^{-1}}^{r}(w)j_{\beta_{i}^{-1}}^{s}(\overline{w})(X-\beta_{i}^{-1}wY)^{r}(X-\beta_{i}^{-1}\overline{w}Y)^{s}.

8.4.
The general case

Using Hecke correspondences, we can extend the results of §
8.2
to the fully general case when the cuspidal cohomology
H^{1}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})
does not vanish. Let
T
be a
\mathbb{Z}
-linear combination of Hecke correspondences on
\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K}
of the form
T_{\alpha}
, as above.

Lemma 8.9
.

Let
w_{1}\in\mathfrak{H}
and
r,s\geq 0
with
r+s=k>0
. If

(8.13)

\Res\left(T\Psi^{r,s}_{\Gamma}(z,w_{1})(X-zY)^{k}dz\right)=0,

then

T\,{}^{\Gamma}\!G_{r,s}^{p,q}(z,w_{1})\equiv 0

for every
p,q\geq 0
with
p+q=k
.

Proof.

By applying the residue sequence of Proposition
5.12
and Proposition
3.3
, one sees that equation (
8.13
) implies that
T\Psi^{r,s}_{\Gamma}(z,w_{1})

is a (holomorphic) cusp form for
\Gamma
. It therefore defines a class in the pure Hodge structure
H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR},\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma};\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}})
of weight
k+1
. By (
5.12
) it lies in

F^{s+1}\cap\overline{F}^{r+1}\cap W_{k+1}=0
and so its cohomology class vanishes.
By Betti complex-conjugating the right-hand side of (
8.11
), we see that (
8.13
) implies the same equation with
r
and
s
interchanged. Therefore the cohomology class of
T\Psi^{s,r}_{\Gamma}(z,w_{1})(X-zY)^{k}dz
vanishes by the same argument.
Then, since the space of cusp forms injects into cohomology by Lemma
5.11
, we obtain

T\Psi^{r,s}_{\Gamma}(z,w_{1})\equiv 0\equiv T\Psi^{s,r}_{\Gamma}(z,w_{1}).

By Corollary
4.8
, the higher Green’s functions in the statement are the components of a harmonic lift of the pair
(T\Psi^{r,s}_{\Gamma}(z,w_{1}),-T\Psi^{s,r}_{\Gamma}(z,w_{1}))
. We conclude by applying the uniqueness statement of Lemma
2.5
.
∎

From now on, we assume that the induced endomorphism on cuspidal cohomology

(8.14)

T\in\operatorname{End}(H^{1}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k}))\hbox{ is the zero map.}

Note that, when there are no cusp forms (so that
H^{1}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})=0
in
\mathcal{H}_{K}
), one may take
T
to be the identity. In particular, the remaining results of this section generalise those of §
8.2
.

Remark 8.10
.

In the literature, when
\Gamma=\Gamma_{0}(N)
is a Hecke congruence subgroup, an operator satisfying the above condition (
8.14
) is usually constructed by taking
T=\sum_{n\geq 0}\lambda_{n}T_{n}
, where
T_{n}
are the classical
n
th Hecke operators, and
(\lambda_{n})_{n\geq 0}
is a relation in the space of cusp forms
S_{k+2,\Gamma}
(see
[
GZ86
, Section V.4, p. 316]
). Such a relation may be obtained more concretely in terms of the principal part of a weakly holomorphic modular form of weight
-k
(
cf
.
[
Via15
, Proposition 1.1]
and
[
BLY25
, Remark 2.5]
).

For simplicity, let us first consider the case where
P,Q
are orbits of a single non-elliptic element.

Theorem 8.11
.

Let
P=\Gamma w_{1}
and
Q=\Gamma z_{1}
, with
w_{1}
and
z_{1}
not elliptic, and
T
be as above satisfying (
8.14
).
We construct an extension in the category
\mathcal{H}_{K}
of the form:

(8.15)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sym^{k}H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{z_{1},K})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{T}_{z_{1},w_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sym^{k}H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{w_{1},K})(-1)\longrightarrow 0\ .}

If none of the columns of the matrix
T\mathcal{G}_{\Gamma,k}(z,w_{1})
vanishes identically, then the extension

M^{T}_{z_{1},w_{1}}
is a subobject of
H^{1}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K}\setminus T^{-1}\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma,K},\mathcal{Q}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})
and a de Rham basis for
(M^{T}_{z_{1},w_{1}})_{\mathrm{dR}}\otimes_{K}\mathbb{C}
is given by

[(0,\mathrm{comp}_{\mathrm{B},\mathrm{dR}}^{-1}(X-z_{1}Y)^{i}(X-\overline{z}_{1}Y)^{k-i})]_{i=0,\ldots,k}\ ,\quad[(T\Psi_{\Gamma}^{k-r,r}(z,w_{1})(X-zY)^{k}dz,0)]_{r=0,\ldots,k}.

With notation as above, the single-valued map
\mathsf{s}
with respect to this basis is given by:

\begin{pmatrix}J&-T\mathcal{G}_{\Gamma,k}(z_{1},w_{1})\\
0&-J\end{pmatrix}\ .

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{\Gamma,K}=T^{-1}\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma,K}
. The action of
T
defines a morphism in
\mathcal{H}_{K}
which we also denote by:

(8.16)

T:H^{1}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})\longrightarrow H^{1}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K}\setminus\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k}).

We have a commutative diagram with exact rows

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sym^{k}H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{w_{1},K})(-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K}\setminus\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})(-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
(
8.16
)

where the vertical maps are given by the action of
T
and the rows are residue short exact sequences (
8.5
). Since the vertical map on the left is zero by hypothesis (
8.14
), the map (
8.16
) factors through

(8.17)

T:\Sym^{k}H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{w_{1},K})(-1)\longrightarrow H^{1}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K}\setminus\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k}).

Now consider the sequence (
8.4
):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sym^{k}H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{z_{1},K})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\mathrm{cusp}}^{1}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K}\setminus\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{\Gamma,K},\mathcal{Q}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\mathrm{cusp}}^{1}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K}\setminus\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}

By pulling it back along (
8.17
) we deduce an extension

\hbox to346.4pt{\vbox to18.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 173.19719pt\lower-9.33528pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope {

{{{{{}}{

{{{{{}}{

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\ .

We claim that, under the assumptions of the theorem, the map (
8.17
) is injective. In fact, the induced map
\overline{T}:\Sym^{k}H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{w_{1},K})(-1)\rightarrow H^{0}(\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})(-1)

is
a fortiori
injective. To see this, observe that
\overline{T}
induces a morphism of pure Hodge structures of weight
k+2
and hence preserves the Hodge decomposition. Since the
(s+1,r+1)
component
F^{s+1}\cap\overline{F}^{r+1}
of
\Sym^{k}H_{\mathrm{dR}}^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{w_{1},K})(-1)
is one-dimensional, generated by the residue of
\Psi^{r,s}(z,w)(X-zY)^{k}dz
(Corollary
5.16
), it suffices to check that the residue of
T\Psi^{r,s}(z,w)(X-zY)^{k}dz
is non-zero for every
r+s=k
. Therefore, injectivity of (
8.17
) follows from the assumption on
T\mathcal{G}_{\Gamma,k}(z,w_{1})
and Lemma
8.9
. It follows from this that
M^{T}_{z_{1},w_{1}}
is the subobject of
H_{\mathrm{cusp}}^{1}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K}\setminus\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{\Gamma,K},\mathcal{Q}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})
which maps to the image of
\overline{T}
, which is isomorphic to
\Sym^{k}H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{w_{1},K})(-1)
as established above. By Corollary
5.16
, this shows that
(M^{T}_{z_{1},w_{1}})_{\mathrm{dR}}\otimes_{K}\mathbb{C}
admits a basis as in the statement. Finally, the calculation of the single-valued period matrix proceeds exactly as in Theorem
8.4
.
∎

Remark 8.12
.

The hypothesis on
T\mathcal{G}_{\Gamma,k}(z,w_{1})
in the above theorem is always satisfied if
T\neq 0
and
w_{1}
is not CM. Indeed, in this case, by inspection of the formulae (
8.12
), the injectivity of (
8.17
) ultimately follows from the fact that a point of
\mathfrak{H}
is CM if and only if it is fixed by a matrix in
M_{2\times 2}(\mathbb{Z})
which is not proportional to the identity.

Theorem 8.13
.

Let
P=\Gamma w_{1}
and
Q=\Gamma z_{1}
, with
w_{1}
a CM point and
z_{1}
not elliptic, and let
T
be a combination of Hecke operators as above satisfying (
8.14
).
Then we obtain an extension in
\mathcal{H}_{K}
of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sym^{2m}H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{z_{1},K})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{T}_{z_{1},w_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}(-m-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0\ .}

If the middle column

v(z)\coloneqq(T\,{}^{\Gamma}\!G^{2m,0}_{m,m}(z,w_{1}),T\,{}^{\Gamma}\!G^{2m-1,1}_{m,m}(z,w_{1}),\ldots,T\,{}^{\Gamma}\!G^{0,2m}_{m,m}(z,w_{1}))^{t}

of the matrix
T\mathcal{G}_{\Gamma,k}(z,w_{1})
does not vanish identically as a function of
z
, then the extension

N^{T}_{z_{1},w_{1}}
is a subobject of
H^{1}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K}\setminus T^{-1}\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma,K},\mathcal{Q}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})
, and a de Rham basis for
(N^{T}_{z_{1},w_{1}})_{\mathrm{dR}}\otimes_{K}\mathbb{C}
is given by

[(0,\mathrm{comp}_{\mathrm{B},\mathrm{dR}}^{-1}(X-z_{1}Y)^{i}(X-\overline{z}_{1}Y)^{2m-i})]_{i=0,\ldots,2m}\quad,\quad[(T\Psi_{\Gamma}^{m,m}(z,w_{1})(X-zY)^{2m}dz,0)]\ .

The single-valued map
\mathsf{s}
is represented in this basis by the matrix

\begin{pmatrix}J&-v(z_{1})\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}.

Proof.

We may proceed as in the previous theorem by applying Proposition
6.7
to
\Sym^{k}H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{w_{1},K})(-1)
, and pulling back the resulting extension along its summand
\mathbb{Q}(-m-1)
. It is non-zero by Lemma
8.5
. This yields an extension:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sym^{2m}H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{z_{1},K})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{T}_{z_{1},w_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}(-m-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0\ .}

The rest of the proof proceeds by an application of Lemma
8.9
as before, noting that the copy of
\mathbb{Q}(-m-1)
in
\Sym^{k}H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{w_{1},K})(-1)
corresponds to the component
[\Psi_{\Gamma}^{m,m}(z,w_{1})(X-zY)^{k}dz]
.
∎

Theorem 8.14
.

Assume now that both
z_{1}
and
w_{1}
are CM points, and possibly elliptic. We obtain an extension in
\mathcal{H}_{K}
of the form:

(8.18)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}(-m)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{GZ}^{T}_{z_{1},w_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}(-m-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0\ .}

If the function
T\,{}^{\Gamma}\!G^{m,m}_{m,m}(z,w_{1})
does not vanish identically as a function of
z
, then
\mathcal{GZ}^{T}_{z_{1},w_{1}}
is a subobject of
H^{1}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K}\setminus T^{-1}\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma,K},\mathcal{Q}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})
, and
a basis for
(\mathcal{GZ}^{T}_{z_{1},w_{1}})_{\mathrm{dR}}\otimes_{K}\mathbb{C}
is given by
relative cohomology classes

[(0,\mathrm{comp}_{\mathrm{B},\mathrm{dR}}^{-1}(X-z_{1}Y)^{m}(X-\overline{z}_{1}Y)^{m})]\ ,\quad[(T\Psi_{\Gamma}^{m,m}(z,w_{1})(X-zY)^{k}dz,0)]

with corresponding single-valued period matrix

\begin{pmatrix}1&-T\,{}^{\Gamma}\!G^{m,m}_{m,m}(z_{1},w_{1})\\
&-1\end{pmatrix}.

Proof.

By proceeding as in the previous theorem, we obtain an extension

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\Sym^{2m}H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{z_{1},K})\right)^{\Gamma_{z_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{T}_{z_{1},w_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}(-m-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0\ .}

The extension (
8.18
) is obtained by pushing out the above extension along the projection onto the copy of
\mathbb{Q}(-m)
inside
\big(\Sym^{2m}H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{z_{1},K})\big)^{\Gamma_{z_{1}}}
. Since
the copy of
\mathbb{Q}(-m)
is of Hodge type
(m,m)
and since
[(0,(X-z_{1}Y)^{m}(X-\overline{z}_{1}Y)^{m})]\in F^{m}\cap\overline{F}^{m}
, it is spanned by precisely this class. The statement about the de Rham basis therefore follows from (
5.12
). The period computation follows as for the previous theorems.
∎

9.
Realisations of motives, and conjectures of Beilinson and Gross-Zagier

A standard conjecture, in rough terms, states that every extension in the category
\mathcal{H}_{K}
of
\mathbb{Q}(-1)
by
\mathbb{Q}(0)
which lies in the full subcategory generated by the cohomology of algebraic varieties is necessarily isomorphic to a Kummer extension. We first show, using the framework developed above, that this implies a generalised version of the Gross-Zagier conjecture for modular curves.
We then briefly discuss how our matrix Green’s functions, and their cohomological interpretation, actually leads to a much more general prediction involving special values of
L
-functions, via Beilinson’s conjecture.

9.1.
Gross-Zagier conjecture (the case of two CM points)

For the next statement, we write
a\sim_{\mathbb{Q}^{\times}}b
if there is a non-zero rational number
r
such that
a=rb
.

Theorem 9.1
.

With notation as in Theorem
8.14
, assume that there exists a Kummer object
\mathcal{K}_{x}
in
\mathcal{H}_{K}
(
cf.
§
6.2.2
) such that the Gross-Zagier extension
\mathcal{GZ}_{z_{1},w_{1}}^{T}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{K}_{x}(-m)
. Then, either

(9.1)

T\,{}^{\Gamma}\!G_{m,m}^{m,m}(z_{1},w_{1})\sim_{\mathbb{Q}^{\times}}\log|x|\,

or
T\,{}^{\Gamma}\!G_{m,m}^{m,m}(z_{1},w_{1})=0
, in which case
T\,{}^{\Gamma}\!G_{m,m}^{m,m}(z,w_{1})
vanishes identically as a function of
z
. In particular, if
d_{z_{1}}
(resp.
d_{w_{1}}
) denotes the discriminant of the CM point
z_{1}
(resp.
w_{1}
), then (
9.1
) is equivalent to

TG_{\Gamma,m+1}(z_{1},w_{1})\sim_{\mathbb{Q}^{\times}}(d_{z_{1}}d_{w_{1}})^{m/2}\log|x|.

Proof.

We may assume that
T\,{}^{\Gamma}\!G_{m,m}^{m,m}(z,w_{1})
is not identically zero.
We must compare the single-valued period matrix of
\mathcal{K}_{x}(-m)
in the basis
w_{0}=[\textstyle{\frac{dz}{x-1}}](-m),w_{1}=[\textstyle{\frac{dz}{z}}](-m)
(note the Tate twist), namely

\begin{pmatrix}(-1)^{m}&(-1)^{m}\log|x|^{2}\\
0&-(-1)^{m}\end{pmatrix},

with the matrix in Corollary
8.14
. Consider the
K
-basis of
(\mathcal{GZ}_{z_{1},w_{1}}^{T})_{\mathrm{dR}}
:

v_{0}=[(0,(2\pi i)^{m}\mathrm{comp}_{\mathrm{B},\mathrm{dR}}^{-1}(X-z_{1}Y)^{m}(X-\overline{z}_{1}Y)^{m}]\ ,\quad v_{1}=[((2\pi i)^{m}T\Psi_{\Gamma}^{m,m}(z,w_{1})(X-zY)^{2m}dz,0)].

The fact that
v_{0}
is de Rham
K
-rational follows from Lemmas
6.3
and
6.4
(
cf.
Remark
6.6
). For
v_{1}
, this follows from the fact that
T
annihilates cusp forms, together with Proposition
7.5
, which implies that

(2\pi i)^{m}T\Psi_{\Gamma}^{m,m}(z,w_{1})(X-zY)^{2m}dz=\binom{2m}{m}\frac{(2\pi i)^{2m+1}}{(w_{1}-\overline{w}_{1})^{m}}Tg^{m,m}(z)(X-zY)^{2m}dz,

where
g^{m,m}
is a meromorphic modular form with Fourier coefficients defined over
K
(
cf.
Theorem
7.2
).

By construction,
v_{0}
lies in the image of
\mathbb{Q}(-m)_{\mathrm{dR}}\to(\mathcal{GZ}_{z_{1},w_{1}}^{T})_{\mathrm{dR}}
in the extension (
8.18
), which coincides with
W_{2m}(\mathcal{GZ}_{z_{1},w_{1}}^{T})_{\mathrm{dR}}
. Moreover, since
(X-z_{1}Y)^{m}(X-\overline{z}_{1}Y)^{m}
is Betti
\mathbb{Q}
-rational in
\Sym^{k}H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{z_{1},K})
(since
X,Y
are
\mathbb{Q}
-rational by definition,
z_{1}
is quadratic imaginary by assumption, and the whole expression is
\operatorname{Gal}(\mathbb{Q}(z_{1})/\mathbb{Q})
-invariant), we conclude that

\mathrm{comp}_{\mathrm{B},\mathrm{dR}}(v_{0})\in(2\pi i)^{m}W_{2m}(\mathcal{GZ}_{z_{1},w_{1}}^{T})_{\mathrm{B}}.

The element
v_{1}
lies in
F^{m+1}(\mathcal{GZ}_{z_{1},w_{1}}^{T})_{\mathrm{dR}}
, which splits the de Rham realisation of the extension (
8.18
). Its image
\overline{v}_{1}
in the quotient
(\mathcal{GZ}_{z_{1},w_{1}}^{T})_{\mathrm{dR}}/W_{2m}(\mathcal{GZ}_{z_{1},w_{1}}^{T})_{\mathrm{dR}}\cong\mathbb{Q}(-m-1)_{\mathrm{dR}}
equals

(2\pi i)^{m}\,\mathrm{Res}\left(T\Psi_{\Gamma}^{m,m}(z,w_{1})(X-zY)^{2m}dz\right)(-1).

Then, it follows from formula (
8.11
) and the comment immediately afterwards, that

\mathrm{comp}_{\mathrm{B},\mathrm{dR}}(\overline{v}_{1})\in(2\pi i)^{m+1}(\mathcal{GZ}_{z_{1},w_{1}}^{T})_{\mathrm{B}}/W_{2m}(\mathcal{GZ}_{z_{1},w_{1}}^{T})_{\mathrm{B}}.

All of the above properties imply that, under an isomorphism
\mathcal{GZ}_{z_{1},w_{1}}^{T}\cong\mathcal{K}_{x}(-m)
in
\mathcal{H}_{K}
, there are non-zero rational numbers
\lambda,\mu
such that
v_{0}=\lambda w_{0}
and
v_{1}=\mu w_{1}
, where
w_{0},w_{1}
are the de Rham basis of
\mathcal{K}_{x}(-m)
defined above. Since the basis
v_{0},v_{1}
differs from the
\mathbb{C}
-basis in Theorem
8.14
only by a factor of
(2\pi i)^{m}
, the single-valued period matrix with respect to
v_{0},v_{1}
is

\begin{pmatrix}(-1)^{m}&-(-1)^{m}T\,{}^{\Gamma}\!G^{m,m}_{m,m}(z_{1},w_{1})\\
&-(-1)^{m}\end{pmatrix},

(recall from §
6.2.1
that the single-valued version of
2\pi i
is
-1
) and we conclude that

T\,{}^{\Gamma}\!G^{m,m}_{m,m}(z_{1},w_{1})=-\frac{\mu}{\lambda}\log|x|^{2}=-2\frac{\mu}{\lambda}\log|x|.

The last assertion comes from the formula (
4.28
)

{}^{\Gamma}\!G^{m,m}_{m,m}(z,w)=(-1)^{m}\binom{2m}{m}\frac{1}{(z-\overline{z})^{m}(w-\overline{w})^{m}}G_{\Gamma,m+1}(z,w)

and from the fact that
\sqrt{d_{z_{1}}}\sim_{\mathbb{Q}^{\times}}(z_{1}-\overline{z}_{1})
and likewise for
w_{1}
.
∎

Remark 9.2
.

When the modular curve
\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}
is defined over
\mathbb{Q}
, such as in the case
\Gamma=\Gamma_{0}(N)
, the number field
K
in the above statement can be taken to be
\mathbb{Q}(z_{1},w_{1},j(z_{1}),j(w_{1}))
, which, by the classical theory of complex multiplication, is the compositum of the Hilbert class fields of the imaginary quadratic fields
\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{d_{z_{1}}})
and
\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{d_{w_{1}}})
. In particular, the above statement is equivalent to that of
[
Li23
, Theorem 1.1]
.

9.2.
Beyond the Gross-Zagier conjecture

The extension (
8.15
), and its variants with
z,w
CM points, produces a variety of different types of simple extension in the category
\mathcal{H}_{K}
, which are of geometric origin. A version of Beilinson’s conjecture predicts that their periods should be related to special values of
L
-functions.
This suggests a range of new conjectures about the values of vector-valued Green’s functions which goes far beyond the original conjecture of Gross and Zagier, which concerns the very special case of Kummer extensions. A more detailed study of such extensions will be postponed to a future paper, but we briefly give here an appetiser for the types of extensions which may occur.

Since
H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{w,K})
is self-dual (
i.e.
,
H^{\vee}=H(1)
) the sequence (
8.15
) is equivalent to an extension

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{z,w}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}(0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0\,,}

where we write
z=z_{1},w=w_{1}
for simplicity and

M_{z,w}=\Big(\Sym^{k}H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{z,K})\otimes\Sym^{k}H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{w,K})\Big)(k+1)

which has weight
-2
. The corresponding
L
-function
L(M_{z,w},s)
, which is a Rankin-Selberg convolution of two symmetric powers of motives of elliptic curves, can have a pole at
s=0
of order
\dim\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{H}_{K}}(\mathbb{Q}(1),M_{z,w})
which is non-zero if and only if
z,w
are both CM by Proposition
6.7
.
An interesting example is the case when
k=1
and
M_{z,w}
is defined over
\mathbb{Q}
. Then we expect a relation between the four entries of the
2\times 2
matrix-valued Green’s function
\mathcal{G}_{\Gamma,1}(z,w)
defined in Appendix
C
, the periods of
H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{z,K})
and
H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{w,K})
and their conjugates, and the special value of the Rankin-Selberg
L
-function
L(H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{z,K})\otimes H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{w,K}),2)
.

Another case of interest is when
k=2m
is even,
w
has CM, and
z
does not. Then the same proposition and the previous theorems enable us to cut out an extension of the form

\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{K}}(\mathbb{Q}(0),M^{\prime}_{z,w})\quad\hbox{ where }\quad M^{\prime}_{z,w}=\left(\Sym^{2m}H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{z,K})\right)(m+1)

whose periods are related to the symmetric power
L
-value
L(\mathrm{Sym}^{2m}H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{z}),m+1)
.
An interesting case is when
M^{\prime}_{z,w}
is defined over a quadratic imaginary extension
K
of
\mathbb{Q}
, in which case the corresponding Deligne cohomology group has rank one, and Beilinson’s conjecture predicts a relation between the values of our higher Green’s functions and

L(\mathrm{Sym}^{2m}H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{z,K}),m+1)
. For example, when
m=1
, we expect a relation between the three entries (
{}^{\Gamma}\!G^{1,1}_{i,j}
for
(i,j)=(0,2),(1,1),(2,0)
) in the central column of our higher Green’s function matrix
\mathcal{G}_{\Gamma,2}(z,w)
(see Appendix
C
), the periods of
H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{z,K})
, and the
L
-value
L(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}H^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{z,K}),2).

More generally, Beilinson’s conjecture predicts that the space of motivic extensions has a precise finite rank, which implies the existence of relations between our higher Green’s functions for fixed
z
and varying
w
a CM point, and also for varying
\Gamma
. An interesting question, which we plan to investigate in a future paper, is whether we can construct all such predicted extensions via the constructions (
8.15
).

Remark 9.3
.

The discussion above suggests that the vector-valued Green’s functions are universal for expressing certain specific values of
L
-functions of symmetric powers of motives of elliptic curves. This is very much in the spirit of Zagier’s conjecture, which states, for example, that the classical polylogarithm functions are the universal functions for expressing the values of Dedekind zeta functions of number fields.

Note that in (
8.2
) we also considered extensions whose periods are Eichler integrals of meromorphic modular forms. These produce interesting extensions of
\mathbb{Q}
by motives of arbitrary weights
\leq-2
, which should also be related to special values of
L
-functions via Beilinson’s conjecture, and merit further study.

10.
Mixed modular motives via moduli stacks of pointed elliptic curves

We prove that the mixed modular objects
H^{1}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma,K}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma,K},\mathcal{Q}_{\Gamma,K};\mathcal{V}_{k})
constructed in §
7.3
are motivic,
i.e.
, that they arise as realisations of objects in Voevodsky’s triangulated category of motives over the number field
K
. We then explain how well-known and standard conjectures for the motives of cusp forms imply that the ‘Gross-Zagier extensions’ constructed in Theorem
8.14
are also motivic, which immediately implies the Gross-Zagier conjecture.
In Proposition
D.7
, we prove that the motive of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,3}
is mixed Tate, which by our earlier results, gives a complete proof of the Gross-Zagier conjecture in weight
4
and level
1
.

10.1.
Triangulated motives

Given a base field
K
of characteristic zero and a field of coefficients
F
, also of characteristic zero, we denote Voevodsky’s triangulated category of mixed motives over
K
with coefficients in
F
by
\DM(K;F)

[
Voe00
,
Ayo14
]
. When
F=\mathbb{Q}
, we write it simply
\DM(K)
. The category
\DM(K;F)
is triangulated,
F
-linear, tensor, rigid, and pseudo-abelian. There is a canonical functor
\DM(K)\to\DM(K;F)
given by extension of coefficients.

10.1.1.
Relative cohomology motives

We borrow the following notation of
[
DF25
, Appendix A]
: for a pair
(X,Z)
, given by a
K
-variety
X
and a closed subvariety
Z
of
X
, we associate an object
M(X,Z)
of
\DM(K)
called
relative cohomology motive
of the pair
(X,Z)
. If
Z=\emptyset
, we denote it by
M(X)
, and we call it the
motive
of
X
. The relative cohomology motive sits in a natural distinguished triangle

(10.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(Z)[-1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(X,Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(X)}
.
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}

More generally, if there is a decomposition
Z=Z^{\prime}\cup Y
, where both
Z^{\prime}
and
Y
are closed subvarieties of
X
, then there is a distinguished triangle given by ‘partial boundaries’
[
DF25
, Proposition A.5]
:

(10.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(Y,Y\cap Z^{\prime})[-1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(X,Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(X,Z^{\prime})}
.
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}

Remark 10.1
.

Beware that we work with
cohomological
motives, as in
[
DF25
]
, contrary to Voevodsky’s homological convention
[
Voe00
]
. Thus, in this paper, the functor
(X,Z)\mapsto M(X,Z)
is
contravariant
.

Example 10.2

(Tate motives)
.

The trivial and Lefschetz motives in
\DM(K)
are defined by

\mathbb{Q}(0)\coloneqq M(\operatorname{Spec}K),\qquad\mathbb{Q}(-1)\coloneqq M(\mathbb{G}_{m,K},{1})[1].

For
n\geq 0
, we set
\mathbb{Q}(-n)\coloneqq\mathbb{Q}(-1)^{\otimes n}
and
\mathbb{Q}(n)\coloneqq\mathbb{Q}(-n)^{\vee}
. For any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the extension of coefficients of
\mathbb{Q}(n)
to
\DM(K;F)
is denoted by
F(n)
. Given any object
M
of
\DM(K;F)
, we denote
M\otimes F(n)=M(n)
.

Example 10.3

(Kummer motives)
.

For every
x\in K^{\times}
, the corresponding
Kummer motive
is the object of
\DM(K)
given by

K_{x}\coloneqq M(\mathbb{G}_{m,K},\{1,x\})[1].

By taking
Z^{\prime}=\{1\}
and
Y=\{x\}
in (
10.2
), the Kummer motive sits in a distinguished triangle of the form:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}(0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}(-1)}
.
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}

This is the
Kummer extension
.

The triangulated subcategory generated by the Tate motives
\mathbb{Q}(n)
is denoted
\DMT(K;F)
, or
\DMT(K)
when
F=\mathbb{Q}
. If
K
is a number field, then it is known that
\DMT(K)
is equipped with a canonical
t
-structure whose heart is the abelian category of
mixed Tate motives
over
K
, denoted
\MT(K)
(see
[
Lev93
]
). The Kummer motives of the above example are mixed Tate motives. Conversely, the isomorphisms

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\MT(K)}(\mathbb{Q}(-1),\mathbb{Q}(0))\cong\Hom_{\DMT(K)}(\mathbb{Q}(-1),\mathbb{Q}(0)[1])\cong K^{\times}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}

assert that every extension of
\mathbb{Q}(-1)
by
\mathbb{Q}(0)
is a subobject of a finite direct sum of Kummer motives.

More generally, we shall consider motives given as ‘biextensions’, as follows.

Example 10.4

(Biextensions)
.

If
X
is smooth and
A,B\subset X
are smooth components of a normal crossings divisor in
X
, then the relative cohomology motive
M(X\setminus A,B\setminus(A\cap B))
sits in a
biextension
given by the relative cohomology distinguished triangle with respect to
B\setminus(A\cap B)
followed by a Gysin distinguished triangle with respect to
A
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(B\setminus(A\cap B))[-1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(X\setminus A,B\setminus(A\cap B))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(X\setminus A)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(X\setminus A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(A)(-1)[-1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}

Alternatively, one might consider first a Gysin triangle for
M(X\setminus A,B\setminus(A\cap B))
(see
[
DF25
, Propositon A.18]
), followed by a relative cohomology triangle for
M(A,A\cap B)
.

10.1.2.
Motives of smooth Deligne-Mumford stacks

By
[
Cho12
]
, to every smooth separated Deligne-Mumford stack over
K
, we may associate a motive
M(\mathcal{X})
in
\DM(K)
satisfying the following properties (beware, again, that our convention is cohomological):

(1)

The association
\mathcal{X}\mapsto M(\mathcal{X})
is a contravariant functor.

(2)

If
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow X
is the coarse moduli space of
\mathcal{X}
, then
M(X)\cong M(\mathcal{X})
(
[
Cho12
, Theorem 3.3]
).

(3)

If
X
is a smooth quasi-projective scheme with an action of a
finite
group
G
, then the motive of the quotient stack
[X/G]
is isomorphic to the
G
-invariants of
M(X)
(
[
Cho12
, Lemma 3.1]
):

M([X/G])\cong M(X)^{G}.

Remark 10.5
.

By property (3) above, if the motive
M(X)
is mixed Tate, then the motive of the quotient
M([X/G])
is also mixed Tate. The converse is not true; see Remark
D.3
.

If
\mathcal{X}
is a smooth separated Deligne-Mumford stack over
K
, and
\mathcal{Z}
is a smooth closed substack, we may also consider the relative cohomology motive
M(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Z})
, which fits in a distinguished triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(\mathcal{Z})[-1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(\mathcal{X})}
.
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}

If the coarse moduli spaces
X
of
\mathcal{X}
and
Z
of
\mathcal{Z}
are schemes, then
M(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Z})\cong M(X,Z)
, and the above distinguished triangle is isomorphic to (
10.1
). Similarly, if
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}\subset\mathcal{X}
are smooth components of a normal crossings divisor in
\mathcal{X}
, the motive
M(\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}\setminus(\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{B}))
sits in a biextension as in Example
10.4
.

Example 10.6
.

Let
K[x_{0},\ldots,x_{n}]
be the graded polynomial ring where
x_{i}
has degree
d_{i}\geq 1
. Equivalently, we equip
\mathbb{A}^{n+1}=\Spec K[x_{0},\ldots,x_{n}]
with the action of the multiplicative group
\mathbb{G}_{m}
in which
x_{i}
has weight
d_{i}
. Then the
weighted projective stack

\mathcal{P}(d_{0},\ldots,d_{n})\coloneqq\left[\frac{\mathbb{A}^{n+1}\setminus(0,\ldots,0)}{\mathbb{G}_{m}}\right]

is a separated smooth Deligne-Mumford stack. The open substack where
x_{i}\neq 0
is isomorphic to a quotient
[\mathbb{A}^{n}/\mu_{d_{i}}]
(see Lemma
D.1
), and its complement is a weighted projective stack of smaller dimension. The motive
M(\mathcal{P}(d_{0},\ldots,d_{n}))
is mixed Tate.

10.1.3.
Realisation functors

If
K\subset\mathbb{C}
is a number field, there is an
\mathcal{H}
-realisation functor

\DM(K;F)\longrightarrow D(\mathcal{H}_{K}\otimes F),

which is
F
-linear and compatible with the triangulated and tensor structures. The realisation of an object of
\DM(K)
of the form
M(X,Z)
is an object in the derived category of
\mathcal{H}_{K}
which computes the cohomology of the pair
(X,Z)
; by abuse, we shall denote it by
H^{\bullet}(X,Z)
.

10.2.
Moduli stacks of pointed elliptic curves and statement of the main result

For simplicity, we work at level 1, and we refer to
[
Pet12
]
for the necessary modifications in higher levels.

For every integer
n\geq 1
, let
\mathcal{M}_{1,n}
be the smooth Deligne-Mumford stack over
\mathbb{Q}
classifying smooth genus 1 curves with
n
ordered marked points, and let
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,n}
be its Deligne-Mumford compactification, which is a smooth and proper Deligne-Mumford stack over
\mathbb{Q}
classifying stable genus 1 curves with
n
ordered marked points. We denote by

(10.3)

\pi_{n}:\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,n}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,1}

the canonical projection which forgets the last
n-1
marked points and contracts the possible destabilizing genus zero components. In the notation of Section
7
, we have
\mathcal{M}_{1,1}=\mathcal{Y}_{\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}),\mathbb{Q}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,1}=\mathcal{X}_{\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}),\mathbb{Q}}
.

To an elliptic curve
(E,0)
with field of definition
K=\mathbb{Q}(j(E))
, there corresponds a
K
-point
\Spec K\to\mathcal{M}_{1,1}
, which factors through a reduced closed substack
\mathcal{G}_{E}\subset\mathcal{M}_{1,1}
, called its
residue gerbe
, whose underlying space is a single point:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}_{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{1,1}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}

If
\Gamma_{E}
denotes the group of automorphisms of
(E,0)
, then the residue gerbe
\mathcal{G}_{E}
is isomorphic to the classifying stack
B_{K}\Gamma_{E}=[(\Spec K)/\Gamma_{E}]
. The closed substack given by the ‘cusp at infinity’, corresponding to a nodal cubic curve, is denoted
\infty\subset\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,1}
(see also (
D.3
)).

Let us denote by
E^{\prime}=E\setminus\{0\}
the punctured elliptic curve. The fibre of
\pi_{n}
at a
K
-point corresponding to
(E,0)
, as above, is isomorphic to a compactification
\overline{C}_{n-1}(E^{\prime})
of the configuration space

\displaystyle C_{n-1}(E^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\{(x_{2},\ldots,x_{n})\in(E^{\prime})^{n-1}:x_{i}\neq x_{j}\text{ for all }i\neq j\}.

Note that
\Gamma_{E}
acts on
\overline{C}_{n-1}(E^{\prime})
and the fibre of
\pi_{n}
at the residue gerbe
\mathcal{G}_{E}
is the quotient stack

\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,n}\times_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,1}}\mathcal{G}_{E}\cong[\overline{C}_{n-1}(E^{\prime})/\Gamma_{E}].

The symmetric group
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
acts on
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,n}
by permuting the marked points. The open substack
\mathcal{M}_{1,n}\subset\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,n}
is preserved by this action. The fibres of
\pi_{n}
are also preserved by the action of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
. This follows from the fact that, if
(C,p)
is a stable genus 1 curve with one marked point, and
q
is any other smooth point of
C
, then there is a natural isomorphism
(C,p)\cong(C,q)
: if
C
is smooth, then
(C,p)
is an elliptic curve with identity
p
, and the isomorphism is translation by
q-p
; if
C
is singular, then it is isomorphic to the nodal curve
\mathbb{P}^{1}/\{0,\infty\}
and the statement follows from the fact that
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{1})
acts
3
-transitively on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Note that the previous argument also works in families.

Remark 10.7
.

Explicitly, on the fibre at
(E,0)
, the induced action on
C_{n-1}(E^{\prime})
is given as follows: by setting
x_{1}=0
, we have
\sigma\cdot(x_{2},\ldots,x_{n})=(x_{\sigma(2)}-x_{\sigma(1)},\ldots,x_{\sigma(n)}-x_{\sigma(1)})
.

Let
\varepsilon:\mathfrak{S}_{n}\to\{\pm 1\}
be the alternating (sign) character. If
V
is an object of a pseudo-abelian category with an action of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, we let
V_{\varepsilon}
denote the ‘alternating part’ of
V
, namely the object obtained as the image of the projector
\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\varepsilon(\sigma)\sigma
.

The main result of
[
CF06
]
implies that, for any integer
n\geq 1
, the object
H^{1}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathcal{M}_{1,1};\mathcal{V}_{n-1})
of
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{Q}}
can be obtained as the realisation of the motive

M(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,n})_{\varepsilon}[n]\in\DM(\mathbb{Q}).

We now explain how to extend this to the mixed modular objects of Section
7
. Let
K
be a number field, and let
\mathcal{P}\coloneqq\mathcal{P}_{\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}),K}
and
\mathcal{Q}\coloneqq\mathcal{Q}_{\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}),K}
be the closed substacks of
\mathcal{M}_{1,1}\times_{\mathbb{Q}}K=\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z}),K}
(given by finite unions of residue gerbes of points) as in §
7.3
. Note that we may also allow
\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q}
to be the empty stack
\emptyset
.

Theorem 10.8
.

The object of
\DM(K)
defined by

M^{n}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})\coloneqq M(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,n}\times_{\mathbb{Q}}K\setminus\pi_{n}^{-1}(\mathcal{P}),\pi_{n}^{-1}(\mathcal{Q}))_{\varepsilon}[n]

has cohomology concentrated in degree zero and satisfies:

H^{0}(M^{n}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q}))\cong H_{\mathrm{cusp}}^{1}(\mathcal{M}_{1,1}\setminus\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q};\mathcal{V}_{n-1})

in the category of realisations
\mathcal{H}_{K}
.

The proof will rely on a computation of the cohomology of the fibres of
\pi_{n}
, which is set out in the next paragraph.There is a variant of the above statement for higher levels.

10.3.
Alternating cohomology of the fibres of
\pi_{n}

We recall some standard preliminaries concerning the stratification of the boundary of the Deligne-Mumford compactification
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,n}
.

The boundary strata of the Deligne-Mumford compactification
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,n}
correspond to connected, stable, weighted graphs
G
of genus 1 with
n
external half-edges, which are labeled
1,\ldots,n
. Each vertex
v\in V_{G}
is assigned an integer weight
w(v)\geq 0
and the total genus is
g(G)=h_{G}+\sum_{v\in V_{G}}w(v)
, where
h_{G}
denotes the Betti number of
G
. Since
g(G)=1
, there is at most one vertex with a non-zero weight. The codimension of the corresponding boundary stratum is the number of edges of
G
. Inclusions of (closed) boundary strata are dual to contractions of internal edges: the contraction of a self-edge produces a vertex of weight one, and the contraction of an edge whose endpoints have weights
w_{1},w_{2}
is a vertex of weight
w_{1}+w_{2}
.

A boundary stratum of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,n}
lies over the cusp
\infty\subset\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,1}
if and only if the corresponding graph has a cycle, i.e.,
h_{G}=1
. Thus, the remaining strata in
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,n}\setminus\pi_{n}^{-1}(\infty)
correspond to stable weighted trees
T
of genus
g(T)=1
, that is, with a single vertex of weight
1
. Such a graph has no automorphisms. In codimension
k
, the corresponding (closed) strata of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,n}\setminus\pi^{-1}(\infty)
are therefore isomorphic to

(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,m}\setminus\pi_{m}^{-1}(\infty))\times\prod_{j=1}^{k}\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,m_{j}},

where
m+m_{1}+\cdots+m_{k}=n+2k
.

Proposition 10.9
.

Let
\pi_{n}^{-1}(\mathcal{G}_{E})\subset\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,n}
be the fibre of
\pi_{n}:\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,n}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,1}
above the residue gerbe
\mathcal{G}_{E}\subset\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,1}
of an elliptic curve
(E,0)
defined over a number field. Then, the alternating part of the cohomology satisfies:

H^{i}_{\mathrm{B}}\left(\pi_{n}^{-1}(\mathcal{G}_{E})\right)_{\varepsilon}\cong\begin{cases}\left(\Sym^{n-1}\,H^{1}_{\mathrm{B}}(E)\right)^{\Gamma_{E}}&\hbox{ if }i=n-1\\
0&\hbox{ otherwise }\ .\end{cases}\ .

Proof.

The following argument paraphrases results in
[
CF06
]
. It follows from the preliminaries preceding the statement of this proposition that the fibre of
\pi_{n}
at
(E,0)
, which is isomorphic to
\overline{C}_{n-1}(E^{\prime})
, admits an open stratification by:

C_{m}(E^{\prime})\times\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathcal{M}_{0,m_{j}}.

The symmetric group
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
acts by permuting the marked points, and in particular permutes the boundary strata. As in
[
CF06
]
, consider an extremal vertex of weight 0 in the dual graph corresponding to any open boundary stratum. It corresponds to a component
\mathcal{M}_{0,m_{j}}
, where one of the
m_{j}
points corresponds to an internal edge, and the remaining
m_{j}-1
points correspond to external half-edges. The subgroup
\mathfrak{S}_{m_{j}-1}\subset\mathfrak{S}_{n}
permutes the latter. As shown in
[
CF06
, Lemma 5]
, the alternating representation of
\mathfrak{S}_{m_{j}-1}
does not occur in
H^{i}(\mathcal{M}_{0,m_{j}})
. It follows via the Künneth formula that
H^{i}(C_{m}(E^{\prime})\times\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathcal{M}_{0,m_{j}})_{\varepsilon}=0
whenever
k>0
. By a relative cohomology spectral sequence relative to the strict normal crossing divisor
\overline{C}_{m}(E^{\prime})\setminus C_{m}(E^{\prime})
, or by using excision exact sequences for cohomology with compact supports, we deduce that

H^{i}(\overline{C}_{n-1}(E^{\prime}))_{\varepsilon}=H_{c}^{i}(C_{n-1}(E^{\prime}))_{\varepsilon}.

Now,
[
CF06
, Lemma 2]
states that
H_{c}^{i}(C_{n-1}(E^{\prime}))_{\varepsilon}\cong\Sym^{n-1}H^{1}(E)
for
i=n-1
, and vanishes otherwise. We conclude that

H^{i}(\pi_{n}^{-1}(\mathcal{G}_{E}))_{\varepsilon}\cong\left(H_{c}^{i}(\overline{C}_{n-1}(E^{\prime}))^{\Gamma_{E}}\right)_{\varepsilon},

which implies the statement of the proposition, since the action of
\Gamma_{E}
and
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
commute.
∎

From now on, a sheaf on a stack will mean a sheaf on the underlying complex orbifold.

Corollary 10.10
.

Let
\mathbb{Q}
denote the constant sheaf on
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,n}\setminus\pi_{n}^{-1}(\infty)
. Then in the derived category of sheaves on
\mathcal{M}_{1,1}
we have an isomorphism

\mathbb{V}^{\mathrm{B}}_{n-1}[1-n]\cong(R\pi_{n*}\mathbb{Q})_{\varepsilon},

where
\mathbb{V}_{n-1}^{\mathrm{B}}
is the Betti local system on
\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{an}}_{1,1}=\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})\backslash\!\!\backslash\mathfrak{H}
defined in §
5.2
.

Proof.

By Proposition
10.9
, the
k
th cohomology sheaf of the object
(R{\pi_{n}}_{*}\mathbb{Q})_{\varepsilon}
in the derived category of sheaves on the analytification of
\mathcal{M}_{1,1}
vanishes if
k\neq n-1
and satisfies
\Sym^{n-1}R^{1}{\pi_{n}}_{*}\mathbb{Q}\cong(R^{n-1}{\pi_{n}}_{*}\mathbb{Q})_{\varepsilon}
, because the natural map given by the cup product is an isomorphism on stalks, again by Proposition
10.9
. Conclude using the standard fact that an object in a derived category is isomorphic to its cohomology when the latter is concentrated in a single degree.
∎

10.4.
Proof of Theorem
10.8

First, some notation. In this proof we shall assume for simplicity of notation that
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{Q}
are defined over
\mathbb{Q}
. In the general case, simply replace every occurrence of
\mathcal{M}_{1,n}
,
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,n}
and so on, with their extensions of scalars
\mathcal{M}_{1,n}\times_{\mathbb{Q}}K
,
etc
. Moreover, to fix ideas and avoid introducing further notation, we shall only work with Betti realisations.

For any closed substack
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathcal{M}_{1,1}
given by a finite union of residue gerbes of points in
\mathcal{M}_{1,1}
(typically
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{P}\cup\mathcal{Q}
), consider the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,n}\setminus\pi_{n}^{-1}(\mathcal{S}\cup\infty)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,n}\setminus\pi_{n}^{-1}(\mathcal{S})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{1,1}\setminus\mathcal{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,1}\setminus\mathcal{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

where the vertical maps are given by
\pi_{n}
. The map
j
therefore denotes, without ambiguity, the inclusion of the complement of the cusp (resp. the fiber of
\pi_{n}
over the cusp).

Now consider the commutative diagram:

(10.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,n}\setminus\pi_{n}^{-1}(\mathcal{P}\cup\mathcal{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,n}\setminus\pi_{n}^{-1}(\mathcal{P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}^{-1}(\mathcal{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,1}\setminus(\mathcal{P}\cup\mathcal{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,1}\setminus\mathcal{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

where
i
and
k
also denote inclusions. Restricting to the complement of the cusp
\infty
in the bottom row (resp. the complement of
\pi_{n}^{-1}(\infty)
in the top row), yields an analagous commutative diagram, whose maps will be denoted without ambiguity using the same symbols. This new commutative diagram forms, together with (
10.4
), a commutative diagram which consists of a pair of commutative cubes glued together along a face.

We have at our disposal the following extensions of the constant sheaf
\mathbb{Q}
over
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,n}\setminus\pi_{n}^{-1}(\mathcal{P}\cup\infty)
to a sheaf, or an object of the derived category of sheaves, on
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,n}\setminus\pi_{n}^{-1}(\mathcal{P})
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}\mathbb{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{*}\mathbb{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Rj_{*}\mathbb{Q}\ .}

For now we shall focus on the extension by zero,
j_{!}\mathbb{Q}
. With reference to the top line of the diagram (
10.4
), we deduce the following exact sequence of sheaves on
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,n}\setminus\pi_{n}^{-1}(\mathcal{P})
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{!}k^{*}j_{!}\mathbb{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}\mathbb{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}i^{*}j_{!}\mathbb{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0\ .}

Consider the motive
M^{n}_{!}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})\coloneqq M(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,n}\setminus\pi_{n}^{-1}(\mathcal{P}),\pi_{n}^{-1}(\mathcal{Q}\cup\infty))_{\varepsilon}[n]
. By Corollary
10.10
,
(R{\pi_{n}}_{*}j_{!}\mathbb{Q})_{\varepsilon}
is isomorphic, in the derived category of sheaves, to
j_{!}\mathbb{V}^{\mathrm{B}}_{n-1}[n-1]
. This follows from the properness of
\pi_{n}
,
i.e.
,
{\pi_{n}}_{*}={\pi_{n}}_{!}
, and the exactness of
j_{!}=Rj_{!}
, which together imply that
R{\pi_{n}}_{!}j_{!}=R(\pi_{n}\circ j)_{!}=R(j\circ\pi_{n})_{!}=j_{!}R{\pi_{n}}_{!}
.
The action of the symmetric group preserves the fibers of
\pi_{n}
.

By definition of
j
and slight abuse of notation,
k_{!}k^{*}j_{!}\mathbb{Q}=k_{!}j_{!}j^{*}k^{*}\mathbb{Q}
and hence

\displaystyle H^{m-n}_{\mathrm{B}}(M^{n}_{!}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q}))

\displaystyle\cong H^{m}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,n}\setminus\pi_{n}^{-1}(\mathcal{P});k_{!}k^{*}j_{!}\mathbb{Q})_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\cong H^{m}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,1}\setminus\mathcal{P};(R{\pi_{n}}_{*}(k_{!}k^{*}j_{!}\mathbb{Q}))_{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle\cong H^{m}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,1}\setminus\mathcal{P};k_{!}k^{*}(R{\pi_{n}}_{*}j_{!}\mathbb{Q})_{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle\cong\begin{cases}H^{1}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,1}\setminus\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q};j_{!}\mathbb{V}^{\mathrm{B}}_{n-1}),&\hbox{if }m=n\\
0,&\hbox{otherwise,}\end{cases}

where the commutation
(R{\pi_{n}}_{*}(k_{!}k^{*}j_{!}\mathbb{Q}))_{\varepsilon}\cong k_{!}k^{*}(R{\pi_{n}}_{*}j_{!}\mathbb{Q})_{\varepsilon}
follows from a similar argument to the one given above for
j_{!}
.
We now consider the motive
M^{n}_{\circ}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})\coloneqq M(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,n}\setminus\pi_{n}^{-1}(\mathcal{P}\cup\infty),\pi_{n}^{-1}(\mathcal{Q}))_{\varepsilon}[n]
and compute its cohomology along similar lines applying Corollary
10.10
(here, again by abuse of notation,
k
is restricted along
j
):

\displaystyle H_{\mathrm{B}}^{m-n}(M^{n}_{\circ}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q}))

\displaystyle\cong H^{m}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,n}\setminus\pi_{n}^{-1}(\mathcal{P}\cup\infty);k_{!}k^{*}\mathbb{Q})_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\cong H^{m}(\mathcal{M}_{1,1}\setminus\mathcal{P};(R{\pi_{n}}_{*}(k_{!}k^{*}\mathbb{Q}))_{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle\cong H^{m}(\mathcal{M}_{1,1}\setminus\mathcal{P};k_{!}k^{*}(R{\pi_{n}}_{*}\mathbb{Q})_{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle\cong\begin{cases}H^{1}(\mathcal{M}_{1,1}\setminus\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q};\mathbb{V}_{n-1}^{\mathrm{B}}),&\text{if }m=n\\
0,&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

Now consider the natural maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\mathrm{B}}^{m-n}(M_{!}^{n}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\mathrm{B}}^{m-n}(M^{n}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\mathrm{B}}^{m-n}(M_{\circ}^{n}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})).}

To conclude, it suffices to show that the first is surjective, and the second is injective, for all
m
(see (
7.9
)). To establish this, we note that the cohomology of each of the three objects
M^{n}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q}),M^{n}_{!}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q}),M^{n}_{\circ}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})
lies in two exact sequences (relative cohomology and Gysin):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\mathrm{B}}^{m-1}(\pi_{n}^{-1}(\mathcal{Q}))_{\varepsilon}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\mathrm{B}}^{m-n}(M^{n}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\mathrm{B}}^{m-n}(M^{n}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{P},\emptyset))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\mathrm{B}}^{m-n}(M^{n}_{\bullet}(\emptyset,\emptyset))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\mathrm{B}}^{m-n}(M^{n}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{P},\emptyset))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\mathrm{B}}^{m-1}(\pi_{n}^{-1}(\mathcal{P}))_{\varepsilon}(-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

where
\bullet\in\{\emptyset,!,\circ\}
. It suffices, therefore, to show that in the natural maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\mathrm{B}}^{m-n}(M_{!}^{n}(\emptyset,\emptyset))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\mathrm{B}}^{m-n}(M^{n}(\emptyset,\emptyset))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\mathrm{B}}^{m-n}(M_{\circ}^{n}(\emptyset,\emptyset)),}

the first is surjective, and the second injective, for all
m
. This is equivalent to the statement:

H_{\mathrm{B}}^{m-n}(M^{n}(\emptyset,\emptyset))=\im\left(H_{\mathrm{B}}^{m-n}(M_{!}^{n}(\emptyset,\emptyset))\rightarrow H_{\mathrm{B}}^{m-n}(M_{\circ}^{n}(\emptyset,\emptyset))\right)

By the above computations, the right-hand side vanishes if
m\neq n
and for
m=n
equals

\mathrm{im}\left(H^{1}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,1};j_{!}\mathbb{V}_{n-1}^{\mathrm{B}})\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathcal{M}_{1,1};\mathbb{V}_{n-1}^{\mathrm{B}})\right)\cong H^{1}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathcal{M}_{1,1};\mathbb{V}_{n-1}^{\mathrm{B}})\ .

The assertion that, indeed,
H_{\mathrm{B}}^{0}(M^{n}(\emptyset,\emptyset))=H^{1}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathcal{M}_{1,1};\mathbb{V}_{n-1}^{\mathrm{B}})
and vanishes in all other degrees is the main result of
[
CF06
]
, and thus completes the proof.

10.5.
Motivic biextensions for cusp form motives

Let
M^{n}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})
be defined as above and write
M^{n}(\mathcal{P})
for the case when
\mathcal{Q}=\emptyset
. Let
M^{n}_{\mathrm{cusp}}
denote the ‘cusp form motive’ which corresponds to the case when
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{Q}=\emptyset.
With notations as in §
8.3
, any Hecke correspondence
T_{\alpha}
defines an endomorphism of cusp form motives

T_{\alpha}:M_{\mathrm{cusp}}^{n}\longrightarrow M_{\mathrm{cusp}}^{n}\ .

Theorem 10.11
.

Suppose that
T
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-linear combination of Hecke correspondences
T_{\alpha}
such that

(10.5)

T\in\mathrm{End}(M_{\mathrm{cusp}}^{n})\hbox{ is the zero map.}

Suppose that
\mathcal{P}=\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})w_{1}
and
\mathcal{Q}=\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})z_{1}
, where
z_{1},w_{1}
are defined over
K
. Then there exists a distinguished triangle in
\DM(K)

(10.6)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\mathrm{Sym}^{n-1}\,H^{1}(E_{z_{1}})\right)^{\Gamma_{z_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\mathrm{Sym}^{n-1}\,\,H^{1}(E_{w_{1}})\right)^{\Gamma_{w_{1}}}(-1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}

whose realisation, in the case where
w_{1},z_{1}
are not elliptic, is the extension (
8.15
).

Proof.

There exists a closed substack
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{M}_{1,1}
, given by a finite union of residue gerbes of points, such that the following map is a morphism of motives:

T:M^{n}(\mathcal{P})\longrightarrow M^{n}(\mathcal{P}^{\prime})

We shall write
R_{\mathcal{P}}
for
\left(\Sym^{n-1}\,H^{1}(E_{w_{1}})\right)^{\Gamma_{w_{1}}}(-1)
. Consider the diagram of distinguished triangles

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{n}_{\mathrm{cusp}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{n}(\mathcal{P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{\mathcal{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{n}(\mathcal{P}^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{n}(\mathcal{P}^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}

where the vertical morphism is induced by the Hecke correspondence
T
, which gives rise to a morphism
R_{\mathcal{P}}\rightarrow M^{n}(\mathcal{P}^{\prime})
. Now consider the relative cohomology distinguished triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{\mathcal{Q}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{n}(\mathcal{P^{\prime}},\mathcal{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{n}(\mathcal{P}^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}

where
S_{\mathcal{Q}}=\left(\Sym^{n-1}\,H^{1}(E_{z_{1}})\right)^{\Gamma_{z_{1}}}
. By composing the morphism
M^{n}(\mathcal{P}^{\prime})\rightarrow S_{Q}[1]
with
R_{P}\rightarrow M^{n}(\mathcal{P}^{\prime})
, we deduce the existence of
\mathcal{E}
and a commutative diagram with two distinguished triangles:

\hbox to201.6pt{\vbox to54.57pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 100.79741pt\lower-26.35612pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{{{{{}}{

{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 97.39 -23.99 L 112.89 -23.99}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{81.78639pt}{-17.33531pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{71.12386pt}{-14.1492pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\qed

Corollary 10.12
.

In the setting of Theorem
10.11
, suppose that
z_{1},w_{1}
have complex multiplication defined over
K
. Then the extension (
8.18
) is the realisation of a Kummer extension in
\DM(K)
. Consequently, Theorem
9.1
applies and the Gross-Zagier conjecture is true.

Proof.

By the remark which follows Proposition
6.10
, the decomposition (
8.8
) is motivic and therefore the Kummer extension

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}(0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}(-1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}

may be extracted from (
10.6
) by
pushout and pullback. The conclusion then follows from Theorem
9.1
.
∎

The previous results essentially prove a weaker version of the Gross-Zagier conjecture under the assumption (
10.5
). To deduce the full conjecture requires the statement that (
10.5
) follows from (
8.14
).

10.5.1.
Vanishing of the endomorphism
T

If the Betti realisation functor is faithful on the motives of cusp forms, then (
8.14
) indeed implies (
10.5
). In a weight
n+1
in which there are no cusp forms, this follows from the conservativity conjecture (cf.
[
Ayo17
, Conjecture 2.12]
) for the restriction of the Betti realisation functor to the subcategory of motives generated by
M^{n}_{\mathrm{cusp}}.

In this case, the conservativity conjecture is equivalent to showing that the corresponding cusp form motive
M^{n}_{\mathrm{cusp}}
is zero, which appears to be an open problem. In Appendix
D
, we show that this is indeed true in the first interesting case, which gives a complete proof of the Gross-Zagier conjecture using the geometric techniques of this paper.

Theorem 10.13
.

The motive
M^{3}_{\mathrm{cusp}}
is zero. Therefore the Gross-Zagier conjecture is true in level 1 and weight
4
(
i.e.
, for the function
G_{\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}),2}(z,w)
), for any pair of CM points
z\notin\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})w
.

Proof.

By Proposition
D.7
, the motive of
\mathcal{M}_{1,3}
is mixed Tate and hence so is
M^{3}_{\mathrm{cusp}}
. Since the Betti realisation functor restricted to the triangulated category of mixed Tate motives is conservative, it follows that the cusp form motive
M^{3}_{\mathrm{cusp}}
is zero since its Betti realisation vanishes. It follows that the hypotheses of Theorem
10.11
and Corollary
10.12
hold, and the statement then follows from Theorem
9.1
.
∎

We expect that our methods can be extended to prove a similar theorem for
n\leq 10
by showing that
\mathcal{M}_{1,n}
is mixed Tate in this range.
1
1

1

A well-known result of Belorousski
[
Bel98
]
shows that the coarse moduli spaces
M_{1,n}
are rational algebraic varieties for
n\leq 10
, but this does not imply the mixed Tate property.
The conservativity conjecture is open in general, but is known to hold on the thick rigid tensor triangulated subcategory generated by motives of smooth projective curves; see (
[
Wil12
,
Wil19
,
Anc15
]
and
[
Ayo17
, Corollary 2.33]
).

10.5.2.
Higher levels and alternative approaches

In the above discussion we considered modular forms of level 1. However, by using the results of Petersen
[
Pet12
, Theorem 5.1]
one may use the moduli spaces of curves with level structure
\mathcal{M}_{1,n}(m)
to obtain similar results to Theorem
10.11
and Corollary
10.12
but for the congruence group
\Gamma(m)
. By Remark 6.4 of
loc. cit.
, this can be extended to other congruence subgroups of
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, specifically
\Gamma_{1}(m)
and
\Gamma_{0}(m)
giving a motivic interpretation of the Gross-Zagier conjecture in an identical manner. In particular, in every weight, level and nebentypus for which the corresponding space of cusp form vanishes (see,
e.g.
,
[
Zho15
, Appendix 2]
), one hopes to prove, in the spirit of Theorem
10.13
, that the corresponding motive vanishes.

Remark 10.14
.

We have constructed our modular biextensions out of the motives of moduli spaces of curves. An alternative approach is to use the motives of Kuga-Sato varieties, following Scholl
[
Sch90
]
.

Remark 10.15
.

Instead of appealing to the faithfulness of the Betti realisation functor, it is enough to show that the cusp form motives (in suitable weight and level) decompose into finitely many Hecke eigenspaces, which implies that
(
10.5
) follows from (
8.14
). Whilst this can be shown for motives modulo homological equivalence, the general statement follows from the standard conjectures, as pointed out in
[
Sch90
, Remark 1.2.6]
and
[
Pet12
, Remark 6.3]
.

Appendix A
Complements in algebraic de Rham cohomology

For lack of a suitable reference, we collect in this appendix a few basic definitions and results concerning relative algebraic de Rham cohomology with non-trivial coefficients.

A.1.
Relative algebraic de Rham cohomology with coefficients on smooth varieties

Let
X
be a smooth algebraic variety over a field
k
of characteristic zero,
i:Y\hookrightarrow X
be a smooth closed
k
-subscheme of positive codimension, and
(\mathcal{V},\nabla)
be a vector bundle with integrable connection on
X
, defined over
k
.

We denote by
\Omega^{\bullet}_{X/k}(\mathcal{V})
the
k
-linear complex of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules induced by the integrable connection
\nabla
:

(A.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\Omega^{1}_{X/k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\Omega^{2}_{X/k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla^{2}}

and by
\Omega^{\bullet}_{Y/k}(i^{*}\mathcal{V})
the analogous complex on
Y
given by restricting
(\mathcal{V},\nabla)
via
i
. The pullback of differential forms then induces a
k
-linear morphism of complexes on
X

i^{*}:\Omega^{\bullet}_{X/k}(\mathcal{V})\longrightarrow i_{*}\Omega^{\bullet}_{Y/k}(i^{*}\mathcal{V})\text{, }\qquad\alpha\longmapsto\alpha|_{i(Y)}\ ,

The mapping cone of
i^{*}
is the
k
-linear complex of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules denoted by

\Omega^{\bullet}_{(X,Y)/k}(\mathcal{V})\coloneqq C(i^{*}).

Concretely,
\Omega^{n}_{(X,Y)/k}(\mathcal{V})=\Omega^{n}_{X/k}(\mathcal{V})\oplus i_{*}\Omega^{n-1}_{Y/k}(i^{*}\mathcal{V})
and the differential is given by

(\alpha,\beta)\longmapsto(\nabla^{n}\alpha,\alpha|_{i(Y)}-\nabla^{n-1}\beta)

Definition A.1
.

The
n
th algebraic de Rham cohomology of
X
relative to
i:Y\hookrightarrow X
with coefficients in
\mathcal{V}
is the
n
th hypercohomology

H^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X,Y;\mathcal{V})\coloneqq\mathbb{H}^{n}(X,\Omega^{\bullet}_{(X,Y)/k}(\mathcal{V}))\,.

By abuse, we omit in the above notation the base field
k
, the connection
\nabla
, and the closed immersion
i
, as they are often clear from context.

Remark A.2
.

Note that
H^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X,Y;\mathcal{V})
is always a finite-dimensional
k
-vector space. When
(\mathcal{V},\nabla)=(\mathcal{O}_{X},d)
is trivial, then
H^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X,Y;\mathcal{V})
coincides with the usual relative de Rham cohomology
H^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X,Y)
. When
Y
is empty, we recover the ‘absolute’ de Rham cohomology with coefficients
H^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X;\mathcal{V})
.

The relative de Rham cohomology with coefficients is related to the de Rham cohomology with coefficients of
X
and
Y
via the long exact sequence of
k
-vector spaces

(A.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{n-1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(Y;i^{*}\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\mathrm{dR}}^{n}(X,Y;\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X;\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(Y;i^{*}\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}

induced by the short exact sequence of complexes

\hbox to311.1pt{\vbox to18.38pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 155.54834pt\lower-9.1875pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{}{}}
