A.2.
Residue exact sequence

Let
\iota:D\hookrightarrow X
be the inclusion of a smooth divisor in
X
such that
D\cap Y
is a smooth divisor in
Y
. Recall that there is a complex

(A.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\bullet}_{X/k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\bullet}_{X/k}(\log D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{*}\Omega^{\bullet}_{D/k}[-1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0\ ,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res_{D}}

where
\Res_{D}:\Omega^{n}_{X/k}(\log D)\to\iota_{*}\Omega^{n-1}_{D/k}
is the Poincaré residue map. We denote by
\Omega^{\bullet}_{X/k}(\log D,\mathcal{V})
the complex analogous to (
A.1
) defined using the complex of logarithmic forms
\Omega^{\bullet}_{X/k}(\log D)
in place of
\Omega^{\bullet}_{X/k}
. Pullback by
i
yields a map

i^{*}:\Omega^{\bullet}_{X/k}(\log D,\mathcal{V})\longrightarrow i_{*}\Omega^{\bullet}_{Y/k}(\log D\cap Y,i^{*}\mathcal{V})\ ,

the mapping cone of which is denoted by
\Omega^{\bullet}_{(X,Y)/k}(\log D,\mathcal{V})
. We get from (
A.3
) an exact sequence

(A.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\bullet}_{(X,Y)/k}(\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\bullet}_{(X,Y)/k}(\log D,\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{*}\Omega^{\bullet}_{(D,D\cap Y)/k}(\iota^{*}\mathcal{V})[-1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0\ .}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res_{D}}

Proposition A.3
.

The restriction from
X
to
X\setminus D
induces an isomorphism of
k
-vector spaces

\mathbb{H}^{n}(X,\Omega^{\bullet}_{(X,Y)/k}(\log D,\mathcal{V}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X\setminus D,Y\setminus(D\cap Y);\mathcal{V})\,.

Proof.

When
Y
is empty, this is a special case of Deligne’s theorem
[
Del70
, II, Corollaire 3.15]
. The general case reduces to this one by considering the hypercohomology long exact sequence associated to

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}\Omega^{\bullet}_{Y/k}(\log D\cap Y,i^{*}\mathcal{V})[-1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\bullet}_{(X,Y)/k}(\log D,\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\bullet}_{X/k}(\log D,\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

and applying the five lemma.
∎

It follows from the above that the hypercohomology long exact sequence associated to (
A.4
) is

(A.5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X,Y;\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X\setminus D,Y\setminus(D\cap Y);\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{n-1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(D,D\cap Y;\iota^{*}\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots.}

A.3.
The case of a smooth affine curve

We keep the above notation and assume moreover that
X
is a smooth affine algebraic variety over
k
of dimension 1. Since
X
is affine, it follows from Serre’s vanishing theorem that

H^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X,Y;\mathcal{V})=H^{n}(\Gamma(X,\Omega^{\bullet}_{(X,Y)/k}(\mathcal{V})).

Since
X
(resp.
Y
) has dimension
1
(resp.
0
), the complex
\Gamma(X,\Omega^{\bullet}_{(X,Y)/k}(\mathcal{V}))
is simply

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma(X,\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma(X,\Omega^{1}_{X/k}(\mathcal{V}))\oplus\Gamma(Y,i^{*}\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0\,,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}

where
\partial s=(\nabla s,s|_{i(Y)})
, so that

(A.6)

H^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X,Y;\mathcal{V})=\begin{cases}\ker\partial&n=0\\
\coker\partial&n=1\\
0&n\geq 2\,.\end{cases}

Elements of
H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X,Y;\mathcal{V})
are thus given by equivalence classes of pairs
(\alpha,v)\in\Gamma(X,\Omega^{1}_{X/k}(\mathcal{V}))\oplus\Gamma(Y,i^{*}\mathcal{V})
, where
(\alpha,v)
is equivalent to
(\beta,w)
if and only if there is
s\in\Gamma(X,\mathcal{V})
such that
(\alpha-\beta,v-w)=(\nabla s,s|_{i(Y)})
.

Remark A.4
.

If
Y
is non-empty, then it is given by a
\Gal(\overline{k}/k)
-invariant finite subset
\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}\}
of
X(\overline{k})
, where
\overline{k}
denotes an algebraic closure of
k
. Note that we can identify
\Gamma(Y,i^{*}\mathcal{V})
with a subset of
\bigoplus_{i=1}^{m}\mathcal{V}(y_{i})
, where
\mathcal{V}(y_{i})\coloneqq\mathcal{V}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X,y_{i}}}k(y_{i})
denotes the fibre of
\mathcal{V}
at the point
y_{i}
. Then, the restriction
s|_{i(Y)}
is identified with the tuple
(s(y_{1}),\ldots,s(y_{m}))
.

The long exact sequence in relative cohomology (
A.2
) boils down to

(A.7)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X,Y;\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}_{\mathrm{dR}}(Y;i^{*}\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X,Y;\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X;\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}

where
\delta
sends
v\in H^{0}_{\mathrm{dR}}(Y;i^{*}\mathcal{V})=\Gamma(Y,i^{*}\mathcal{V})
to the class of
(0,v)
in
H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X,Y;\mathcal{V})
.

Lemma A.5
.

If
\ker(\nabla:\Gamma(X,\mathcal{V})\to\Gamma(X,\Omega^{1}_{X/k}(\mathcal{V})))=0
, then

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}_{\mathrm{dR}}(Y;i^{*}\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X,Y;\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X;\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[(0,v)\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[(\alpha,v)\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\alpha\right]}

is an exact sequence of
k
-vector spaces.

Proof.

By (
A.6
), the hypothesis implies that
H^{0}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X,Y;\mathcal{V})=\ker\partial\subset\ker\nabla=0
. The result immediately follows from (
A.7
).
∎

A.4.
Hodge filtration

We keep the hypothesis that
X
is a smooth affine curve over
k
, and we denote by
\overline{X}
the smooth compactification of
X
over
k
, with divisor at infinity
Z
, so that
X=\overline{X}\setminus Z
. As before, let
(\mathcal{V},\nabla)
be a vector bundle with integrable connection on
X
. We further impose the following:

(A.8)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&(\mathcal{V},\nabla)\text{ extends to a vector bundle }\overline{\mathcal{V}}\text{ with a logarithmic connection}\\
&\,\overline{\nabla}:\overline{\mathcal{V}}\longrightarrow\overline{\mathcal{V}}\otimes\Omega^{1}_{\overline{X}/k}(\log Z)\text{ whose residue along }Z\text{ is nilpotent}.\end{split}

The extension
(\overline{\mathcal{V}},\overline{\nabla})
is uniquely determined by the above properties. Let

(A.9)

\Omega^{\bullet}_{(\overline{X},Y)/k}(\log Z,\overline{\mathcal{V}}):\qquad\overline{\mathcal{V}}\longrightarrow(\overline{\mathcal{V}}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X}}}\Omega^{1}_{\overline{X}/k}(\log Z))\oplus\overline{i}_{*}\overline{i}^{*}\overline{\mathcal{V}}

be the two-term complex defined as the cone of

\overline{i}^{*}:\Omega^{\bullet}_{\overline{X}/k}(\log Z,\overline{\mathcal{V}})\longrightarrow\overline{i}_{*}\Omega^{\bullet}_{Y/k}(\overline{i}^{*}\overline{\mathcal{V}}),

with
\overline{i}:Y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\hookrightarrow}}X\hookrightarrow\overline{X}
the inclusion.

Lemma A.6
.

The restriction induces a canonical isomorphism

\mathbb{H}^{1}(\overline{X},\Omega^{\bullet}_{(\overline{X},Y)/k}(\log Z,\overline{\mathcal{V}}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X,Y;\mathcal{V}).

Proof.

This follows from Deligne’s theorem
[
Del70
, II, Corollaire 3.15]
(
cf.
Proposition
A.3
).
∎

Assume that the pair
(\overline{\mathcal{V}},\overline{\nabla})
is equipped with a filtration by sub-bundles

(A.10)

\overline{\mathcal{V}}=F^{0}\overline{\mathcal{V}}\supset F^{1}\overline{\mathcal{V}}\supset\cdots\supset F^{m}\overline{\mathcal{V}}\supset F^{m+1}\overline{\mathcal{V}}=0

satisfying Griffiths’ transversality condition

\overline{\nabla}(F^{p}\overline{\mathcal{V}})\subset F^{p-1}\overline{\mathcal{V}}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X}}}\Omega^{1}_{\overline{X}/k}(\log Z),\qquad p=1,\ldots,m.

Then, (
A.10
) induces a filtration by
k
-linear subspaces

H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X,Y;\mathcal{V})=F^{0}H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X,Y;\mathcal{V})\supset\cdots\supset F^{m+1}H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X,Y;\mathcal{V})\supset F^{m+2}H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X,Y;\mathcal{V})=0

as follows. Consider the filtration of (
A.9
) by the subcomplexes

F^{p}\Omega^{\bullet}_{\overline{X}/k}(\log Z,\overline{\mathcal{V}}):\qquad F^{p}\overline{\mathcal{V}}\longrightarrow(F^{p-1}\overline{\mathcal{V}}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X}}}\Omega^{1}_{\overline{X}/k}(\log Z))\oplus\overline{i}_{*}\overline{i}^{*}F^{p}\overline{\mathcal{V}},

and set, under the isomorphism of Lemma
A.6
,

(A.11)

F^{p}H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X,Y;\mathcal{V})\coloneqq\im(\mathbb{H}^{1}(\overline{X},F^{p}\Omega^{\bullet}_{\overline{X}/k}(\log Z,\overline{\mathcal{V}}))\longrightarrow H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X,Y;\mathcal{V})).

Remark A.7
.

When
k=\mathbb{C}
and
(\mathcal{V},\nabla,F^{\bullet}\mathcal{V})
underlies a variation of Hodge structures, then
H^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X,Y;\mathcal{V})
has a natural mixed Hodge structure with Hodge filtration given by (
A.11
), and weight filtration related to cuspidal cohomology (
cf.

[
Zuc79
, §13]
,
[
Hai16
, §6]
). This mixed Hodge structure is functorial and compatible with the relative cohomology and residue exact sequences (
A.7
) and (
A.5
).

Appendix B
Complex conjugation in cohomology with coefficients

The purpose of this appendix is to cast the notion of harmonic lifts of modular forms, studied in Section
2
, in a more general setting concerning complex conjugation on the de Rham cohomology with non-trivial coefficients of complex manifolds.

B.1.
Reminders on analytic de Rham cohomology with coefficients

Let
M
be a complex manifold. We denote its sheaf of holomorphic functions by
\mathcal{O}_{M}
and its complex of sheaves of holomorphic differential forms by
\Omega_{M}^{\bullet}
.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a coherent locally free sheaf of
\mathcal{O}_{M}
-modules, which corresponds to the sheaf of sections of a holomorphic vector bundle on
M
. From now on, we use these two notions interchangeably. A holomorphic integrable connection
\nabla:\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{V}\otimes\Omega^{1}_{M}
induces a complex

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{M}}\Omega^{1}_{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{M}}\Omega^{2}_{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla^{2}}

which we denote by
\Omega^{\bullet}_{M}(\mathcal{V})
. Recall that the analytic de Rham cohomology with coefficients in
(\mathcal{V},\nabla)
is the finite-dimensional complex vector space defined by the hypercohomology

H^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(M;\mathcal{V})\coloneqq\mathbb{H}^{n}(M,\Omega^{\bullet}_{M}(\mathcal{V})).

Example B.1
.

If
M
is a Stein manifold, then Cartan’s theorem B implies that the sheaves
\Omega^{p}_{M}(\mathcal{V})
,
p\geq 0
, are
\Gamma(M,-)
-acylic, so that the de Rham cohomology can be computed by global holomorphic forms:

H^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(M;\mathcal{V})\cong H^{n}(\Gamma(M,\Omega_{M}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{V}))).

Remark B.2
.

If
M=X^{\mathrm{an}}
is the analytification of a smooth algebraic variety
X
over
\mathbb{C}
, and
(\mathcal{V},\nabla)
is the analytification of an algebraic vector bundle with integrable connection
(\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{alg}},\nabla^{\mathrm{alg}})
on
X
with regular singularities, then a theorem of Deligne
[
Del70
, §II.6]
shows that there is a canonical isomorphism of complex vector spaces

H^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X;\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{alg}})\cong H^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(M;\mathcal{V}),

where
H^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X;\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{alg}})
is the algebraic de Rham cohomology of Appendix
A
.

Consider the sheaf of horizontal sections

(B.1)

\mathbb{V}\coloneqq\ker(\nabla:\mathcal{V}\longrightarrow\mathcal{V}\otimes\Omega^{1}_{M}),

which is a
\mathbb{C}
-local system such that
(\mathbb{V}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{O}_{M},\mathrm{id}\otimes d)\cong(\mathcal{V},\nabla)
. The holomorphic Poincaré lemma implies that
\Omega_{M}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{V})
is a resolution of
\mathbb{V}
, yielding the canonical isomorphism with the sheaf cohomology of
M

(B.2)

H^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(M;\mathcal{V})\cong H^{n}(M,\mathbb{V})

Finally, we recall that the analytic de Rham cohomology can also be computed via smooth differential forms,. Let
\mathcal{A}_{M}^{\bullet}
be the complex of
C^{\infty}
complex-valued differential forms on
M
, which contains
\Omega^{\bullet}_{M}
as a subcomplex, and let

\mathcal{A}_{M}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{V})=\mathcal{V}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{M}}\mathcal{A}^{\bullet}_{M}\cong\mathbb{V}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{A}^{\bullet}_{M}

be the complex induced by
\nabla
.

Lemma B.3
.

There is a canonical isomorphism

H^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(M;\mathcal{V})\cong H^{n}(\Gamma(M,\mathcal{A}_{M}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{V}))).

Proof.

From (
B.2
) and the
C^{\infty}
Poincaré lemma, we obtain an isomorphism
H^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(M;\mathcal{V})\cong\mathbb{H}^{n}(M,\mathcal{A}^{\bullet}_{M}(\mathcal{V}))
. The statement then comes from the existence of partitions of unity, which shows that, for every
p\geq 0
, the sheaf
\mathcal{A}^{p}_{M}(\mathcal{V})
is fine, thus
\Gamma(M,-)
-acyclic.
∎

B.2.
Complex conjugation

Let us assume that the vector bundle with integrable connection
(\mathcal{V},\nabla)
over
M
as above is equipped with the following additional structure:

(i)

a holomorphic sub-bundle
\mathcal{W}\subset\mathcal{V}
, which is not necessarily horizontal,

(ii)

a real structure
\mathbb{V}_{\mathbb{R}}
on the sheaf of horizontal sections
\mathbb{V}
,
i.e.
,
\mathbb{V}_{\mathbb{R}}\subset\mathbb{V}
is a sub-
\mathbb{R}
-local system satisfying

\mathbb{V}_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}\cong\mathbb{V}.

Lemma B.4
.

The datum (ii) is also equivalent to:

(ii)’

a complex anti-linear involution

c:\mathcal{A}^{0}_{M}(\mathcal{V})\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}^{0}_{M}(\mathcal{V}),

which is horizontal for
\nabla
.

Proof.

We use the identification
\mathcal{A}^{0}_{M}(\mathcal{V})\cong\mathbb{V}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{A}_{M}^{0}
. If
\mathbb{V}_{\mathbb{R}}
is a real structure on
\mathbb{V}
, then
c
is defined by
c(s\otimes\varphi)=s\otimes\overline{\varphi}
whenever
s
is a section of
\mathbb{V}_{\mathbb{R}}
. Conversely, given an involution
c
as in the statement, the real structure
\mathbb{V}_{\mathbb{R}}
is given by the sections
s
of
\mathbb{V}
such that
c(s\otimes 1)=s\otimes 1
.
∎

The involution
c
on
\mathcal{A}^{0}_{M}(\mathcal{V})
extends to
\mathcal{A}^{p}_{M}(\mathcal{V})\cong\mathbb{V}_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{A}_{M}^{p}
by

c(s\otimes\alpha)=s\otimes\overline{\alpha},

where
\alpha\mapsto\overline{\alpha}
is the usual complex conjugation of
C^{\infty}
complex-valued differential forms.

Definition B.5
.

A section
\xi
of
\mathcal{A}^{p}_{M}(\mathcal{V})
is said to be
harmonic
, relative to the sub-bundle
\mathcal{W}
and the real structure

\mathbb{V}_{\mathbb{R}}
, if

(B.3)

\nabla^{p}\xi=\omega-c\eta

where both
\omega,\eta
are sections of
\mathcal{W}\otimes\Omega^{p+1}_{M}\subset\mathcal{A}^{p+1}_{M}(\mathcal{V})
.

Definition B.6
.

Given a section
\omega
of
\mathcal{W}\otimes\Omega^{p+1}_{M}
, a
harmonic lift
of
\omega
is a section
\xi
of
\mathcal{A}^{p}_{M}(\mathcal{V})
satisfying (
B.3
) for some
\eta
in
\mathcal{W}\otimes\Omega^{p+1}_{M}
; we may also say that
\xi
is a harmonic lift of the pair
(\omega,\eta)
. When such a harmonic lift exists, we say that
\omega
is a
Betti-conjugate
of
\eta
.

Since
c
is horizontal for
\nabla
, it induces a complex anti-linear involution on cohomology, which we denote by the same symbol:

(B.4)

c:H^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(M;\mathcal{V})\longrightarrow H^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(M;\mathcal{V}).

The involution
c
coincides, under the isomorphisms

H^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(M;\mathcal{V})\cong H^{n}(M,\mathbb{V})\cong H^{n}(M,\mathbb{V}_{\mathbb{R}})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}

with the complex conjugation induced by the real structure given by the ‘Betti cohomology’
H^{n}(M,\mathbb{V}_{\mathbb{R}})
; this justifies the terminology ‘Betti-conjugate’.

Proposition B.7
.

Let
n\geq 1
be the (complex) dimension of
M
. If the natural map
\Gamma(M,\mathcal{W}\otimes\Omega^{n}_{M})\to H^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(M;\mathcal{V})
is surjective, then every section
\omega
of
\mathcal{W}\otimes\Omega^{n}_{M}
admits a harmonic lift.

Proof.

Note that
\Gamma(M,\mathcal{W}\otimes\Omega^{n}_{M})\to H^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(M;\mathcal{V})
,
\omega\mapsto[\omega]
, is well-defined since
\Omega^{p}_{M}=0
for
p\geq n+1
. By the surjectivity hypothesis, given
\omega\in\Gamma(M,\mathcal{W}\otimes\Omega^{n}_{M})
there is
\eta\in\Gamma(M,\mathcal{W}\otimes\Omega^{n}_{M})
such that

c[\omega]=[\eta]

in
H^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(M;\mathcal{V})
. Since
c
is an involution, this is equivalent to
[\omega]=c[\eta]
, which also amounts to
[\omega-c\eta]=0
. By Lemma
B.3
, there exists
\xi
in
\mathcal{A}^{n-1}_{M}(\mathcal{V})
satisfying equation (
B.3
).
∎

Remark B.8
.

In the algebraic setting of Remark
B.2
, we can also ask the sub-bundle
\mathcal{W}
of
\mathcal{V}
to be the analytification of an algebraic sub-bundle
\mathcal{W}^{\mathrm{alg}}
of
\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{alg}}
. It follows from the same proof above that, if the natural map

\Gamma(X,\mathcal{W}^{\mathrm{alg}}\otimes\Omega^{n}_{X/\mathbb{C}})\longrightarrow H^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X;\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{alg}})\cong H^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(M;\mathcal{V})

is surjective, then any
\omega\in\Gamma(X,\mathcal{W}^{\mathrm{alg}}\otimes\Omega^{n}_{X/\mathbb{C}})
admits a harmonic lift
\xi
relative to
\mathcal{W}^{\mathrm{alg}}
and
\mathbb{V}_{\mathbb{R}}
, meaning that
\eta
in equation (
B.3
) can also be assumed to lie in
\Gamma(X,\mathcal{W}^{\mathrm{alg}}\otimes\Omega^{n}_{X/\mathbb{C}})
.

B.3.
Harmonic lifts and complex conjugation in relative cohomology

We keep the above notation, and consider a closed embedded complex submanifold
i:N\hookrightarrow M
. The
n
th relative analytic de Rham cohomology with coefficients in
\mathcal{V}
is defined as in §
A.1
:

H^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(M,N;\mathcal{V})\coloneqq\mathbb{H}^{n}(M,\Omega^{\bullet}_{(M,N)}(\mathcal{V})),

where
\Omega^{\bullet}_{(M,N)}(\mathcal{V})
is the mapping cone of
i^{*}:\Omega^{\bullet}_{M}(\mathcal{V})\to i_{*}\Omega^{\bullet}_{N}(i^{*}\mathcal{V})
. As in the algebraic case, there is a long exact sequence of complex vector spaces

(B.5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{n-1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(N;i^{*}\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\mathrm{dR}}^{n}(M,N;\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(M;\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(N;i^{*}\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}

induced by the short exact sequence of complexes

\hbox to291.26pt{\vbox to18.38pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 145.62843pt\lower-9.1875pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Lemma B.9
.

If
M=X^{\mathrm{an}}
and
(\mathcal{V},\nabla)
is the analytification of
(\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{alg}},\nabla^{\mathrm{alg}})
over
X
, with hypotheses as in Remark
B.2
, and moreover the submanifold
N\hookrightarrow M
is the analytification of a smooth closed subvariety
Y\hookrightarrow X
, then there is a canonical isomorphism

H^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X,Y;\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{alg}})\cong H^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(M,N;\mathcal{V}).

Proof.

This follows from Deligne’s theorem in the absolute case, as explained in Remark
B.2
, together with an application of the five lemma to the long exact sequences (
A.2
) and (
B.5
).
∎

If
M
is Stein as in Example
B.1
, then the sheaves
\Omega^{p}_{(M,N)}(\mathcal{V})
are acyclic, and the relative analytic de Rham cohomology is computed by global sections:
H^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(M,N;\mathcal{V})=H^{n}(\Gamma(M,\Omega^{\bullet}_{(M,N)}(\mathcal{V})))
.

More generally, without any hypothesis on
M
, the relative analytic de Rham cohomology can be computed by global
C^{\infty}
sections. Let
\mathcal{A}^{\bullet}_{(M,N)}(\mathcal{V})
denote the cone of
i^{*}:\mathcal{A}^{\bullet}_{M}(\mathcal{V})\to i_{*}\mathcal{A}^{\bullet}_{N}(i^{*}\mathcal{V})
; we may also write

\mathcal{A}^{\bullet}_{(M,N)}(\mathcal{V})\cong\mathbb{V}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{A}_{(M,N)}^{\bullet},

where
\mathbb{V}
is the local system of horizontal sections of
(\mathcal{V},\nabla)
, as before. Then, there is a canonical isomorphism

H^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(M,N;\mathcal{V})\cong H^{n}(\Gamma(M,\mathcal{A}^{\bullet}_{(M,N)}(\mathcal{V})).

The proof is similar to that of Lemma
B.3
.

Now, assume that
(\mathcal{V},\nabla)
is equipped with the additional structure (i) and (ii) of §
B.2
. The complex conjugation
c:\mathcal{A}_{M}^{0}(\mathcal{V})\to\mathcal{A}_{M}^{0}(\mathcal{V})
induces, for all
n
, a
\mathbb{C}
-antilinear involution on relative cohomology

c:H^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(M,N;\mathcal{V})\longrightarrow H^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(M,N;\mathcal{V})

which is compatible with the involution on absolute cohomology (
B.4
) by the long exact sequence (
B.5
). The following lemma implies that the map
c
may be computed in top degree by a harmonic lift.

Lemma B.10
.

Suppose that
M
has dimension
n
and let
\omega,\eta\in\Gamma(M,\mathcal{W}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{M}}\Omega^{n}_{M})
. They define classes
[(\omega,0)],[(\eta,0)]\in H^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(M,N;\mathcal{V})
. Let
\xi\in\Gamma(M,\mathcal{A}^{n-1}_{M}(\mathcal{V}))
be a harmonic lift of
(\omega,\eta)
. Then we have

(B.6)

c[(\eta,0)]=[(\omega,0)]+[(0,i^{*}\xi)],

where
[(0,i^{*}\xi)]
is the image of the class
[i^{*}\xi]\in H^{n-1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(N;i^{*}\mathcal{V})
under the boundary map

H^{n-1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(N;i^{*}\mathcal{V})\longrightarrow H^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(M,N;\mathcal{V}).

Equivalently, one may write
c[(\omega,0)]=[(\eta,0)]-[(0,i^{*}c\,\xi)]
.

Proof.

Since
\omega,\eta
are holomorphic and of top degree, they are closed and hence the pairs
(\omega,0)
and
(\eta,0)
are closed in the mapping cone
\Omega^{\bullet}_{(M,N)}(\mathcal{V})=C(i^{*})
. By Definition
B.5
,
\nabla^{n-1}\xi=\omega-c\eta
. We deduce that the following relation

d(\xi,0)=(\nabla^{n-1}\xi,\xi|_{i(N)})=(\omega-c\eta,0)+(0,\xi|_{i(N)})

holds in
\Omega^{\bullet}_{(M,N)}(\mathcal{V})
. Therefore, in relative analytic de Rham cohomology, we obtain

c[(\eta,0)]=[(\omega,0)]+[(0,i^{*}\xi)].

Note that
i^{*}\xi
is closed, since
\xi
is harmonic and
\dim N<n
. The final equation follows from
c^{2}=\mathrm{id}.

∎

Remark B.11
.

There is also an algebraic version of the above result. In the situation of Lemma
B.9
and Remark
B.8
, we may take
\omega,\eta\in\Gamma(X,\mathcal{W}^{\mathrm{alg}}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\Omega^{n}_{X/\mathbb{C}})
to be algebraic Betti-conjugate sections. Then, the equation (
B.6
) holds in algebraic de Rham cohomology
H^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X,Y;\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{alg}})
. Note that the harmonic lift
\xi
is only smooth, and
not
algebraic, but
[i^{*}\xi]\in H^{n-1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(Y;i^{*}\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{alg}})
does indeed define a class in algebraic de Rham cohomology via Lemma
B.3
.

Appendix C
Examples of matrix-valued higher Green’s functions

We provide examples of our
(k+1)\times(k+1)
matrix-valued Green’s functions for
k=1,2
.
Their existence and properties follow from the results of §
4.1
. When
k=2m
is even, the central element of this matrix is proportional to the ‘classical’ higher Green’s function (
1.2
), and all other entries are deduced from it by applying raising and lowering operators. However, when
k
is odd, corresponding to modular forms of odd weight, these matrices seem to be new. We give here the simplest non-trivial example in each case.

C.1.
The
2\times 2
case

First, recall that for
z,w
in the upper or lower half planes,

C=\frac{(z-w)(\overline{z}-\overline{w})}{(z-\overline{z})(w-\overline{w})}\ \qquad\hbox{ and }\qquad\psi^{p,q}(z,w)=\frac{(w-\overline{w})}{(z-w)^{p+1}(z-\overline{w})^{q+1}}\ .

Define the following function

f_{2}(z,w)=\frac{1}{(w-\overline{w})(z-\overline{z})}\log\left(\frac{C}{C-1}\right)=\frac{1}{(w-\overline{w})(z-\overline{z})}\log\frac{(z-w)(\overline{z}-\overline{w})}{(z-\overline{w})(\overline{z}-w)}\ .

It takes values in
\mathbb{R}
, which is clear from writing it as a single-valued logarithm:

f_{2}(z,w)=\frac{-1}{4\,\mathrm{Im}(z)\mathrm{Im}(w)}\log\left|\frac{z-w}{z-\overline{w}}\right|^{2}.

Consider the
2\times 2
matrix whose entries are

\mathsf{g}_{1}(z,w)=\begin{pmatrix}g^{1,0}_{0,1}&g^{0,1}_{0,1}\\
g^{1,0}_{1,0}&g^{0,1}_{1,0}\end{pmatrix}

where

g^{0,1}_{0,1}=\frac{1}{\overline{w}-\overline{z}}+(z-w)\,f_{2}(z,w)\ ,\quad g^{0,1}_{1,0}=\frac{1}{z-\overline{w}}+(w-\overline{z})\,f_{2}(z,w)

g^{1,0}_{0,1}=\frac{1}{\overline{z}-w}+(\overline{w}-z)\,f_{2}(z,w)\ ,\quad g^{1,0}_{1,0}=\frac{1}{w-z}+(\overline{z}-\overline{w})\,f_{2}(z,w)\ .

The formula for
g^{1,0}_{1,0}(z,w)
is given by
(
4.6
) and (
4.7
), since
\mathcal{L}_{1}(x)=-1-\log(1-x)/x,
where
x=C^{-1}.

Then we may check that all the entries of the matrix are compatible with raising and lowering operators:

\partial_{r}g^{p,q}_{r,s}=g^{p,q}_{r+1,s-1}\ ,\quad\overline{\partial}_{s}g^{p,q}_{r,s}=g^{p,q}_{r-1,s+1}

\partial^{p}g^{p,q}_{r,s}=g^{p+1,q-1}_{r,s}\ ,\quad\overline{\partial}^{q}g^{p,q}_{r,s}=g^{p-1,q+1}_{r,s}

where all indices lie in the range of the matrix. Furthermore we verify that

\partial_{1}g^{0,1}_{1,0}=\frac{(z-\overline{z})(w-\overline{w})}{(z-w)(z-\overline{w})^{2}}\ ,\quad\partial_{1}g^{1,0}_{1,0}=\frac{(z-\overline{z})(w-\overline{w})}{(z-w)^{2}(z-\overline{w})}

hold, as do similar equations for
\partial^{1}
and their complex conjugates. The situation is completely summarised by the following diagram:

\begin{array}[]{c|cc|c}\frac{(z-\overline{z})(w-\overline{w})}{(\overline{z}-w)^{3}}&\frac{(z-\overline{z})(w-\overline{w})}{(\overline{z}-w)^{2}(\overline{z}-\overline{w})}&\frac{(z-\overline{z})(w-\overline{w})}{(\overline{z}-w)(\overline{z}-\overline{w})^{2}}&\frac{(z-\overline{z})(w-\overline{w})}{(\overline{z}-\overline{w})^{3}}\\
\hline\cr\frac{(z-\overline{z})(w-\overline{w})}{(z-w)(\overline{z}-w)^{2}}&g^{1,0}_{0,1}&g^{0,1}_{0,1}&\frac{(z-\overline{z})(w-\overline{w})}{(z-\overline{w})(\overline{z}-\overline{w})^{2}}\\
\frac{(z-\overline{z})(w-\overline{w})}{(z-w)^{2}(\overline{z}-w)}&g^{1,0}_{1,0}&g^{0,1}_{1,0}&\frac{(z-\overline{z})(w-\overline{w})}{(z-\overline{w})^{2}(\overline{z}-\overline{w})}\\
\hline\cr\frac{(z-\overline{z})(w-\overline{w})}{(z-w)^{3}}&\frac{(z-\overline{z})(w-\overline{w})}{(z-w)^{2}(z-\overline{w})}&\frac{(z-\overline{z})(w-\overline{w})}{(z-w)(z-\overline{w})^{2}}&\frac{(z-\overline{z})(w-\overline{w})}{(z-\overline{w})^{3}}\end{array}

For example, the entries in all four rows satisfy the following relations with respect to raising and lowering operators in the variable
w

(\overline{w}-w)\psi^{1,0}(w,z)\ \overset{\partial^{1}}{\longleftarrow}\ \ g^{1,0}_{1,0}\overset{\partial^{0},\overline{\partial}^{0}}{\longleftrightarrow}\ g^{0,1}_{1,0}\ \overset{\overline{\partial}^{1}}{\longrightarrow}\ (\overline{w}-w)\psi^{0,1}(\overline{w},z)

Every column satisfies a similar relation with respect to
z
.

Let
\Gamma\leq\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
be a finite index subgroup. Then the higher Green’s matrix is defined by averaging over the action of
\Gamma
as follows:

\mathcal{G}_{\Gamma,1}(z,w)=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\begin{pmatrix}j_{\gamma}^{-1}(\overline{z})&0\\
0&j_{\gamma}(z)^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\mathsf{g}_{1}(\gamma z,w)\ .

C.2.
The
3\times 3
case

Consider the
3\times 3
array of functions in two variables starting with the following function, which is to be placed in the middle:

g^{1,1}_{1,1}(z,w)=\frac{4\,Q_{1}(1-2\,C(z,w))}{(z-\overline{z})(w-\overline{w})}\ ,\

where
Q_{1}(t)=\frac{t}{2}\log(\frac{1+t}{t-1})-1
is the Legendre function. We define:

\mathsf{g}_{2}(z,w)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c|c}g^{2,0}_{0,2}&g^{1,1}_{0,2}&g^{0,2}_{0,2}\\
\hline\cr g^{2,0}_{1,1}&g^{1,1}_{1,1}&g^{0,2}_{1,1}\\
\hline\cr g^{2,0}_{2,0}&g^{1,1}_{2,0}&g^{0,2}_{2,0}\\
\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c|c}\frac{1}{4}\partial^{1}\overline{\partial}_{1}g^{1,1}_{1,1}&\frac{1}{2}\overline{\partial}_{1}g^{1,1}_{1,1}&\frac{1}{4}\overline{\partial}^{1}\overline{\partial}_{1}g^{1,1}_{1,1}\\
\hline\cr\frac{1}{2}\partial^{1}g^{1,1}_{1,1}&g^{1,1}_{1,1}&\frac{1}{2}\overline{\partial}^{1}g^{1,1}_{1,1}\\
\hline\cr\frac{1}{4}\partial^{1}\partial_{1}g^{1,1}_{1,1}&\frac{1}{2}\partial_{1}g^{1,1}_{1,1}&\frac{1}{4}\overline{\partial}^{1}\partial_{1}g^{1,1}_{1,1}\\
\end{array}\right)

which, if one wishes, can be suitably interpreted in matrix form:

\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2}\overline{\partial}_{1}&&\\
&1&\\
&&\frac{1}{2}\partial_{1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}g^{1,1}_{1,1}&g^{1,1}_{1,1}&g^{1,1}_{1,1}\\
g^{1,1}_{1,1}&g^{1,1}_{1,1}&g^{1,1}_{1,1}\\
g^{1,1}_{1,1}&g^{1,1}_{1,1}&g^{1,1}_{1,1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2}\partial^{1}&&\\
&1&\\
&&\frac{1}{2}\overline{\partial}^{1}\end{pmatrix}

The vertical and horizontal lines are merely there for the sake of legibility. Nevertheless, crossing such a line, in either direction, corresponds to the action of an operator
\partial_{\bullet},\overline{\partial}_{\bullet}
to move up and down (with an appropriate scaling factor), and to the action of the operators
\partial^{\bullet},\overline{\partial}^{\bullet}
to move left and right.
For example:

\partial_{r}g^{p,q}_{r,s}=(r+1)g^{p,q}_{r+1,s}\ ,\quad\overline{\partial}_{s}g^{p,q}_{r,s}=(s+1)g^{p,q}_{r-1,s+1}

for all indices in the range above, and similarly for
\partial^{p}
,
\overline{\partial}^{q}
.

The bottom-left entry, for example, can be written more simply

g^{2,0}_{2,0}(z,w)=\left(\frac{\overline{z}-\overline{w}}{(z-\overline{z})(w-\overline{w})}\right)^{2}\log\left|\frac{z-w}{z-\overline{w}}\right|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{2C(z,w)+1}{(z-w)^{2}}\ ,

which is equivalent to (
4.6
), since
\mathcal{L}_{2}(x)=-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{x}-\log(1-x)/x^{2}
in (
4.7
).

If we apply the raising and lowering operators in
z,w
again to take us one further step outside the range of this array, we obtain only rational functions of
z,w
and their complex conjugates, as shown:

\begin{array}[]{c|ccc|c}\frac{(z-\overline{z})(w-\overline{w})}{(\overline{z}-w)^{4}}&\frac{(z-\overline{z})(w-\overline{w})}{(\overline{z}-w)^{3}(\overline{z}-\overline{w})}&\frac{(z-\overline{z})(w-\overline{w})}{(\overline{z}-w)^{2}(\overline{z}-\overline{w})^{2}}&\frac{(z-\overline{z})(w-\overline{w})}{(\overline{z}-w)(\overline{z}-\overline{w})^{3}}&\frac{(z-\overline{z})(w-\overline{w})}{(\overline{z}-\overline{w})^{4}}\\
\hline\cr\frac{(z-\overline{z})(w-\overline{w})}{(z-w)(\overline{z}-w)^{3}}&g^{2,0}_{0,2}&g^{1,1}_{0,2}&g^{0,2}_{0,2}&\frac{(z-\overline{z})(w-\overline{w})}{(z-\overline{w})(\overline{z}-\overline{w})^{3}}\\
\frac{(z-\overline{z})(w-\overline{w})}{(z-w)^{2}(\overline{z}-w)^{2}}&g^{2,0}_{1,1}&g^{1,1}_{1,1}&g^{0,2}_{1,1}&\frac{(z-\overline{z})(w-\overline{w})}{(z-\overline{w})^{2}(\overline{z}-\overline{w})^{2}}\\
\frac{(z-\overline{z})(w-\overline{w})}{(z-w)^{3}(\overline{z}-w)}&g^{2,0}_{2,0}&g^{1,1}_{2,0}&g^{0,2}_{2,0}&\frac{(z-\overline{z})(w-\overline{w})}{(z-\overline{w})^{3}(\overline{z}-\overline{w})}\\
\hline\cr\frac{(z-\overline{z})(w-\overline{w})}{(z-w)^{4}}&\frac{(z-\overline{z})(w-\overline{w})}{(z-w)^{3}(z-\overline{w})}&\frac{(z-\overline{z})(w-\overline{w})}{(z-w)^{2}(z-\overline{w})^{2}}&\frac{(z-\overline{z})(w-\overline{w})}{(z-w)(z-\overline{w})^{3}}&\frac{(z-\overline{z})(w-\overline{w})}{(z-\overline{w})^{4}}\end{array}

For example, we check that

\partial_{2}g^{p,q}_{2,0}(z,w)=\frac{(z-\overline{z})(w-\overline{w})}{(z-w)^{p+1}(z-\overline{w})^{q+1}}=(z-\overline{z})\psi^{p,q}(z,w)

for all
p+q=2
,
p,q\geq 0.

Let
\Gamma\leq\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
be a finite index subgroup. Then the higher Green’s function matrix is defined by averaging over the action of
\Gamma
as follows:

\mathcal{G}_{\Gamma,2}(z,w)=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\begin{pmatrix}j_{\gamma}^{-2}(\overline{z})&0&0\\
0&j_{\gamma}(z)^{-1}j_{\gamma}(\overline{z})^{-1}\\
0&0&j_{\gamma}(z)^{-2}\end{pmatrix}\mathsf{g}_{2}(\gamma z,w)\ .

Appendix D
Geometry of
\mathcal{M}_{1,3}

Let
\mathcal{M}^{\node}_{1,n}
denote the moduli stack of geometrically integral genus one curves with at most nodal singularities, and
n
distinct marked points. It is the union of
\mathcal{M}_{1,n}
with the configuration space of
n
distinct ordered points on the nodal cubic. We shall give explicit stratifications of
\mathcal{M}^{\node}_{1,n}
for
n\leq 3
.

Throughout this section, we work over the base ring
R=\mathbb{Z}[1/6]
.

D.1.
The moduli stack
\mathcal{M}_{1,1}
of elliptic curves

Recall that the moduli stack of geometrically integral projective curves of arithmetic genus one and one marked smooth point
(C,p)
is isomorphic to the quotient stack
[\mathbb{A}^{2}/\mathbb{G}_{m}]
, where
\mathbb{A}^{2}=\Spec R[g_{2},g_{3}]
is equipped with the
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action for which
g_{2}
and
g_{3}
have weights
4
and
6
, respectively. Explicitly, to a triple
(C,p,v)
, where
v
is a non-zero tangent vector at
p
, there corresponds a unique Weierstrass equation

(D.1)

y^{2}=4x^{3}-g_{2}x-g_{3}

where
p
is identified with the point at infinity
(0:1:0)
, and
v
with the tangent vector dual to
dx/y
. The action of
\mathbb{G}_{m}=\Spec R[t,t^{-1}]
comes from the different choices of
v
: the triple
(C,p,tv)
corresponds to the Weierstrass coefficients
(t^{4}g_{2},t^{6}g_{3})
via the change of variables
(x,y)\mapsto(t^{2}x,t^{3}y)
.

Let
\Delta=g_{2}^{3}-27g_{3}^{2}
. The projective curve defined by (
D.1
) is smooth if and only if
\Delta\neq 0
. It has nodal singularities if
\Delta=0
and
(g_{2},g_{3})\neq(0,0)
, and it has cuspidal singularities if
(g_{2},g_{3})=(0,0)
. Then,

(D.2)

\mathcal{M}^{\node}_{1,1}=\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,1}\cong\left[\frac{\mathbb{A}^{2}\setminus(0,0)}{\mathbb{G}_{m}}\right]=\mathcal{P}(4,6)

and

\mathcal{M}_{1,1}\cong\left[\frac{\mathbb{A}^{2}\setminus V(\Delta)}{\mathbb{G}_{m}}\right]\cong\mathcal{M}^{\node}_{1,1}\setminus\infty,

where

(D.3)

\infty\cong\left[\frac{V(\Delta)\setminus(0,0)}{\mathbb{G}_{m}}\right]\cong\left[\frac{\Spec R}{\mu_{2}}\right]

is the cusp. Here, and in what follows, we denote by
\mu_{n}=\Spec R[t]/(t^{n}-1)
the affine subgroup scheme of
\mathbb{G}_{m}
given by
n
th roots of unity. The isomorphism (
D.3
) may be obtained by the following auxiliary result, which will be used multiple times in the following paragraphs.

Lemma D.1

(Slicing lemma)
.

Consider
\mathbb{A}^{n}=\Spec R[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
with the
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action for which
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
have weights
r_{1},\ldots,r_{n}\geq 1
, and let
X\subset\mathbb{A}^{n}
be a
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-invariant
R
-subscheme. Let
f\in R[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]\setminus\{0\}
be a homogeneous polynomial of degree
r
with respect to the
(r_{1},\ldots,r_{n})
-grading, and assume that
X\subset\mathbb{A}^{n}\setminus V(f)
. Then the natural map

\left[\frac{X\cap V(f-1)}{\mu_{r}}\right]\to\left[\frac{X}{\mathbb{G}_{m}}\right]

is an isomorphism of stacks.

Proof.

An explicit inverse for the map in the statement is induced by the morphism
X\to[X\cap V(f-1)/\mu_{r}]
corresponding to the
\mu_{r}
-torsor
P\to X
defined by the equation
f(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=t^{r}
in
X\times\mathbb{A}^{1}
. It is equipped with the
\mu_{r}
-equivariant map
P\to X\cap V(f-1)
defined on points by
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r},t)\mapsto t^{-1}\cdot(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r})
.
∎

D.2.
The universal punctured elliptic curve
\mathcal{M}_{1,2}

It follows from the discussion at the beginning of §
D.1
that a quadruple
(C,p_{1},p_{2},v)
, where
C
is a (geometrically integral, projective) non-cuspidal genus 1 curve,
p_{1},p_{2}
are distinct marked points, and
v
is a tangent vector at
p_{1}
, corresponds to a unique
(x,y,g_{2},g_{3})
such that (
D.1
) holds and
(g_{2},g_{3})\neq(0,0)
. Since (
D.1
) may be solved for
g_{3}
, we can identify:

(D.4)

\mathcal{M}^{\node}_{1,2}\cong\left[\frac{\mathbb{A}^{3}\setminus V(g_{2},y^{2}-4x^{3})}{\mathbb{G}_{m}}\right]

where
\mathbb{A}^{3}=\Spec R[x,y,g_{2}]
is equipped with the
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action for which
x
,
y
, and
g_{2}
have weights
2
,
3
, and
4
, respectively.

Lemma D.2
.

The stack
\mathcal{M}^{\node}_{1,2}
admits a stratification

\mathcal{M}^{\node}_{1,2}\cong[\mathbb{A}^{2}/\mu_{4}]\cup[Z/\mu_{6}]

where
\mathbb{A}^{2}=\Spec R[x,y]\cong V(g_{2}-1)
, and
Z=V(g_{2},y^{2}-4x^{3}-1)
.
It may also be identified with

(D.5)

\mathcal{M}^{\node}_{1,2}\cong\mathcal{P}(2,3,4)\setminus\Spec R,

the copy of
\Spec R
in
\mathcal{P}(2,3,4)
being given by the quotient
[V(g_{2},y^{2}-4x^{3})/\mathbb{G}_{m}]
.

Proof.

The scheme
\mathbb{A}^{3}\setminus V(g_{2},y^{2}-4x^{3})
is stratified by the open subscheme
\mathbb{A}^{3}\setminus V(g_{2})
and its complement
V(g_{2})\setminus V(g_{2},y^{2}-4x^{3})
. Since these subschemes are
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-invariant, we obtain from (
D.4
):

\mathcal{M}^{\node}_{1,2}\cong\left[\frac{\Spec R[x,y,g_{2},g_{2}^{-1}]}{\mathbb{G}_{m}}\right]\cup\left[\frac{\Spec R[x,y,(y^{2}-4x^{3})^{-1}]}{\mathbb{G}_{m}}\right].

Since
g_{2}
is homogeneous of degree 4, it follows from Lemma
D.1
that

\left[\frac{\Spec R[x,y,g_{2},g_{2}^{-1}]}{\mathbb{G}_{m}}\right]\cong\left[\frac{\Spec R[x,y]}{\mu_{4}}\right].

Likewise, as
y^{2}-4x^{3}
is homogeneous of degree 6, Lemma
D.1
yields

\left[\frac{\Spec R[x,y,(y^{2}-4x^{3})^{-1}]}{\mathbb{G}_{m}}\right]\cong\left[\frac{V(y^{2}-4x^{3}-1)}{\mu_{6}}\right].

For the second part, we stratify differently and write

\mathcal{M}^{\node}_{1,2}=\left[\frac{\mathbb{A}^{3}\setminus(0,0,0)}{\mathbb{G}_{m}}\right]\setminus\left[\frac{V(g_{2},y^{2}-4x^{3})\setminus(0,0,0)}{\mathbb{G}_{m}}\right].

The stratum on the left is
\mathcal{P}(2,3,4)
. To compute the one on the right, normalise the plane affine cuspidal cubic
y^{2}=4x^{3}
by
\mathbb{A}^{1}=\Spec R[z]
via
z\mapsto(z^{2},2z^{3})
. The normalisation is
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-equivariant (where
z
is taken with weight one) and induces an isomorphism outside the origin, thus:

\left[\frac{V(g_{2},y^{2}-4x^{3})\setminus(0,0,0)}{\mathbb{G}_{m}}\right]\cong\left[\frac{\Spec R[z,z^{-1}]}{\mathbb{G}_{m}}\right]=\Spec R.\qed

Remark D.3
.

If
Z
is as in the above statement, one checks that

[Z/\mu_{6}]=\mathcal{P}(2,3)\setminus\Spec R

where
\Spec R
corresponds to the
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-orbit given by the punctured cuspidal cubic, as in the above proof. Note that, although the motive of
Z
is not mixed Tate (since
Z
is a punctured elliptic curve), its invariant submotive for the action of
\mu_{6}
, is.

Remark D.4
.

We refer to
[
Inc22
]
for a description of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,2}
as a weighted blow-up of
\mathcal{P}(2,3,4).

Corollary D.5
.

Under the isomorphism (
D.4
), we have

\mathcal{M}_{1,2}^{\node}\setminus[V(y)/\mathbb{G}_{m}]\cong(\mathcal{P}(2,3,4)\setminus\mathrm{Spec}(R))\setminus\mathcal{P}(2,4).

Proof.

By the discussion preceding Lemma
D.2
, we can identify

\mathcal{M}^{\node}_{1,2}\cap\left[\frac{V(y)}{\mathbb{G}_{m}}\right]\cong\left[\frac{\Spec R[x,g_{2}]\setminus V(x,g_{2})}{\mathbb{G}_{m}}\right]\cong\mathcal{P}(2,4).\qed

D.3.
Three marked points
\mathcal{M}_{1,3}

The data of three distinct marked points on a non-cuspidal curve of genus one is encoded by two distinct pairs of points
(x_{1},y_{1})
,
(x_{2},y_{2})
, and
(g_{2},g_{3})\neq(0,0)
, subject to the equations (
D.1
).

Lemma D.6
.

Let
\mathcal{U}_{3}\subset\mathcal{M}^{\node}_{1,3}
denote the open substack where
x_{1}\neq x_{2}
, and let
\mathcal{Z}_{3}
denote its complement, so that
\mathcal{M}_{1,3}^{\node}=\mathcal{U}_{3}\cup\mathcal{Z}_{3}
. Then

\mathcal{Z}_{3}\cong\mathcal{P}(2,3,4)\setminus(\mathrm{Spec}(R)\cup\mathcal{P}(2,4)),\quad\mathcal{U}_{3}\cong\left[\mathbb{A}^{3}/\mu_{2}\right]\setminus W,

where
W
is an
R
-scheme with a stratification of the form

W\cong(\mathbb{A}^{1}\setminus\{-1,0,1\})\cup(\Spec R\cup\Spec R).

The space
W
is the moduli space of two points with distinct
x
coordinate on a cuspidal cubic; the points
\Spec R
correspond to the locus where one or other of these point is the cusp.

Proof.

The equations (
D.1
) for
(x_{i},y_{i})
can be written in matrix form:

\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}&1\\
x_{2}&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}g_{2}\\
g_{3}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}-y_{1}^{2}+4x_{1}^{3}\\
-y_{2}^{2}+4x_{2}^{3}\end{pmatrix}.

When
x_{1}\neq x_{2}
, they may be uniquely solved for
(g_{2},g_{3})
, and hence

\mathcal{U}_{3}=\left[\frac{\Spec R[x_{1},y_{1},x_{2},y_{2},(x_{1}-x_{2})^{-1}]\setminus V(y^{2}_{1}-4x^{3}_{1},y^{2}_{2}-4x^{3}_{2})}{\mathbb{G}_{m}}\right].

Since
x_{1},x_{2}
have weight
2
for the action of
\mathbb{G}_{m}
, slicing by
x_{1}-x_{2}=1
(Lemma
D.1
) yields

\left[\frac{\Spec R[x_{1},y_{1},x_{2},y_{2},(x_{1}-x_{2})^{-1}]}{\mathbb{G}_{m}}\right]\cong\left[\frac{\Spec R[x_{1},y_{1},y_{2}]}{\mu_{2}}\right]\cong\left[\frac{\mathbb{A}^{3}}{\mu_{2}}\right].

By normalising the cuspidal cubics
y_{i}^{2}=4x_{i}^{3}
and deleting
V(x_{1}x_{2})
, we have furthermore

\left[\frac{V(y^{2}_{1}-4x^{3}_{1},y^{2}_{2}-4x^{3}_{2})\setminus V(x_{1}x_{2}(x_{1}-x_{2}))}{\mathbb{G}_{m}}\right]\cong\left[\frac{\Spec R[t_{1},t_{2},t_{1}^{-1},t_{2}^{-1}]\setminus V(t_{1}^{2}-t_{2}^{2})}{\mathbb{G}_{m}}\right]\cong\mathbb{A}^{1}\setminus\{1,0,-1\},

where the last isomorphism is obtained by slicing the quotient by
t_{2}=1
(Lemma
D.1
). The stratum where
x_{1}=0
(and similarly
x_{2}=0
) is isomorphic to a point (again by slicing):

\left[\frac{V(y^{2}_{2}-4x^{3}_{2})\setminus V(x_{2})}{\mathbb{G}_{m}}\right]\cong\Spec R.

The locus
\mathcal{Z}_{3}
is defined by
x_{1}=x_{2}
. Then, equation (
D.1
) implies that
y_{1}^{2}=y_{2}^{2}
, and hence the condition
(x_{1},y_{1})\neq(x_{2},y_{2})
amounts to
y_{2}=-y_{1}
. In particular,
y_{1},y_{2}\neq 0
. The data of the pair of points
(x_{1},y_{1})
,
(x_{2},y_{2})
is uniquely determined by
(x_{1},y_{1})
, and hence

\mathcal{Z}_{3}\cong\mathcal{M}^{\node}_{1,2}\setminus\left[\frac{V(y)}{\mathbb{G}_{m}}\right]\cong\mathcal{P}(2,3,4)\setminus(\mathrm{Spec}(R)\cup\mathcal{P}(2,4)),

where the last isomorphism is given by Corollary
D.5
.
∎

D.4.
Mixed Tate motives in genus
1

To show that the motive of
\mathcal{M}_{1,n}
over
\mathbb{Q}
is mixed Tate, it suffices to find a stratification whose strata are mixed Tate.

Proposition D.7
.

The motives
M(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,n})
and
M(\mathcal{M}_{1,n})
are mixed Tate for
n\leq 3
.

Proof.

The compactification
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,n}
is stratified by finite quotients of products of the moduli stacks
\mathcal{M}_{0,r}
and
\mathcal{M}_{1,s}
, for
3\leq r\leq n+2
and
1\leq s\leq n
. Since the motives
M(\mathcal{M}_{0,r})
are mixed Tate (
\mathcal{M}_{0,r}
is a complement of a hyperplane arrangement in a projective space), it follows that
M(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,s})
is mixed Tate for all
1\leq s\leq n
if and only if the same is true for
M(\mathcal{M}_{1,s})
for all
1\leq s\leq n
. Since
\mathcal{M}_{1,s}^{\node}
is the complement in
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,s}
of a finite number of strata of the above form, this is in turn equivalent to
M(\mathcal{M}_{1,s}^{\node})
being mixed Tate for all
1\leq s\leq n
. But this holds for
n=3
by (
D.2
) and the stratifications given in Lemmas
D.2
and
D.6
.
∎

One expects that
M(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,n})
and
M(\mathcal{M}_{1,n})
are mixed Tate for all
n\leq 10
. They are not for
n\geq 11
.
